# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 343

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Sefton, M., Shupp, R., & Walker, J. M. (2007). The effect of rewards and sanctions in provision of public goods. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with four players and ten rounds per sequence, the introduction of costly sanctioning (punishment) opportunities after an initial baseline phase leads to sustained higher contributions and, after an initial efficiency loss due to sanctioning costs, to higher group efficiency compared to the baseline. Reward opportunities alone do not maintain high contributions or efficiency, as the use of rewards declines quickly. The combination of sanctions and rewards produces the highest average contributions and earnings, but the effect is primarily due to the sanctioning mechanism. The cost structure is linear: each token used for punishment costs the punisher 10 cents and reduces the target's earnings by 10 cents (total group loss: 20 cents per token). The marginal per-capita return (MPCR) is 0.5. Players cannot identify who punished or rewarded them, and all information is anonymous. No communication is allowed. The number of rounds is known in advance. The findings are robust across groups, though there is heterogeneity in group-level outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a standard linear public goods game with four players, ten rounds, no communication, and known round structure. The results show that enabling costly punishment (with a 1:1 cost-impact ratio) increases efficiency over the baseline, but only after an initial period where the direct costs of punishment outweigh the gains from increased cooperation. Over time, as the threat of punishment sustains cooperation and the use of punishment declines, efficiency improves. The effect is robust to the absence of communication and anonymity of punishment. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (4 players, 10 rounds, MPCR=0.5, linear punishment, no chat, anonymous), enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control, especially in later rounds. The cost structure of punishment is critical: with a 1:1 cost-impact, the efficiency gain is positive but moderate; more effective punishment (higher impact per cost) would likely yield larger efficiency gains. The paper also shows that reward mechanisms alone are insufficient to sustain efficiency. These results should be directly used for prediction in similar lab PGG settings.

- source: Gürerk, Lauer, T., & Scheuermann, M. (2018). Leadership with individual rewards and punishments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, the addition of individual punishment or reward mechanisms (either centralized via a leader or decentralized among peers) leads to significantly higher contributions and higher net payoffs compared to the baseline game without such mechanisms. The marginal per capita return is 0.4. Punishment is more effective than reward at sustaining high contributions over time, especially in the peer punishment treatment, where contributions even increase in the second half of the game. Net payoffs are highest in reward treatments (due to the reward mechanism's structure), but also higher in punishment treatments than in the baseline. The presence of a leader (who contributes first and can reward or punish) does not further increase contributions or payoffs compared to peer-based reward or punishment. The effect of punishment on increasing contributions and payoffs is robust, and punishment from leaders has a stronger effect on subsequent contributions than peer punishment. Anti-social punishment is less frequent when punishment is centralized with a leader.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, enabling individual punishment (with a cost/impact ratio of 1:3) increases both contributions and net payoffs (i.e., efficiency) compared to the baseline without punishment. The effect is robust whether punishment is centralized (leader) or decentralized (peers), and is not further enhanced by adding a leader who leads by example. The control (no punishment) efficiency is lower than in the punishment-enabled treatments. The findings support the prediction that, holding game design dimensions constant, enabling punishment will increase average efficiency. The specific cost and impact structure (1 point cost reduces target by 3 points) is important for generalization. Reward mechanisms also increase efficiency, but the structure of the reward mechanism in this experiment is payoff-increasing by design. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar public goods game environments.

- source: Gürerk, Irlenbusch, B., & Rockenbach, B. (2009). Motivating teammates: The leader's choice between positive and negative incentives. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Leaders overwhelmingly prefer to use rewards initially, but some switch to punishment if free-riding is high. Punishment schemes (NEG) lead to higher contributions than reward schemes (POS), both for leaders and teammates. However, overall payoffs and efficiency are higher under rewards, because the reward mechanism increases total team surplus (each reward token increases team payoff, while each punishment token reduces it). Leaders benefit more from punishment, but teammates benefit more from rewards. The most efficient outcome (in terms of team payoff relative to the maximum possible) is achieved when leaders stick to rewards throughout. Switching from rewards to punishment increases contributions immediately and persistently, but does not increase efficiency. The effectiveness of incentive schemes is path-dependent and history matters. The study provides direct evidence on how the structure and cost/impact of incentives affect efficiency and payoffs in public goods games with centralized leader-administered incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game with centralized leader-administered incentives. It shows that while punishment increases contributions, it does not increase efficiency or total team payoff compared to rewards; in fact, efficiency is slightly higher with rewards. The marginal per-capita return, punishment/reward cost and effectiveness, and the structure of the incentive mechanism are all specified and can be mapped to prediction dimensions. The results suggest that enabling punishment may increase contributions but not efficiency, and that the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost/impact structure of the punishment mechanism. The findings are most applicable to settings with centralized, leader-chosen incentives, continuous contributions, and full information about contributions and payoffs.

- source: Page, T., Putterman, L., & Garcia, B. (2013). Voluntary contributions with redistribution: The effect of costly sanctions when one person's punishment is another's reward. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, the authors compare standard costly punishment (where fines are burned) to a redistributive punishment treatment (where fines are transferred to other group members as rewards). Both treatments use the same marginal per capita return (MPCR = 0.4), punishment cost (0.25 per unit), and group structure. The redistributive punishment treatment leads to significantly higher average contributions (9.03 vs. 7.09), higher average earnings (15.18 vs. 12.88), and higher efficiency, even after controlling for the resource-conserving effect of redistribution (earnings net of transfers: 14.20 vs. 12.88). The effect on efficiency is marginally statistically significant after netting out transfers. Redistributive punishment also reduces the share of punishment targeted at high contributors and increases the monetary return to contributing (from negative to positive). The study provides direct evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism can moderate the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players, 20 rounds, no communication, and fixed partners, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not always increase efficiency due to the costliness and mis-targeting of punishment. However, if the punishment mechanism is modified so that fines are redistributed as rewards to other group members (rather than burned), both contributions and efficiency increase, and the return to contributing becomes positive. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the structure of the punishment mechanism (specifically, whether punishment is redistributive or not) is a key moderator of the efficiency effect. The control (punishment-off) efficiency is not directly reported, but the paper references prior work showing that standard punishment does not increase efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, while redistributive punishment does. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the nature of the punishment and reward mechanisms.

- source: Reif, C., Rübbelke, D., & Löschel, A. (2017). Improving Voluntary Public Good Provision Through a Non-governmental, Endogenous Matching Mechanism: Experimental Evidence. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that subjects are willing to pay to establish a norm enforcement mechanism that punishes or rewards deviations from the group average contribution. With the mechanism, average group contributions to the public good are substantially higher than in the baseline, and average earnings are also higher. The mechanism is almost always implemented, and its strength (enforcement parameter) is positively associated with higher public good contributions. Over time, groups learn to reduce over-investment in the mechanism. The mechanism treatment results in more stable and higher efficiency (measured as group earnings relative to the maximum possible).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that enabling an endogenous, average-oriented punishment/reward mechanism in a repeated public good game with three players and ten rounds increases both group efficiency and average earnings compared to a control without such a mechanism. The design closely matches standard PGGs, and the efficiency improvement is robust. The findings support predicting a more positive effect of punishment/reward mechanisms on efficiency, especially when the mechanism is endogenously funded and targets deviations from the group average. The results are most directly applicable to small, fixed groups with repeated interaction and endogenous institution formation.

- source: Engl, F., Riedl, A., & Weber, R. (2021). Spillover Effects of Institutions on Cooperative Behavior, Preferences, and Beliefs. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Institutions that enforce cooperation in a public goods game (via a minimum contribution requirement, with full forfeiture for under-contribution) substantially increase group welfare in the regulated game and also increase welfare in an unregulated, otherwise identical game played by the same group. The direct effect on welfare is large, and the spillover effect is significant and economically meaningful (about 3-3.4 points on a 20-point endowment, or 30-40% of the direct effect). Both exogenous and endogenous institutions produce these effects, but the endogenous institution's spillover effect grows over time as the minimum requirement increases. The effect is mainly due to higher contributions among those already contributing, not by converting free riders. The positive effects on welfare and contributions persist after the institution is removed and extend to new partners. The mechanisms include increased beliefs about others' cooperativeness and stronger conditional cooperation preferences. No spillover is found to trust games. Heterogeneity exists: younger subjects and those with high positive reciprocity show smaller spillover effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of introducing a strong, central punishment institution (with full forfeiture for under-contribution) in a repeated linear public goods game with four players, 20 rounds, and MPCR 0.5. The control (no institution) game shows declining contributions and welfare over time, while the treatment (with punishment) achieves near-full cooperation and much higher welfare. Critically, the paper quantifies both the direct and spillover effects on group welfare, allowing for prediction of treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design. The results are robust across exogenous and endogenous institution types, though the time dynamics differ. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with similar group size, round structure, and punishment technology (centralized, all-or-nothing, no cost to punisher). The absence of spillover to trust games suggests limits to generalizability across domains. The evidence is strongest for games with continuous contribution choices, no chat, and full information about others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: JARUNGRATTANAPONG, R. (2022). PROMOTING COOPERATION IN A PUBLIC GOOD EXPERIMENT THROUGH OSTRACISM AS A PUNISHMENT MECHANISM. *Singapore Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing group members to ostracize (temporarily exclude) others via majority vote in a repeated public goods game increases average contributions by about 45 percentage points and increases average earnings (welfare) by about 23% compared to the control (no punishment) game. The effect is strongest for free-riders but is present for all contributor types. Ostracism is implemented with a small direct cost (1% of endowment per vote), but the main cost is the reduction in group size and thus potential public good returns. Anti-social punishment is observed: non-cooperators who are punished tend to retaliate by voting against others, but this is not seen among high contributors. The positive effect on efficiency is robust, but cooperation and efficiency decline when ostracism is removed (end-game effect).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ostracism (a form of social punishment) in a repeated public goods game with 4 players, 6 rounds, no communication, and MPCR=0.625 increases both contribution rates and average group earnings (efficiency) compared to a control game without punishment. The effect is large and robust across contributor types. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are clearly specified, making the findings directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar lab PGGs. The main caveat is the presence of anti-social punishment and the collapse of cooperation when punishment is removed, suggesting that the positive effect is contingent on the continued availability of the punishment mechanism.

- source: Lee, J., & Min, S. (2021). The effects of repeated induction of emotions on cooperation and punishment. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This experiment used a repeated public goods game with costly punishment and stranger matching. In each round, groups of three played five rounds, with either happiness or anger induced via video clips after the contribution stage. The only difference between treatments was the induced emotion. The main findings are: (1) Contributions decay over rounds in the happiness treatment but remain stable in the anger treatment; (2) Final-round welfare and group earnings are higher in the anger treatment than in the happiness treatment; (3) The key mechanism is that antisocial-punished cooperators reduce their contributions in the happiness treatment but not in the anger treatment; (4) Prosocial and antisocial punishment levels do not differ significantly between treatments, but their trends and effects do. Thus, the emotional context moderates the effect of punishment on efficiency: punishment sustains efficiency only when anger is present, not when happiness is induced.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated public goods games depends on the emotional environment. With standard parameters (3 players, 5 rounds, MPCR 0.6, costly punishment with 1:3 cost:impact), punishment sustains or increases efficiency when anger is induced, but not when happiness is induced. In the absence of induced happiness, the efficiency effect of punishment is positive or neutral; with induced happiness, the effect is negative. For prediction, the emotional context (incidental happiness vs. anger) is a critical moderator. The control (no-punishment) baseline is not directly reported, but a baseline without emotion induction (see Appendix J) shows similar trends to the anger treatment, suggesting that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is typical unless happiness is induced. Thus, when predicting efficiency with punishment enabled, consider whether the environment is emotionally neutral/angry (expect positive or stable efficiency effect) or happy (expect negative effect).

- source: Noussair, C., & Tucker, S. (2005). Combining monetary and social sanctions to promote cooperation. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with groups of 4 over 15 rounds, three treatments were compared: monetary punishment (MP), nonmonetary punishment (NP), and both punishments (BP). All sanctioning systems increased contributions and welfare relative to no-sanction baselines from prior literature. NP (informal) sanctions alone initially produced high welfare but lost effectiveness over time. MP (monetary) sanctions sustained higher contributions but incurred enforcement costs, reducing efficiency. The BP treatment, combining both, resulted in the highest group earnings and contributions in the later rounds, with lower deadweight loss from punishment than MP. The combined system allows for nuanced sanctioning, using costless informal sanctions when effective and costly formal sanctions when needed, maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard VCM with 4 players, 15 rounds, and no communication, enabling both monetary and nonmonetary punishment increases group efficiency (payoff/welfare) above either system alone. The efficiency gain is especially pronounced in later rounds, as informal-only sanctions lose effectiveness. The combined system also reduces the deadweight loss from punishment compared to monetary-only systems. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling both types of punishment should be expected to yield a substantial and sustained increase in efficiency, with the combined system outperforming either punishment type alone. The results are directly relevant for standard PGG/VCM designs with similar parameters.

- source: Ye, H., Zhang, Y. Y., & Lee, C. R. (2023). Dual Effects of Cognitive Ability in Public Goods Dilemmas. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally studies the effects of punishment (none, small, large) on efficiency (collective payoff) in both one-shot and repeated public goods games with groups of four. It finds that: (1) In one-shot PGGs, both small and large punishment increase collective payoff compared to no punishment, with large punishment having a stronger effect. Cognitive ability does not significantly affect efficiency except under large punishment, where high cognitive ability groups achieve higher payoffs. (2) In repeated PGGs, the effect of punishment is more complex: small punishment can reduce efficiency for high cognitive ability groups (who contribute less than in the no-punishment baseline), while large punishment increases efficiency for both high and low cognitive ability groups. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive and depends on both the magnitude of punishment and group cognitive composition.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs depends on the punishment magnitude and the cognitive ability of participants. Small punishment may not always increase efficiency and can even reduce it in repeated games with high cognitive ability groups. Large punishment reliably increases efficiency. The game design dimensions most relevant here are player_count (4), num_rounds (1 for one-shot, 10 for repeated), mpcr (0.5), punishment_cost (institutional, fixed, not paid by peers), and punishment_tech (penalty for under-contribution, with two levels: small and large). There is no chat, no reward, and players receive feedback about others' investments. The findings suggest that, for similar lab PGGs with institutional punishment, enabling large punishment will increase efficiency, but enabling only small punishment may not, especially in repeated settings with high cognitive ability participants.

- source: Wang, S. Y., & Qin, X. D. (2015). The Effect of Digitalization on Penalty Payments: An Experimental Investigation. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that exogenous punishment mechanisms (both cash and electronic token penalties) significantly increase cooperation and group earnings in a repeated public goods game compared to a no-punishment control. Cash penalties are more effective than token penalties at increasing both contributions and earnings, especially in the early rounds, but the difference between cash and token punishment diminishes over time. The deterrent effect of punishment is strong enough to compensate for the deadweight loss from penalties, resulting in higher overall group welfare. The results suggest that the tangibility of punishment (cash vs. digital) matters for short-run deterrence, but adaptation reduces this effect with experience.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that introducing exogenous monetary punishment in a standard linear public goods game increases group efficiency (earnings as a fraction of the cooperative optimum) relative to a no-punishment control. The effect is robust for both cash and digital (token) punishment, but is stronger for cash, especially in early rounds. The design dimensions are well specified: 4 players, 10 rounds, no chat, continuous integer contributions, MPCR=0.4, punishment is exogenous and automatic, and no reward mechanism is present. The control group efficiency (average earnings) is lower than in the punishment treatments, and the difference is statistically significant. This paper should be used as a strong positive case for predicting that exogenous monetary punishment increases efficiency in similar lab PGG designs, and that the tangibility of punishment (cash vs. digital) can moderate the effect, especially in the short run.

- source: Peng, H. C., & Fan, Y. H. (2023). Incomplete punishment networks, heterogeneity, and cooperation in public good experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the structure of the punishment network strongly moderates the effect of peer punishment on both cooperation and efficiency in public goods games with heterogeneous MPCRs. Incomplete punishment networks (circle and homo-pairwise) lead to higher group payoffs (efficiency) and higher contributions than the complete punishment network. The complete network, despite more potential punishers, results in lower efficiency, lower contributions, and lower punishment severity, likely due to diffusion of responsibility (bystander effect). The effect of punishment is thus not monotonic in network density: more potential punishers do not always increase efficiency. The type of MPCR (high or low) also affects individual contributions, with high-MPCR individuals contributing more. The punishment mechanism is costly and binary (1 token cost, 3 token impact), and the experiment uses stranger matching over 15 rounds. No communication or chat is allowed. The results suggest that the design of the punishment network is crucial for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games, especially with heterogeneous returns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that the structure of the punishment network (i.e., who can punish whom) is a critical moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games with heterogeneous MPCRs. When predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, it is not sufficient to know only the presence of punishment; the network structure (complete vs. incomplete, and the type of incomplete network) must be considered. In this design, incomplete networks (circle and homo-pairwise) outperform the complete network in both contributions and efficiency. The findings also show that punishment severity is lower in the complete network, likely due to a bystander effect. The experiment uses a four-player, 15-round, linear PGG with binary punishment, no chat, and random reassignment of MPCRs and groupings each round. These details should be matched when using this evidence for prediction. The results are most relevant for settings with heterogeneous returns and similar punishment mechanics.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Gürerk, & Yahsi, M. (2021). Culture and prevalence of sanctioning institutions. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game where participants can choose between a standard VCM (no punishment) and a PUN (punishment) institution, the PUN institution is strongly preferred over time in both Turkey and Germany. Contributions and payoffs are much higher in the PUN institution than in the VCM institution, and this holds across both cultures. The average payoff in the PUN institution exceeds that in the VCM institution by about 4 tokens (Turkey: 45.33 vs 41.34; Germany: 45.64 vs 41.75). The efficiency effect of punishment is thus strongly positive and robust to cultural context. The design allows for endogenous migration between institutions, and the results show that the efficiency advantage of punishment is not limited to exogenous assignment. Punishment is used primarily to discipline free-riders, and anti-social punishment is rare and similar across cultures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods games with endogenous institution choice, enabling punishment leads to a substantial and robust increase in group efficiency (payoff) compared to a control game without punishment. The effect is consistent across two culturally distinct countries (Turkey and Germany), and the game design is a close match to standard PGGs. The results suggest that, for similar game parameters (group size, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, information structure), enabling punishment will reliably increase efficiency, even when the control efficiency is low. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Angelovski, A., Di Cagno, D., Güth, W., Marazzi, F., & Panaccione, L. (2018). Does heterogeneity spoil the basket? The role of productivity and feedback information on public good provision. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 4-player, 10-round public goods game with centralized punishment, the presence of a sanctioner (punishment enabled) increases both average contributions and total group payoffs (social welfare) compared to a control with no punishment. However, if the sanctioner's payoff is made contingent on group cooperation (pool punishment), both contributions and efficiency are lower than when the sanctioner receives a fixed endowment. The negative effect of the pool punishment scheme is not due to differences in punishment frequency or severity, but rather to contributors' aversion to the sanctioner's extrinsic incentives (perceived as indirect free-riding). The paper provides direct evidence that punishment increases efficiency, but the institutional details of the punishment mechanism (especially the sanctioner's payoff scheme) are crucial moderators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment controls, but the magnitude of the effect depends on the institutional design—specifically, whether the sanctioner is paid a fixed or variable (pool) payoff. For prediction tasks, this means that the presence of punishment should be expected to increase efficiency, but the effect will be larger when the punishment institution uses fixed payoffs for sanctioners. The game design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and type of punishment, the sanctioner's payoff scheme, and the marginal per-capita return (MPCR). Control efficiency (no-punishment) is a strong baseline, but the treatment effect of punishment is moderated by the sanctioner's incentive structure.

- source: Kingsley, D. C. (2016). Endowment heterogeneity and peer punishment in a public good experiment: Cooperation and normative conflict. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that peer punishment increases contributions and group earnings in public goods games with homogeneous endowments, but not in games with heterogeneous endowments. In homogeneous groups, punishment mitigates the decay of cooperation and raises average contributions and pre-punishment earnings, though post-punishment earnings are lower due to the cost of punishment. In heterogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly increase contributions or earnings, and punishment costs further reduce efficiency. The results suggest that normative conflict arising from endowment heterogeneity undermines the effectiveness of peer punishment as a self-governance mechanism for promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on endowment homogeneity. When endowments are homogeneous, enabling punishment increases contributions and can increase efficiency (though punishment costs may offset gains). When endowments are heterogeneous and endowment information is not available in the punishment stage, enabling punishment does not increase contributions or efficiency, and punishment costs reduce group earnings. For prediction, the presence of endowment heterogeneity (without endowment information in punishment) should be expected to moderate or eliminate the positive effect of punishment on efficiency, even if control efficiency is known. The game is a continuous-contribution, interior-solution PGG with 4 players, 15 rounds, no chat, and punishment cost/impact of 1:3.

- source: Gürerk (2013). Social learning increases the acceptance and the efficiency of punishment institutions in social dilemmas. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that endogenously chosen punishment institutions in a repeated public goods game become more efficient and more widely accepted when participants are provided with social history (detailed information about the outcomes of a previous cohort). Social history leads to higher initial acceptance of the punishment institution, higher initial and sustained contributions, lower punishment expenses, and higher average payoffs. The efficiency (measured as surplus ratio and average payoff) is significantly higher in the social history treatment than in the baseline. The effect is attributed to social learning rather than mere imitation, as shown by a control treatment with only partial history information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods games with endogenous institution choice, enabling social learning (by providing detailed social history) increases both the acceptance and the efficiency of punishment institutions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment is larger when participants have access to social learning or history information. The paper also provides detailed information on game parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, no reward, etc.), making it directly informative for mapping design dimensions to efficiency outcomes. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency when social learning is present, and provide baseline efficiency values for both control (no punishment) and treatment (punishment enabled) conditions.

- source: Kamei, K. (2024). Self-regulatory resources and institutional formation: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with five players and MPCR 0.4, the introduction of punishment mechanisms (either formal, with a fixed administrative cost and group-voted sanction rate, or informal, with peer-to-peer costly punishment) leads to large and significant increases in group efficiency and payoffs. Without punishment, free riding is prevalent and efficiency is low. With punishment enabled, average group payoffs rise significantly, and a much higher proportion of groups achieve high payoff thresholds. The effect is present regardless of whether participants’ self-regulatory resources are depleted. Formal punishment, when implemented with a deterrent sanction rate, achieves almost full efficiency. Informal punishment also increases efficiency, though not as much as formal deterrent punishment. The act of voting on institutions or sanction rates does not itself account for the efficiency gains; the presence of the punishment mechanism is the key driver.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a standard repeated public goods game. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, no reward, etc.) are all clearly specified and match the canonical PGG setup. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low efficiency, while enabling punishment (either formal or informal) produces large, robust increases in efficiency and group payoff. The results are robust to manipulations of self-regulatory resources and to whether the punishment institution is chosen endogenously or imposed exogenously. For prediction tasks, the findings support a strong positive effect of punishment on efficiency in similar game designs, with formal deterrent punishment achieving near-full efficiency and informal punishment producing substantial, though somewhat lower, efficiency gains.

- source: Mantilla, C., Zhou, L., Wang, C., Yang, D. H., Shen, S. P., & Seabright, P. (2021). Favoring your in-group can harm both them and you: Ethnicity and public goods provision in China. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in public goods games with punishment, ethnically mixed groups experience lower contributions and lower group earnings than homogeneous groups. This is primarily because participants are less likely to punish co-ethnic free-riders when out-group members are present, reducing the deterrent effect of punishment. The effect is strongest in unbalanced mixed groups (3+1 composition), where efficiency losses are largest. The study provides direct evidence that the effectiveness of punishment as a cooperation-enforcing institution is undermined by in-group favoritism in diverse groups, leading to lower efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with punishment, the efficiency gains from punishment are reduced or even reversed in ethnically mixed groups due to in-group favoritism in punishment assignment. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the group composition (homogeneous vs mixed) is a critical moderator: punishment is less effective (or even harmful) in mixed groups because in-group members are less likely to punish each other, reducing deterrence and lowering contributions and earnings. The game uses standard PGG parameters (4 players, 5 rounds, MPCR=0.5, punishment cost=2, punishment impact=5, no chat, no rewards, anonymous punishment). Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the main comparison is between group types with punishment enabled. The findings suggest that, holding other design dimensions constant, enabling punishment in mixed-ethnicity groups may not increase efficiency and can even decrease it relative to homogeneous groups.

- source: Bruhin, A., Janizzi, K., & Thöni, C. (2020). Uncovering the heterogeneity behind cross-cultural variation in antisocial punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper shows that the effectiveness of peer punishment in public goods games is highly heterogeneous and depends critically on the composition of punishment types within groups. Using a finite mixture model, the authors classify subjects into three types: non-punishers (Type N), antisocial/free rider punishers (Type AF), and free rider-only punishers (Type F). The presence of Type AF subjects, who punish both low and high contributors, is strongly associated with lower group earnings and reduced efficiency. In contrast, groups with only Type F and Type N subjects achieve much higher efficiency. The number of Type AF subjects in a group explains a large share of the variance in group earnings, more than baseline cooperativeness or period effects. Cross-cultural differences in the prevalence of Type AF subjects explain much of the observed variation in the effectiveness of punishment across societies.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not uniform: it depends on the prevalence of antisocial punishers (Type AF) in the group or population. In environments where antisocial punishment is common, enabling punishment can substantially reduce efficiency. In environments where only free rider punishment occurs, enabling punishment can increase or sustain efficiency. The paper provides quantitative evidence (e.g., groups with four Type AF subjects earn 68.7% less than groups with only Type F/N) and regression models showing that the number of Type AF subjects is a strong predictor of group earnings. Thus, for prediction, the cultural or group composition regarding punishment types is a critical moderator, and control game efficiency alone is insufficient to predict treatment efficiency without considering this heterogeneity.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Morales, A. J., & Walker, J. M. (2019). Peer punishment of acts of omission versus acts of commission in give and take social dilemmas. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with four players and 30 rounds (20 with/without punishment), the opportunity for peer punishment increases group contributions in both Give and Take frames. However, only in the Take frame does punishment significantly increase group earnings (efficiency) relative to the no-punishment baseline. In the Give frame, the increase in contributions does not translate into a significant increase in group earnings, as the costs of punishment offset the gains. The effect is driven by more effective targeting of low contributors in the Take game, where acts of commission are punished more harshly and by more group members. The magnitude of punishment per se is not higher in the Take game, but the likelihood of being punished for low contributions is greater. Thus, the efficiency-enhancing effect of punishment is context-dependent, being stronger in environments where non-cooperation is framed as an act of commission.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends on the framing of the social dilemma (Give vs. Take). For games isomorphic to the Take frame (where non-cooperation is an act of commission), enabling punishment is likely to yield a substantial increase in efficiency, as measured by group earnings relative to the social optimum. For standard Give frame public goods games, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not reliably increase efficiency, due to the costliness of punishment. The game design dimensions—player count (4), number of rounds (30), no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR 0.5, punishment cost 1, punishment impact 3, no rewards, full information on round number and others' contributions, but no punishment source identification—are all precisely reported and should be used for prediction. The control efficiency (group earnings in the no-punishment condition) is a strong predictor of the potential for efficiency gains from punishment, especially in Take games where baseline efficiency is lower and punishment is more effective.

- source: Cobo-Reyes, R., Katz, G., & Meraglia, S. (2019). Endogenous sanctioning institutions and migration patterns: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with two groups and endogenous migration, the presence of formal sanctioning institutions (a fixed fee plus a fine on private account allocations) leads to higher contributions and significantly higher average payoffs compared to sanction-free groups. When only one group has sanctions (No-Voting treatment), that group becomes overpopulated, and payoff inequality emerges. When groups can vote on whether to implement sanctions (Voting treatment), most groups eventually choose sanctions, group sizes become more balanced, migration decreases, and overall efficiency and average payoffs increase further. The ability to endogenously choose institutions (via voting) leads to more efficient and equitable outcomes than exogenously imposed sanctions. The effect of sanctions on efficiency is robust and positive, and the endogenous choice of sanctions amplifies this effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling formal punishment in a repeated PGG with migration increases efficiency and average payoffs, relative to a control without punishment. The effect is even stronger when the sanctioning institution is chosen endogenously by group vote. The design dimensions—such as player count (10, split into two groups of 5), number of rounds (30), absence of chat, continuous contributions, and a formal, automatic punishment mechanism—are all specified. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR) varies with group size but is always less than 1, and the punishment is highly effective (80% fine on private account, plus a fixed fee). There is no reward mechanism. Players are shown summary information about both groups' contributions and rule-sets. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will reliably increase efficiency, and allowing endogenous institutional choice (voting) will further boost efficiency and reduce inequality. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is substantially lower than with punishment enabled. This paper is highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Boosey, L., & Isaac, R. M. (2016). Asymmetric network monitoring and punishment in public goods experiments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment compares three network structures (Complete, Circle, Asymmetric) in a repeated linear public goods game with decentralized punishment. In all treatments, punishment is possible, but the network structure determines who can punish whom. The Asymmetric network features one under-monitored player (D) who can only be punished by one other player (C), while C is responsible for punishing all others. The main findings are: (1) Average contributions and efficiency are significantly lower in the Asymmetric network than in the Complete or Circle networks; (2) The drop in efficiency is driven entirely by the under-monitored player D, who contributes less and retaliates more against punishment; (3) There is no evidence that players with sole punishment responsibility (C) punish their unique target (D) more harshly; (4) Retaliatory (anti-social) punishment is more frequent from the under-monitored player, deterring effective punishment and reducing efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that network asymmetry can undermine the positive effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment network is a critical moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency in public goods games. When punishment is possible but monitoring/punishment is asymmetric (i.e., some players are under-monitored), efficiency gains from punishment are reduced or eliminated, primarily due to lower contributions and increased retaliation by under-monitored players. In symmetric networks (Complete, Circle), punishment supports higher contributions and efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the symmetry and coverage of the punishment network. If the control (no punishment) efficiency is known, the expected efficiency gain from punishment will be lower or even negative in asymmetric/patched networks compared to symmetric ones, even with identical punishment parameters.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Godoy, S., Morales, A. J., & Walker, J. M. (2016). An individualistic approach to institution formation in public good games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated linear public goods games with four players and 20 rounds, the study compares four treatments: (1) standard VCM (no punishment), (2) exogenous universal punishment (StdPun), (3) endogenous punishment with zero cost to acquire the right to punish (CTP0), and (4) endogenous punishment with a small or large cost to acquire the right to punish (CTP1, CTP5). When the right to punish is costless (CTP0), participation is high enough that group contributions and efficiency are as high as in the exogenous punishment treatment (StdPun), and both are significantly higher than in the no-punishment baseline (VCM). When there is any cost to acquiring the right to punish (CTP1, CTP5), participation drops, antisocial punishment increases, and group contributions and efficiency fall to levels not significantly different from the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the size of the cost: even a negligible cost eliminates the efficiency gains from punishment. The number of persistent punishers is a key moderator: at least two are needed for punishment to be effective. The study provides direct evidence that the institutional details of how punishment rights are provided (costless vs. costly, endogenous vs. exogenous) are critical for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games depend critically on whether the right to punish is costless and universally available, or must be acquired at a cost. When punishment is exogenously provided or costlessly available to all, efficiency increases substantially relative to the no-punishment baseline. When even a small cost is required to acquire the right to punish, participation drops, antisocial punishment rises, and efficiency gains disappear. For prediction tasks, this means that the presence and cost of acquiring punishment rights are key moderators: if the game design includes a cost to acquire punishment rights, do not expect efficiency gains from punishment, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. If punishment is costless and universally available, expect large efficiency gains. The number of persistent punishers is also important: at least two are needed for effectiveness in this four-player design.

- source: Casari, M., & Luini, L. (2009). Cooperation under alternative punishment institutions: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This experiment compares three peer punishment institutions in a repeated public goods game: Baseline (standard, individual punishment), Sequential (punishment with information about others' punishment actions), and Consensual (punishment only enacted if at least two group members request it). All treatments use groups of 5, 20 rounds (10 without punishment, 10 with), no communication, and full information about group contributions and payoffs (but not identities). The marginal per capita return is 0.4. Punishment costs 1 token per point and reduces the target's payoff by 3 tokens per point. The main findings are: (1) Punishment opportunities increase contributions in all treatments, but only the Consensual institution increases net earnings (efficiency) above the no-punishment baseline. In Baseline and Sequential, punishment reduces efficiency due to high punishment costs. (2) The Consensual institution achieves higher cooperation and efficiency with less punishment, as it censors anti-social punishment (punishment of high contributors) and requires consensus to punish. (3) Providing more information about others' punishment actions (Sequential) does not improve coordination or efficiency. (4) The form of the punishment institution is a critical moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of enabling punishment in public goods games is highly sensitive to the institutional details of the punishment mechanism. In standard and sequential punishment settings (where any individual can punish unilaterally), punishment increases contributions but reduces efficiency due to high punishment costs. In contrast, a consensual punishment rule (requiring at least two agents to agree to punish) both increases contributions and improves efficiency, even surpassing the no-punishment baseline. For prediction, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be inferred from control efficiency and game structure alone; the specific punishment institution (especially whether it censors anti-social punishment and requires consensus) is a key moderator. The paper also shows that increasing information about others' punishment actions does not necessarily improve efficiency. Thus, for games with similar structure, expect punishment to reduce efficiency unless the institution filters out anti-social punishment and requires consensus.

- source: Arechar, A. A., Gächter, S., & Molleman, L. (2018). Conducting interactive experiments online. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that enabling peer punishment in a repeated four-person public goods game (20 rounds: 10 without, 10 with punishment) substantially increases average contributions and group efficiency in both laboratory and online (MTurk) samples. In the no-punishment condition, average contributions and earnings are lower and decay over time. When punishment is introduced, contributions jump and are sustained at higher levels, and average earnings per participant per period increase (lab: 22.64 to 26.91; online: 22.64 to 26.91, as reported). Punishment is mostly prosocial (cooperators punish defectors), and the effect is robust across both environments. The paper also finds that attrition (dropout) is not related to experimental condition or behavior, supporting the internal validity of the results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a standard repeated PGG with peer punishment (cost 1, impact 3), enabling punishment increases group efficiency (average earnings as a fraction of the maximum possible) compared to the same game without punishment. The effect is robust across both lab and online samples, and the design matches canonical PGG-with-punishment studies. The paper's detailed reporting of game parameters, outcome measures, and the direct comparison of control and punishment conditions make it highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., Ramalingam, A., Sääksvuori, L., & Walker, J. M. (2015). Incomplete punishment networks in public goods games: experimental evidence. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study experimentally manipulates punishment network structure and punishment capacity in a repeated linear public goods game with four players per group. Treatments include no-punishment, complete punishment, pairwise punishment, and untouchable punishment networks, as well as variants with increased punishment capacity. The main findings are: (1) Complete punishment networks lead to higher contributions than no-punishment or pairwise networks, but not necessarily higher group earnings after accounting for punishment costs. (2) Pairwise punishment networks are less effective at increasing contributions and do not improve efficiency. (3) Increasing punishment capacity in incomplete networks does not significantly improve contributions or efficiency. (4) The structure of the punishment network is more important than the absolute punishment capacity for group outcomes. (5) Targeted revenge is a major driver of antisocial punishment. (6) The presence of an 'untouchable' (who cannot punish or be punished) does not significantly reduce the willingness of others to contribute, but untouchables contribute less and earn more.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment network (who can punish whom) is a critical moderator. Enabling punishment in a complete network increases contributions but does not necessarily increase efficiency due to the cost of punishment. Incomplete networks (e.g., pairwise) do not yield efficiency gains and may even reduce efficiency. Increasing punishment capacity without changing network structure does not improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting efficiency with punishment enabled, it is crucial to account for both the network structure and the cost/effectiveness of punishment. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency unless network structure and punishment costs are considered.

- source: Nikiforakis, N., Normann, H. T., & Wallace, B. (2010). Asymmetric Enforcement of Cooperation in a Social Dilemma. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment compares symmetric and asymmetric punishment institutions in a standard public goods game with punishment. Asymmetry is introduced by giving one player higher punishment effectiveness (impact per unit cost) than the other three. The main findings are: (1) Asymmetric punishment institutions are as effective as symmetric ones in sustaining cooperation and efficiency, as measured by group earnings. (2) There is no significant difference in efficiency (group earnings) between symmetric and asymmetric punishment institutions, controlling for average punishment effectiveness. (3) Higher average punishment effectiveness increases contributions, but does not significantly increase efficiency. (4) Strong players (with higher punishment effectiveness) punish more and earn more than weak players, but do not contribute less. (5) The behavioral regularities of symmetric punishment institutions (e.g., punishment targeting free riders, response to punishment) carry over to asymmetric institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with punishment, the presence of asymmetry in punishment effectiveness (i.e., one player can punish more effectively than others) does not significantly affect group efficiency compared to symmetric punishment, as long as average punishment effectiveness is held constant. Thus, for the prediction task, the efficiency effect of enabling punishment should be predicted primarily based on the average punishment effectiveness, not on whether punishment power is symmetric or asymmetric. The paper also confirms that higher average punishment effectiveness increases contributions, but this does not necessarily translate into higher efficiency due to the costs of punishment. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the main comparison is between symmetric and asymmetric punishment with the same average effectiveness.

- source: Fatas, E., Melendez-Jimenez, M. A., & Solaz, H. (2020). SOCIAL HIERARCHIES: A LABORATORY STUDY ON PUNISHMENT PATTERNS ACROSS NETWORKS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in repeated public goods games increases contributions in all network structures, but the effect on efficiency (group profit) depends on the network. In the complete and star (hierarchical) networks, punishment leads to higher efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline. In the circle and line networks, punishment increases contributions but the cost of punishment outweighs the gains, resulting in lower efficiency than the no-punishment baseline. Central players in the star network use punishment in a prosocial way and do not retaliate, leading to higher group efficiency. In contrast, antisocial punishment and retaliation are more common in the line and circle networks, undermining efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to the network structure. If the game design includes a hierarchical (star) or complete network, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency relative to the control. In other incomplete networks (circle, line), punishment may reduce efficiency due to costly antisocial punishment and retaliation. The paper also provides detailed parameterization of the game (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech), making it highly informative for prediction tasks that use these dimensions. Control (VCM) and treatment (PUN) efficiency values are reported for each network, allowing for direct calibration.

- source: Waichman, I. (2020). LINKING WEALTH AND PUNISHMENT EFFECTIVENESS: PUNISHMENT AND COOPERATION UNDER CONGRUENT HETEROGENEITIES. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This lab experiment uses a linear public good game with peer punishment to study the effects of heterogeneity in endowment and punishment effectiveness, both separately and jointly. The main findings are: (1) Heterogeneity in punishment effectiveness alone does not affect efficiency or cooperation compared to symmetric groups. (2) Endowment heterogeneity (rich and poor members) reduces efficiency and cooperation, as rich members contribute a lower share of their endowment. (3) When both endowment and punishment effectiveness are heterogeneous (either the rich or the poor is the most effective punisher), efficiency and cooperation are not reduced compared to symmetry, and are higher than under endowment heterogeneity alone. (4) Among groups that achieve high cooperation, efficiency is higher in the 'Rich strong' treatment (richest is most effective punisher) than in the 'Poor strong' treatment, because less punishment is needed to sustain cooperation. (5) Overall, congruent heterogeneities (richest is most effective punisher) do not hinder and may even facilitate efficiency and cooperation compared to endowment heterogeneity alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in linear public good games with peer punishment, the presence of punishment generally increases efficiency compared to control conditions with endowment heterogeneity alone. However, the efficiency gain from punishment is moderated by the structure of heterogeneity: punishment is most effective (in terms of efficiency) when either all players are symmetric or when the richest player is also the most effective punisher. Endowment heterogeneity without matching punishment effectiveness reduces efficiency, but joint heterogeneities (especially congruent ones) restore efficiency to levels comparable to symmetry. For prediction, this suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency, but the magnitude of the effect depends on the alignment of endowment and punishment effectiveness. The control efficiency (without punishment) is lowest under endowment heterogeneity, and the efficiency gain from enabling punishment is largest in this case, especially if punishment effectiveness is also heterogeneous and congruent with endowment.

- source: Joseph, G., Miquel-Florensa, J., Pahuja, S., Hoo, Y. R., & Tebekew, T. (2025). Motivating Public Sector Employees: Public Good Contributions in Addis Ababa Water and Sewerage Authority. *Economic Development and Cultural Change*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compared three public good game variants: (1) standard, (2) threshold (group must reach a minimum contribution or all is lost), and (3) monitor (one randomly chosen, anonymous group member can punish others at a cost). Both the threshold and monitor games increased individual contributions compared to the standard game, with the threshold game increasing contributions the most. However, because about one-quarter of groups failed to meet the threshold and lost all contributions, the monitor game produced the highest average group payoff (efficiency). The punishment mechanism was effective: monitors punished in about 73% of groups, and punishment was targeted at low contributors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to order effects and individual characteristics. The study provides direct evidence that introducing costly punishment in a one-shot, anonymous, five-player public good game increases group efficiency relative to the control.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a five-player, one-shot public good game with continuous contributions, no communication, and a known MPCR of 0.4. The monitor (punishment) treatment increased group efficiency/payoff compared to the control. The punishment mechanism involved a cost of 1 token to the punisher and a loss of 3 tokens to the punished. The design closely matches standard PGGs, and the efficiency outcome is directly reported. The findings support the prediction that, in similar settings, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The paper also provides comparative evidence on a threshold (common goal) mechanism, which increased contributions but did not increase group efficiency due to the risk of losing all contributions if the threshold was not met.

- source: Glöckner, A., Kube, S., & Nicklisch, A. (2018). The joint benefits of observed and unobserved social sanctions. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with four players over ten rounds, the authors compare four conditions: no punishment, observed punishment only, unobserved (delayed) punishment only, and both punishment channels available. Both observed and unobserved punishment alone increase contributions but do not improve efficiency (average payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline, due to the costliness of punishment. However, when both punishment channels are available, group efficiency is significantly higher than in all other conditions. This is because the threat of unobserved punishment increases the effectiveness of observed punishment, leading to higher cooperation with less punishment expenditure. The combination of punishment channels thus overcomes the typical efficiency losses seen in standard lab PGG punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games depends critically on the punishment institution. Standard punishment (observed or unobserved alone) does not improve efficiency over the control, but the combination of both channels leads to a large positive effect. For prediction, this means that the presence of multiple punishment channels (observed and unobserved) is a key moderator: if only one is present, efficiency is not improved; if both are present, efficiency is substantially increased. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the punishment technology (timing/observability), cost, and whether multiple channels are available. Control efficiency (no punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency unless the institutional details of punishment are considered.

- source: Grieco, D., Faillo, M., & Zarri, L. (2017). Enforcing cooperation in public goods games: Is one punisher enough?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with punishment, concentrating punishment power in the hands of the top contributor (with frequent turnover) significantly increases both cooperation and group earnings compared to the standard decentralized punishment baseline. Randomly assigning a single punisher per round does not improve efficiency. The mechanism works not because of immunity from punishment, but because the right to punish motivates higher contributions. However, antisocial punishment remains present. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment institution—specifically, how the punisher is selected—has a strong effect on both cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and a convex punishment cost function, assigning punishment power to the top contributor (with frequent turnover) increases group earnings (i.e., efficiency) relative to both the no-punishment and the standard decentralized punishment baseline. The effect is not observed when the single punisher is selected randomly. For prediction, this suggests that the selection mechanism for the punisher is a critical moderator: 'top contributor as punisher' yields higher efficiency than both unrestricted and random single punisher regimes, holding other design features constant. The results are most directly applicable to games with similar structure (N=4, 20 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, anonymous, continuous contributions, punishment cost and impact as specified).

- source: Qin, X. D., & Wang, S. Y. (2013). Using an exogenous mechanism to examine efficient probabilistic punishment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing an exogenous, probabilistic punishment mechanism in a repeated public goods game significantly increases both contributions and average payoffs (efficiency) compared to a control with no punishment. Among three punishment treatments with equal expected penalty (10 points), the treatment with a 50% probability of a 20-point punishment produces the highest average payoff and contribution, outperforming both a 100% probability of a 10-point punishment and a 20% probability of a 50-point punishment. The 20% probability/50-point punishment treatment does not significantly increase efficiency over the control. The results show an inverse-U relationship between punishment probability and efficiency, given constant expected penalty. The findings highlight that the design of the punishment mechanism (probability and severity) crucially affects efficiency, not just the expected value of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with exogenous, automatic punishment for the lowest contributor, enabling punishment increases efficiency compared to no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude of the effect depends on the probability and severity of punishment. For prediction, the presence of exogenous punishment (with moderate probability and severity) should be expected to increase efficiency, but low-probability, high-severity punishment may not improve efficiency over baseline. The results also show that the structure of punishment (probability vs. severity) matters even when expected penalty is held constant. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in similar lab PGGs.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Kingsley, D. C. (2021). Norm enforcement with incomplete information *. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with two high-endowment and two low-endowment players per group, and costly peer punishment, the ability to observe others' endowments (complete information) leads to the emergence and enforcement of efficient 'contribute-your-endowment' norms. This results in higher contributions and group earnings, especially from high-endowment players, and higher payoffs for low-endowment players. When endowments are private (incomplete information), groups adapt by enforcing a 'contribute-the-Low-endowment' norm, primarily enforced by low-endowment players.
