This norm prevents total unraveling of cooperation but enables high-endowment players to mimic low-endowment behavior and contribute less, reducing group efficiency. The expected costs and payoffs of noncompliance are explicitly estimated, showing that punishment is less targeted and less effective at sustaining high efficiency under incomplete information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the information structure. When group members can observe both contributions and endowments, punishment supports high efficiency by enabling enforcement of optimal contribution norms. When endowments are unobservable, punishment still deters total free-riding but cannot sustain full efficiency, as high-endowment members can hide behind the low-endowment norm. For prediction, this means that the presence of punishment will increase efficiency relative to no punishment, but the magnitude of the effect is moderated by whether individual capacities (endowments) are observable. The paper also provides detailed parameterization of the punishment technology and payoff structure, which can be used to calibrate predictive models.

- source: Koch, C., Nikiforakis, N., & Noussair, C. N. (2021). Covenants before the swords: The limits to efficient cooperation in heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment with heterogeneous groups (different returns from the public good), the authors compare three treatments: (1) communication and punishment available together (ComEarly), (2) punishment first, then communication added later (ComLate), and (3) communication only (ComOnly). Efficiency, measured as group earnings relative to the maximum possible, is highest when both communication and punishment are available from the start (ComEarly: 87% of maximum), lower with only one instrument (ComLate: 60-68%; ComOnly: 76% in part 1, 60% overall). However, even with both instruments, most groups do not achieve full efficiency, as covenants typically require compromise between efficiency and equality. Early communication is crucial: it enables groups to establish covenants and avoid costly punishment cycles (feuds), leading to higher efficiency. Late communication cannot fully overcome the negative effects of prior sanctioning. The study demonstrates that while punishment and communication together improve efficiency, concerns for equality limit the ability of heterogeneous groups to reach the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with heterogeneous returns, enabling both communication and punishment increases efficiency (group earnings) compared to either instrument alone. However, the efficiency gain is limited by the tendency of groups to compromise between efficiency and equality, especially when group members have different returns. The timing of communication is a key moderator: early communication (before punishment) is much more effective at raising efficiency than late communication. For prediction, this suggests that the presence of both communication and punishment, especially with early communication, should be expected to yield higher efficiency than punishment alone, but not full efficiency. Control efficiency (with only communication) is a strong predictor, but the incremental effect of punishment depends on whether communication is also present and when it occurs. The results are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in heterogeneous PGGs.

- source: Marcin, I., Robalo, P., & Tausch, F. (2019). Institutional endogeneity and third-party punishment in social dilemmas. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public good game with binary (all-or-nothing) contributions, groups could vote to introduce a third-party punishment institution. When punishment was enabled, a B-type player could assign costly deduction points to defectors (non-contributors), with each deduction point costing the punisher 1 point and reducing the defector's income by 3 points. The main experimental manipulation was whether the punishment institution was implemented endogenously (by majority vote) or exogenously (by random assignment, sometimes overruling the vote). The study finds that: (1) Punishment is significantly milder when the institution is endogenously chosen, but is more effective in inducing cooperation per unit of punishment; (2) Exogenous punishment is harsher, leading to similar or slightly higher cooperation in the long run, but at higher punishment cost; (3) Overall, group efficiency (total payoffs) is not significantly different between endogenous and exogenous punishment conditions; (4) The presence of punishment (vs. no punishment) increases cooperation and efficiency, but the method of institution selection does not further increase efficiency. The study provides direct evidence on efficiency, group payoff, and the moderating role of institutional endogeneity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a public good game increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but the way the punishment institution is implemented (endogenous vs. exogenous) does not significantly affect overall efficiency, even though it affects punishment severity and effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism is a key driver of efficiency gains, but whether the institution is chosen by vote or imposed does not further increase group efficiency. The game design dimensions (player count, binary contribution, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, etc.) are all clearly specified, making this study highly informative for mapping design to efficiency outcomes. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than with punishment enabled, and the efficiency gain from punishment is robust to the method of institution selection.

- source: Kamei, K., & Putterman, L. (2015). In broad daylight: Fuller information and higher-order punishment opportunities can promote cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally studies how the availability of higher-order punishment (punishing punishers or non-punishers) affects cooperation and efficiency in repeated public goods games. The key findings are: (1) When higher-order punishment is enabled with full information (who punished whom), fixed group member IDs, and explicit display of past history, both cooperation and efficiency are significantly higher than in the control with only first-order punishment. (2) When higher-order punishment is enabled but information is ego-centric (subjects only see who punished them), with a dedicated counter-punishment stage and no history, efficiency is significantly lower than in the control. (3) In other treatments (e.g., with only one punishment stage or with history but ego-centric information), efficiency is not significantly different from the control. The main mechanism is that full information allows counter-punishment to be more strongly directed at anti-social or perverse punishers, supporting pro-social punishment and discouraging inefficient retaliation. The results show that the design of information and punishment opportunities moderates the effect of higher-order punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect depends on the information structure and punishment design. If punishment is enabled with full information about who punished whom, fixed IDs, and explicit history, efficiency is likely to increase relative to the control. If punishment is enabled with only ego-centric information, a dedicated counter-punishment stage, and no history, efficiency may decrease. In other cases, the effect may be neutral. The findings suggest that the game design dimensions—especially information about punishment, identifiability, and history—are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the relative differences between treatments are clear.

- source: Bochet, O., Page, T., & Putterman, L. (2006). Communication and punishment in voluntary contribution experiments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard voluntary contribution mechanism (VCM) public goods game, adding a costly punishment option increases contributions but does not increase efficiency or group earnings, because the costs of punishing and being punished offset the gains from higher contributions. Communication, especially face-to-face or via anonymous chat, dramatically increases both contributions and efficiency, with chat being nearly as effective as face-to-face. Adding punishment to communication does not significantly increase contributions or efficiency further. Numerical 'cheap talk' communication (non-binding numerical announcements) has no significant effect on contributions or efficiency. The study also finds that a notable fraction of punishment is 'perverse' (targeted at high contributors), which can reduce their future contributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a 4-player, 10-round VCM with MPCR=0.4, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve efficiency or group earnings compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The cost of punishment cancels out the benefit from higher contributions. If the control game has low efficiency, enabling punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency unless communication is also enabled. Communication (especially open-ended, verbal or chat) is a much more effective lever for increasing efficiency. The specific punishment parameters (cost=0.25 per $1 reduced, impact=1 per $0.25 spent) are important for generalization. The findings suggest that, for similar lab VCMs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be similar to the control, unless communication is also present.

- source: Fu, T. T., Ji, Y. N., Kamei, K., & Putterman, L. (2017). Punishment can support cooperation even when punishable. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment increases cooperation and group earnings in a standard public goods game. When higher-order punishment (counter-punishment) is allowed, it occurs frequently, especially in response to antisocial punishment, but does not significantly reduce the positive effect of punishment on contributions or efficiency. All punishment treatments (first-order and higher-order) result in higher average earnings than the baseline with no punishment. There is no evidence that higher-order punishment deters first-order punishment or reduces efficiency. The presence of higher-order punishment does not provide a net inducement to punish non-punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players and 15 rounds, enabling peer punishment increases efficiency (group earnings) compared to no-punishment baseline. Adding a second or third stage for higher-order punishment (counter-punishment) does not significantly reduce efficiency or the positive effect of punishment. Thus, for similar game designs, the presence of punishment (even if punishers can be punished) is likely to increase efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Botelho, A., Harrison, G. W., Pinto, L. M. C., Ross, D., & Rutström, E. E. (2022). Endogenous choice of institutional punishment mechanisms to promote social cooperation. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study replicates and extends classic public goods experiments by allowing subjects to experience both a punishment-enabled and a no-punishment institution, then vote on which to use for a high-stakes final round. Across 13 sessions, punishment increased contributions but almost always reduced average profits due to the direct costs of punishing. Only one session (out of 13) saw a majority vote for the punishment institution, and this was in a small cohort with high returns and high initial cooperation. Statistical analysis shows that subjects' votes are overwhelmingly determined by which institution gave them higher profits, not by which supported higher cooperation. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in matching protocol, order of experience, and marginal per-capita return. The paper also reviews prior studies and finds similar patterns: punishment often fails to improve, and sometimes reduces, group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard public goods games with costly voluntary punishment, enabling punishment typically reduces efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, even though it increases contributions. The effect is robust across a range of game parameters (player count, MPCR, matching protocol, order, and returns). Exceptions are rare and occur mainly when punishment is used very sparingly and cooperation is already high. For prediction tasks, the presence of costly punishment should generally be expected to lower efficiency relative to control, unless the design or context strongly favors efficient, low-use punishment. The paper also highlights that the marginal per-capita return (MPCR) and matching protocol (reputation effects) can moderate the effect, but the main driver is the costliness and frequency of punishment.

- source: Kim, H., Iris, D., Lee, J., & Tavoni, A. (2025). Representation, Peer Pressure and Punishment in a Public Goods Game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contributions in all treatments, but the efficiency gains depend on the decision structure. In the control (NoR, self-representation), punishment increases contributions to 75% of the endowment and does not reduce group payoffs, so efficiency is maintained or improved. In representation treatments (RnoM and RM), punishment increases contributions but also increases antisocial punishment, leading to lower group payoffs and reduced efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline. Communication (RM) between team members and representatives slightly improves outcomes compared to RnoM but does not fully restore efficiency. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive in self-representation but negative or neutral under representation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is moderated by the decision structure: punishment is most effective (and efficient) when individuals decide for themselves, but can reduce efficiency when representatives decide for groups, especially if communication is limited. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment will likely increase efficiency in self-representation settings, but may decrease or only modestly increase efficiency in representation settings, particularly when antisocial punishment is prevalent. The marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction models. Communication channels (chat/messages) can moderate but not fully reverse negative efficiency effects under representation.

- source: Masclet, D., Noussair, C. N., & Villeval, M. C. (2013). THREAT AND PUNISHMENT IN PUBLIC GOOD EXPERIMENTS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with costly punishment, adding a stage where players can issue non-binding threats to punish (conditioned on others' contribution levels) increases average contributions and, in the latter periods, increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to a baseline with punishment but no threats. However, the cost of punishment initially offsets the benefit of higher contributions, so efficiency only improves in later rounds. If a second-order punishment stage is added (where players can punish those who fail to carry out their threats), threats become less severe, contributions and efficiency fall, and welfare is equal to or below the baseline. The effect of threats is robust to changes in punishment effectiveness, but the efficiency gain is less persistent when punishment is less effective. Overall, threats can improve efficiency, but only under certain conditions and mainly in longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with punishment, adding a non-binding threat stage can increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a baseline with punishment but no threats, but only in the latter periods of repeated play. The efficiency gain is modest and depends on the absence of second-order punishment (punishing those who fail to carry out threats). If second-order punishment is possible, efficiency falls to or below baseline. The effect is also sensitive to punishment effectiveness (punishment technology). For prediction, this suggests that enabling threats (structured, non-binding, and observable) can increase efficiency above the control (punishment-only) baseline, but only in longer games and only if threats are not themselves subject to enforcement. The effect is stronger when punishment is more effective (higher punishment technology). The control efficiency (punishment-only) is a good lower bound; threats can improve on this, but only under the right design conditions.

- source: Lopez, M. C., Murphy, J. J., Spraggon, J. M., & Stranlund, J. K. (2012). COMPARING THE EFFECTIVENESS OF REGULATION AND PRO-SOCIAL EMOTIONS TO ENHANCE COOPERATION: EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE FROM FISHING COMMUNITIES IN COLOMBIA. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a framed field experiment with Colombian fishing communities, groups of 5 played a 15-round linear public goods game with a marginal per capita return of 0.4. Treatments included a baseline, a 'frame' (reminder of social optimum), 'guilt' (private feedback), 'shame' (random public revelation of non-cooperation), and two punishment treatments (low and high penalty, both with 1/5 audit probability and per-unit fines). Both punishment treatments increased contributions and expected earnings relative to baseline, but the high-penalty treatment (designed to induce full compliance) resulted in the lowest average expected earnings due to fines. The shame treatment produced the highest contributions and expected earnings, significantly outperforming both punishment treatments. The paper concludes that social mechanisms (shame) can be more effective and efficient than regulatory punishment in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear PGG with 5 players, 15 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known round count, introducing monetary punishment (with random audits and per-unit fines) increases efficiency relative to baseline, but the effect is limited: high-penalty punishment can reduce efficiency due to fines. The effect of punishment is smaller than that of a social mechanism (random public revelation/shame). For prediction, the paper suggests that punishment will not always increase efficiency above control, especially if fines are high and compliance is imperfect. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, audit probability, punishment cost/tech) are all specified, and the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency under punishment in similar field or framed settings.

- source: Kroll, S., Cherry, T. L., & Shogren, J. F. (2007). Voting, punishment, and public goods. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with five players and repeated rounds, the introduction of a nonbinding voting mechanism alone does not significantly increase efficiency or sustain higher contributions compared to the standard individual contribution baseline. However, when a costly peer punishment mechanism is added—allowing group members to punish those who deviate from the majority-voted contribution plan—both contributions and efficiency increase substantially and persistently. The efficiency in the nonbinding-vote-with-punishment treatment is significantly higher than in the nonbinding-vote treatment and approaches the efficiency observed in the binding-vote (third-party enforced) treatment. The cost of punishment is more than offset by the gains in group payoff, so overall efficiency rises with punishment enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with five players, 10 rounds per stage, and a marginal per-capita return of 0.4, enabling costly peer punishment in a nonbinding voting environment leads to a large and persistent increase in efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The effect is robust and the efficiency gain outweighs the cost of punishment. Thus, when predicting the efficiency of a public goods game with punishment enabled, this paper supports a substantial positive adjustment relative to the control (no-punishment) efficiency, provided the punishment mechanism is similar (costly, peer-administered, and targeted at non-cooperators). The findings are directly relevant for games with similar design parameters.

- source: Peng, H. C. (2022). Punishment mechanisms and cooperation in public goods games: Experimental evidence. *Annals of Public and Cooperative Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab public goods game with four players per group, the introduction of either individual or majority-voting punishment mechanisms increases average contributions relative to the no-punishment baseline, regardless of whether MPCRs are homogeneous or heterogeneous. However, group payoffs (efficiency) do not significantly differ between the no-punishment, individual punishment, and majority-voting punishment conditions. The cost of punishment offsets the gains from increased cooperation. The majority-voting punishment mechanism reduces the frequency of antisocial punishment compared to individual punishment, particularly in homogeneous groups, but antisocial punishment remains present in all conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players, six rounds per condition, and either homogeneous (MPCR=0.5) or heterogeneous (MPCR=0.3/0.5/0.7) returns, enabling punishment (either individual or majority-voting) increases contributions but does not increase efficiency relative to the control. The cost of punishment absorbs the surplus from higher cooperation. Thus, when predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency unless the cost structure or punishment technology is more favorable. The majority-voting mechanism may reduce antisocial punishment but does not improve efficiency over individual punishment. The findings are most applicable to small, anonymous, stranger-matched groups with no communication and known round structure.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Kingsley, D. C. (2019). Endowment heterogeneity, incomplete information & institutional choice in public good experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with three treatments (equal endowments, heterogeneous observed endowments, and heterogeneous unobserved endowments), the introduction of peer punishment increases group payoffs only when endowments are either equal or observed. When endowments are unobserved, peer punishment leads to lower group payoffs than the no-punishment baseline, due to mis-targeted punishment (cooperators being punished, freeriders escaping punishment). Central authority enforcement, which perfectly enforces the social optimum but at a fixed cost, yields similar payoffs across all treatments and is preferred by groups with incomplete information, especially by low-endowment members. The study demonstrates that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on the ability to observe and target deviations from contribution norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of enabling punishment in public goods games depends on whether group members can observe the relevant information (here, endowments) needed to target freeriders. When such information is available, punishment increases efficiency; when it is not, punishment can reduce efficiency below the baseline. The control efficiency (no punishment) is similar across treatments, but the treatment efficiency (with punishment) diverges depending on information structure. For prediction, the presence of incomplete information about contribution capacity (endowments) is a key moderator: with incomplete information, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency; with complete information, it is likely to increase efficiency. Other design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost) are held constant in this study, so the main moderator identified is information about endowments.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Modica, S. (2016). Peer discipline and incentives within groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment, when feasible and not prohibitively costly, enables groups to sustain full contribution to public goods and achieve high efficiency, even in large groups. The model provides conditions under which peer discipline is feasible and optimal, and shows that the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the cost and effectiveness of the punishment and audit technology. The model also shows that, in the absence of peer punishment, only small groups can achieve efficient outcomes, while large groups fail to contribute. The results are robust to various extensions, including renegotiation, spillover costs, and generalized auditing procedures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially as group size increases, provided that punishment and audit costs are not too high. The model gives explicit formulas for group utility as a function of game parameters, including player count, marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), enabling punishment will increase efficiency, particularly in larger groups and when the punishment technology is effective and not too costly. The model also highlights the importance of the cost structure of punishment and auditing, and the quality of monitoring signals, for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Kölle, F. (2015). Heterogeneity and cooperation: The role of capability and valuation on public goods provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This experiment compares three public goods game treatments: homogeneous groups, groups with one high-valuation member (valuation heterogeneity), and groups with one high-capability member (capability heterogeneity). Each group plays 20 rounds, with punishment enabled in rounds 11-20. Efficiency (group payoff as % of social optimum) is measured with and without punishment. In homogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly change efficiency. In valuation-heterogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly increase efficiency and increases inequality. In capability-heterogeneous groups, punishment significantly increases efficiency (from 86% to 93% of optimum) and reduces inequality. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency is thus conditional on the type of heterogeneity, being most effective when group members differ in capability but not in valuation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not uniform but depends on the type of group heterogeneity. If the game is homogeneous or has only capability heterogeneity (where some players' contributions are more productive but all value the public good equally), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially in the capability-heterogeneous case. If the group has valuation heterogeneity (where some players value the public good more), punishment is less effective or even counterproductive for efficiency and increases inequality. The control efficiency (without punishment) is also higher in capability-heterogeneous groups than in valuation-heterogeneous groups. Thus, when using game design dimensions and control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, the type of heterogeneity (capability vs. valuation) is a critical moderator. Other design features (e.g., punishment cost, group size, feedback) are held constant in this study.

- source: Hwang, S. H., & Bowles, S. (2012). Is altruism bad for cooperation?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper models a public goods game with costly punishment, where individuals have both unconditional altruism and reciprocity motives. It finds that increasing unconditional altruism can reduce the willingness to punish free riders, which in turn lowers the threat of punishment and thus reduces equilibrium contributions and group surplus (efficiency) over a substantial range of parameter values. The negative effect of altruism on efficiency is larger when the reciprocity motive is strong or when the fraction of reciprocal altruists in the population is high. For very high levels of altruism, contributions and efficiency rise again, as altruism alone is sufficient to sustain cooperation. The model provides explicit equilibrium expressions for contributions and surplus, and characterizes the parameter ranges where altruism is 'bad for cooperation' and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the interplay between social preferences (altruism, reciprocity) and the punishment mechanism. Specifically, it shows that punishment can increase efficiency, but this effect is weakened or even reversed if group members are highly altruistic, because altruism reduces the willingness to punish defectors. The negative effect is amplified when the reciprocity motive is strong. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment is not monotonic in social preference parameters and may be lower (or even negative) in more altruistic or more reciprocal groups. The model also provides explicit formulas for equilibrium contributions and surplus as a function of game design parameters (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.), which can inform quantitative prediction.

- source: Nikiforakis, N., & Engelmann, D. (2011). Altruistic punishment and the threat of feuds. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This experiment compares three public goods game treatments: (1) Baseline (B), where punishment is possible but cannot be avenged (single punishment stage, random IDs each round); (2) Short Feuds (SF), where multiple punishment stages are possible within a round but IDs are randomized each round (so feuds cannot span rounds); and (3) Long Feuds (LF), where multiple punishment stages are possible and IDs are fixed, so feuds can span rounds. The study finds that as the threat of feuds increases (from B to SF to LF), the frequency of altruistic punishment decreases, and so does the threat of punishment for free-riding. In LF, where feuds are most possible, both cooperation and group earnings decline over time relative to the baseline. Severe punishment is used more in SF to preempt counter-punishment, but in LF, punishment is rare and less targeted at free riders. Overall, the possibility of feuds undermines the efficiency gains from punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on whether punishment can be avenged (i.e., whether feuds are possible). In standard designs where punishment is one-shot and anonymous, punishment can increase or maintain efficiency. However, when punishment can be countered and feuds can develop (especially across rounds), the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is greatly reduced or even reversed, as both cooperation and group earnings decline. For prediction, the presence of counter-punishment opportunities and the ability to identify punishers are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher than with punishment when feuds are possible, due to the breakdown of cooperation and increased costs from feuding.

- source: Huang, S. T., Dong, Y. L., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). THE OPTIMAL INCENTIVE IN PROMOTING COOPERATION: PUNISH THE WORST AND DO NOT ONLY REWARD THE BEST. *Singapore Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical analysis of public goods games with institutional incentives, showing that: (1) Punishing only the lowest contributor (relative punishment with high selectivity) is the most effective way to sustain full cooperation and maximize group welfare for a given punishment budget. (2) Rewarding only the highest contributor is not effective; to promote cooperation, the reward must be distributed such that even lower contributors have a chance to receive it. (3) When both reward and punishment are available, group welfare is maximized by using as much reward as possible and only the minimal punishment needed to sustain full cooperation. (4) The paper gives explicit formulas for the minimum incentive size (punishment and/or reward) required to achieve full cooperation, and shows that, above this threshold, the cooperative Nash equilibrium is globally stable and group efficiency is maximized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It provides explicit theoretical conditions for when punishment (and/or reward) will lead to full cooperation and maximal group welfare, as a function of the number of players, the marginal per-capita return, and the size and targeting of incentives. The results suggest that, for a given game design, introducing punishment targeted at the lowest contributor will sharply increase efficiency if the punishment is just above the critical threshold. The paper also clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment is maximized when the punishment is minimal but sufficient, and that reward should be used as much as possible if available. These findings can be directly mapped to the prediction task, especially for settings where the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the treatment introduces targeted punishment.

- source: Waichman, I., & Stenzel, L. (2019). When Punishment Strikes Late: The Effect of a Delay in Punishment and Punishment Feedback on Cooperation and Efficiency. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment increases both cooperation and efficiency in a linear public good game compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect holds even when punishment is delayed by five periods, as long as participants receive salient feedback linking punishment to their past contributions. If this feedback is absent (i.e., participants are punished after a delay and are not reminded of their past behavior), punishment still increases cooperation but does not improve efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, due to increased punishment costs and less targeted norm enforcement. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency thus depends on the informational environment, specifically whether punished individuals can clearly associate punishment with their own prior actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear PGG with four players and 20 rounds, enabling peer punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, provided that feedback links punishment to past contributions. The effect is robust to a five-period delay in punishment, as long as feedback is salient. If the design omits such feedback, punishment may still increase cooperation but not efficiency, due to higher punishment costs and less effective norm targeting. For prediction, the presence or absence of feedback linking punishment to behavior is a critical moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is lower than treatment efficiency (with punishment and feedback), but not when feedback is absent. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are held constant in this study, so the main moderator evidenced is the feedback/identification of norm violation.

- source: Kingsley, D. C., & Brown, T. C. (2016). Endogenous and costly institutional deterrence in a public good experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with a central authority that can monitor and punish free-riders, increasing the probability of monitoring (deterrence) increases contributions up to a point, but the benefit to group earnings depends on the cost of monitoring. When monitoring is cheap or moderate, higher deterrence increases both contributions and efficiency (group earnings as a share of the social optimum). When monitoring is expensive, the cost can outweigh the benefit, and efficiency does not improve or may decline. Allowing groups to endogenously select the level of monitoring does not increase contributions or efficiency at non-deterrent (low monitoring) levels, contrary to some previous literature. In fact, endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions sometimes reduces contributions. The relationship between monitoring, punishment, and efficiency is thus strongly moderated by the costliness of institutional enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-enabled institutions on efficiency in public goods games, especially when the punishment is implemented via a central authority with costly monitoring. The results show that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on both the probability of monitoring (deterrence) and the cost of monitoring. At low and moderate costs, enabling punishment increases efficiency, but at high costs, the efficiency gain is negated or reversed. The endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions does not provide an efficiency boost. For prediction, the key moderators are the cost of monitoring (punishment_cost), the effectiveness of monitoring (punishment_tech), and whether the institution is deterrent (i.e., the expected cost of free-riding exceeds the benefit). Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with effective, affordable punishment, but high punishment costs can erase the efficiency gain.

- source: Dannenberg, A., & Gallier, C. (2020). The choice of institutions to solve cooperation problems: a survey of experimental research. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The review finds that (1) punishment institutions, when adopted, reliably increase both cooperation and group efficiency/payoff in public goods and related games; (2) the effect is strongest when the institution eliminates free-riding incentives and is not too costly; (3) the popularity and effectiveness of punishment institutions increase with repeated choice and learning; (4) endogenously chosen punishment institutions often yield higher efficiency than exogenously imposed ones, especially for weaker institutions, but the aggregate benefit of endogenous choice is ambiguous because some groups fail to implement the institution; (5) institutional cost and incomplete elimination of free-riding are key barriers to adoption and effectiveness; (6) most studies report both behavioral and payoff outcomes, with efficiency and group payoff being central dependent variables.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides strong evidence that, in public goods game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms generally increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the punishment is effective (eliminates free-riding) and not too costly. The effect is robust across a variety of game designs, player counts, and institutional choice mechanisms. For prediction, the presence of a punishment institution should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, with the magnitude moderated by punishment cost, effectiveness, and whether the institution is adopted. The review also highlights that repeated exposure, information, and learning opportunities increase the likelihood of institution adoption and thus efficiency gains. However, if the institution is costly or does not fully deter free-riding, efficiency gains may be limited or absent. The review's synthesis of many studies with detailed design features makes it highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Chugunova, M., Luhan, W. J., & Nicklisch, A. (2020). When to leave carrots for sticks: On the evolution of sanctioning institutions in open communities. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Participants initially choose reward-based public goods communities, which provide higher payoffs and are preferred by most. Over time, as rewards decline and inequality increases in the reward community, some participants migrate to the punishment community. The punishment community achieves higher contribution rates, but lower average payoffs compared to the reward community. Importantly, when punishment and reward communities compete, the punishment community requires less actual punishment to sustain cooperation, reducing welfare losses typically associated with punishment. The threat of punishment, rather than its frequent use, is sufficient to maintain high contributions. However, the reward community still retains a majority of participants and maintains higher average payoffs, though the difference with the punishment community narrows over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the institutional context. In isolation, punishment communities sustain high contributions but at a welfare cost due to punishment expenditures. When a reward community is available, migration dynamics mean that the punishment community can sustain cooperation with less actual punishment, reducing welfare losses. Thus, the negative efficiency impact of punishment is mitigated when there is institutional competition. For prediction, this implies that the efficiency of punishment-enabled games may be higher when players can choose between institutions, and that the mere threat of punishment can be effective if migration is possible. However, in direct comparison, reward communities yield higher payoffs than punishment communities, though the gap narrows over time.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Malaeb, B., Vlassopoulos, M., & Wahba, J. (2021). Cooperation in a fragmented society: Experimental evidence on Syrian refugees and natives in Lebanon. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Homogeneous groups (same nationality) contribute more to the public good than mixed groups, and this is mainly due to Lebanese participants reducing contributions in mixed groups. (2) Punishment is used more in homogeneous groups, and there is substantial antisocial punishment, especially among Lebanese. (3) The opportunity to punish does not increase contributions overall; in fact, average contributions decrease in the punishment stage for Syrians and do not change significantly for Lebanese or mixed groups. (4) Average earnings after the contribution stage are higher in homogeneous groups, but total earnings (including punishment costs) are not significantly different between treatments, as mixed groups assign less punishment and thus incur lower punishment costs. (5) The presence of punishment does not remedy the lower cooperation in mixed groups. (6) Behavior in mixed groups is more conditionally cooperative, while homogeneous groups show more unconditional cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment in a two-player repeated linear public goods game with a 0.75 MPCR and a 1:3 punishment cost-to-impact ratio. The main finding is that the introduction of punishment does not increase efficiency or average earnings; in fact, contributions do not rise and may even fall, especially due to antisocial punishment. While punishment increases costs (especially in homogeneous groups), it does not lead to higher total payoffs. Thus, for similar game designs (small groups, moderate MPCR, punishment with 1:3 cost-impact, no communication), one should not expect punishment to increase efficiency, especially in settings with potential for antisocial punishment. The paper also highlights that group composition (in-group vs. out-group) can moderate both cooperation and punishment, but the overall effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative in this context.

- source: Tan, F. F. (2008). Punishment in a Linear Public Good Game with Productivity Heterogeneity. *Economist-netherlands*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a four-person linear public good game, groups with heterogeneous productivity (MPCRs 0.9 and 0.3, average 0.6) contribute less than homogeneous groups (all MPCR 0.6) when punishment is not allowed. Introducing a costly punishment mechanism (punisher pays 1, target loses 2 per point) in heterogeneous groups significantly increases average contributions to levels similar to homogeneous groups without punishment. However, the cost of punishment means that average group earnings (welfare) are not higher with punishment: over 15 rounds, average earnings are similar across all treatments, and in early periods, punishment reduces earnings. In the final periods, punishment helps maintain earnings by countering free-riding. Punishment is mainly assigned by high-productivity agents to low-productivity agents in successful groups. High-productivity agents are more responsive to punishment when their contributions are below average.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a linear PGG with productivity heterogeneity, enabling costly punishment increases average contributions but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, due to the cost of punishment. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to heterogeneity, enabling punishment will likely increase contributions, but the net efficiency gain may be small or absent unless punishment is cheap or highly effective. The effect of punishment on efficiency is time-dependent: in later rounds, punishment can prevent the collapse of cooperation and thus improve earnings relative to no-punishment, but over the whole game, the cost of punishment can offset these gains. The findings are most relevant for games with 4 players, 15 rounds, no communication, continuous contributions, and a punishment technology of 1:2 cost:impact.

- source: Bühren, C., Dannenberg, A., & Händel, P. (2025). The demand for complete and incomplete punishment institutions to promote cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with endogenous, costless, formal punishment institutions, complete punishment institutions (where all group members are covered) reliably lead to the implementation of deterrent punishment when cooperation is profitable, resulting in high contributions and, by implication, high efficiency. In contrast, incomplete punishment institutions (where only a subgroup is covered, or where subgroups can independently implement punishment) lead to less consistent implementation of deterrent punishment, lower average punishment rates, and lower contributions, even when cooperation is profitable. The marginal group return (MGR) is a strong predictor of punishment demand and contributions in complete institutions, but much less so in incomplete institutions, where individual cooperativeness and subgroup coordination become more important. Coordination failures between subgroups often prevent the realization of efficient outcomes in incomplete institutions.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment institution (complete vs. incomplete) is a critical moderator. When punishment is costless and covers all players, enabling punishment leads to near-maximal efficiency if cooperation is profitable. When punishment is incomplete (applies only to a subgroup or is implemented separately by subgroups), enabling punishment leads to less reliable efficiency gains, with outcomes depending more on group composition and coordination. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a strong predictor of efficiency gains from punishment in complete institutions, but much weaker in incomplete ones. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the design implies low efficiency in the absence of punishment when cooperation is not profitable. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence of a complete, costless, formal punishment institution is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, while incomplete institutions introduce substantial uncertainty and lower average efficiency.

- source: Kumakawa, T. (2013). Social Rewards and Sanctions: A Voluntary Contribution Mechanism Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a two-player public goods game, the opportunity to send social (free-form) or monetary rewards/sanctions does not increase first-round contributions compared to a baseline with no such opportunity. However, when participants actually experience social sanctions (i.e., receive a negative message from their partner), their contributions in the next round increase significantly. Social rewards (positive messages) do not have a significant effect. Monetary sanctions and rewards are rarely used and do not significantly affect subsequent contributions. The study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player public goods game with a game-theoretic payoff table and no prior knowledge of a second round, social sanctions (blame via free-form messages) can increase subsequent contributions, but only after they are actually experienced. The mere opportunity for sanctions or rewards does not affect cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral (contribution) responses to social sanctions, not efficiency outcomes. The design (two players, two rounds, no chat except for the sanction/reward stage, full information about partner's contribution, and a linear MPCR of 0.7) should be considered when generalizing to other settings.

- source: Krieg, J., & Samek, A. (2017). When charities compete: A laboratory experiment with simultaneous public goods. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that monetary incentives (bonus) increase contributions to the treated public good and, in early rounds, to the untreated public good, resulting in a higher total sum of contributions (i.e., an expanded 'charitable pie'). Recognition and non-monetary sanctions do not significantly increase contributions in the treated game, and recognition leads to a decrease in contributions to the untreated game in later rounds, consistent with expenditure substitution. Sanctions are actively used but do not increase contributions. The findings suggest that monetary incentives are more effective than social incentives in increasing total contributions when multiple public goods are present. However, the study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, so implications for efficiency must be inferred from contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of non-monetary sanctions (costless approval/disapproval) in a simultaneous public goods environment. In this design, punishment is costless and does not affect payoffs, and it does not increase contributions or the sum of contributions. Thus, in similar environments (simultaneous PGGs, costless non-monetary punishment, no chat, n=4, 20 rounds, MPCR=0.4), enabling this form of punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff. The paper does not test costly punishment, so its results are most relevant for costless, non-monetary sanctions. The findings also suggest that the presence of alternative opportunities for prosocial behavior (multiple simultaneous PGGs) may reduce the effectiveness of social incentives. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that costless, non-monetary punishment will not improve efficiency in simultaneous PGGs with these parameters.

- source: Choi, J. K., & Ahn, T. K. (2013). Strategic reward and altruistic punishment support cooperation in a public goods game experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab public goods game with 3 players, 10 rounds per phase, and a marginal per-capita return (MPCR) of 0.5, both voluntary punishment and voluntary reward options significantly increase average group contributions compared to baseline. In the partner condition (fixed groups), average contributions rise from 8.17 (punishment baseline) to 12.51 (punishment with punishment enabled), and from 9.38 (reward baseline) to 12.31 (reward with reward enabled). In the stranger condition (random rematching), punishment sustains higher contributions (11.38) than reward (9.21). The effect of punishment on cooperation is robust to group reshuffling, while reward is much less effective when strategic reciprocity is not possible. The paper does not directly report efficiency, but the increase in group contribution implies higher group payoff and thus higher efficiency when punishment is enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 3 players, 10 rounds, no communication, and MPCR=0.5, enabling voluntary punishment increases group contribution (and thus group payoff/efficiency) compared to a baseline with no punishment. The effect is robust to random rematching (stranger condition), where punishment remains effective but reward does not. The paper does not report efficiency as a percentage of the social optimum, but the group contribution data can be used to estimate efficiency. For prediction, the findings suggest that, under these design parameters, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially when group membership is not fixed. The absence of direct efficiency reporting is a limitation, but the contribution data is a strong proxy.

- source: Coutts, A. (2024). The age of consequences: Unraveling conflict's impact on social preferences, norm enforcement, and risk-taking. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that exposure to conflict (via radio reception of hate messages during the Rwandan genocide) is associated with higher contributions in public goods games, but this effect is entirely driven by individuals who were children during the genocide. In games with punishment and reward options, conflict exposure increases the likelihood of punishing and rewarding others, but this effect is driven by those who were older during the genocide. Risk-taking, as measured by contributions in a risky public goods game and by survey, is higher for all age groups exposed to conflict. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. However, it does show that the presence of punishment increases contributions relative to baseline, and that conflict exposure increases the propensity to punish.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment and reward options affect contribution behavior in public goods games, and how these effects are moderated by past conflict exposure and age at exposure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment increases contributions (relative to baseline), and that the propensity to punish is higher among those exposed to conflict as adults. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation, but does not quantify the effect on group payoff or efficiency. The detailed game design information and behavioral results are useful for understanding the context and potential moderators.

- source: Joffily, M., Masclet, D., Noussair, C. N., & Villeval, M. C. (2014). Emotions, Sanctions, and Cooperation. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that allowing costly punishment in a repeated public goods game leads to a dramatic increase in average contributions, with groups converging to near-full cooperation. Emotional responses (measured by SCR and self-reported valence) are closely linked to both cooperation and punishment: positive emotions are associated with higher contributions, and negative emotions in response to free riding predict punishment behavior. Receiving punishment leads to negative emotions and increased subsequent contributions by free riders. The study provides strong evidence that punishment opportunities can sustain high cooperation, but the main outcomes are emotional and behavioral (contribution rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with four players, 20 rounds, and a punishment stage with convex costs and anonymous assignment, the introduction of punishment opportunities leads to a large increase in average contributions (from about 9.3 to 19.2 out of 20). While efficiency is not directly reported, the near-maximal contributions in the punishment condition imply that group efficiency approaches the social optimum. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, known MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no rewards, anonymous punishment, full feedback) are all clearly specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG designs. However, the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff reporting is a limitation for precise quantitative prediction.

- source: Bortolotti, S., Casari, M., & Pancotto, F. (2015). NORMS OF PUNISHMENT: EXPERIMENTS WITH STUDENTS AND THE GENERAL POPULATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The opportunity for peer punishment increases cooperation among students but not among the general population. In the student sample, average contributions rise substantially when punishment is available (from 6.8 to 12.8 out of 20), while in the representative sample, contributions are slightly lower with punishment (from 9.1 to 8.9). The general population punishes as much or more than students, but punishment is more often directed at cooperators (antisocial punishment), and recipients are less responsive to punishment. As a result, punishment fails to increase cooperation or efficiency in the general population. Earnings per period decrease with punishment in both groups, but more so in the representative sample. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency is highly dependent on the subject pool, with punishment only improving cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGGs depends critically on the subject pool. In a standard 4-player, 8-round PGG with MPCR=0.5 and a punishment technology of 1 token cost to reduce 4 tokens from another, punishment increases cooperation (and likely efficiency) among students but not among a representative general population. The main moderator is the prevalence of antisocial punishment and the responsiveness to punishment. For prediction, if the game is run with a student sample, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency; with a general population sample, punishment may not improve and could even reduce efficiency due to antisocial punishment and lack of response. The paper does not report efficiency directly, but the pattern of contributions and earnings suggests these conclusions.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2021). Cooperation between newcomers and incumbents: The role of normative disagreements. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Normative disagreement between newcomers and incumbents in a public goods game with peer punishment does not reduce average contributions to the public good. While group identification and the emergence of a shared social norm are negatively affected by normative disagreement, cooperation (as measured by contribution rate) is maintained. Punishment is used as a norm enforcement tool, especially targeting low-contributing newcomers, who subsequently increase their contributions to match incumbent standards. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a 3-player repeated public goods game with peer punishment, normative disagreement does not reduce average contributions, and punishment is effective at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and findings are relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can inform the mapping from design to expected contribution rates, but not to efficiency.

- source: Hoeft, L., & Mill, W. (2024). Abuse of power An experimental investigation of the effects of power and transparency on centralized punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated public goods game with a single designated punisher, punishers contribute significantly less than non-punishers, and the gap widens over time. Punishers enforce contribution norms on others that they do not themselves follow. These results are robust to information conditions (private vs. public knowledge of contributions). The paper does not report on group efficiency, total group payoff, or welfare, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the behavioral effects of punishment in a public goods game, specifically that designated punishers may enforce cooperation while themselves contributing less. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of a designated punisher can stabilize higher contributions among non-punishers, but the punisher's own low contributions may offset some of the efficiency gains. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper signals that the structure of punishment (e.g., designated vs. peer) and the behavior of punishers are important, but quantitative efficiency effects cannot be extracted from this study.

- source: Noussair, C. N., Tucker, S., Xu, Y. L., & Breaban, A. (2024). The role of emotions in public goods games with and without punishment opportunities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that both monetary and non-monetary punishment increase cooperation relative to a baseline with no punishment, with monetary punishment sustaining higher contributions over time. Emotional states, especially happiness and negative valence, are correlated with contribution and punishment behavior, but not directly with payoffs. The effect of punishment on future contributions is stronger for monetary punishment, and the emotional state of the recipient moderates the effect of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group earnings, but the behavioral patterns are consistent with prior literature showing that punishment increases group welfare by increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with four players and 10 rounds, both monetary and non-monetary punishment increase cooperation compared to a no-punishment baseline. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to directly predict the change in efficiency from enabling punishment. The findings support the general expectation that punishment increases efficiency via increased cooperation, but for quantitative prediction, one must rely on prior studies that report payoff or efficiency outcomes. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are clearly specified and match canonical PGG lab experiments.

- source: Ramalingam, A., & Stoddard, B. (2024). Does reducing inequality increase cooperation?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implements a 2x2 design varying punishment (present/absent) and instruction format (short/long, less/more explicit). In the punishment treatments, longer and more explicit instructions lead to higher and more stable contributions, with punishment more effectively targeted at low contributors. In the absence of punishment, instruction format has little effect on average contributions over time. Decision times are lower with more explicit instructions, indicating better comprehension. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined, but the results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in raising contributions depends on subjects' comprehension of the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in raising contributions in public goods games depends on participants' comprehension of the incentive structure, which can be improved by more explicit instructions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the realized effect of punishment on efficiency may be moderated by factors affecting comprehension, such as instruction clarity. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency changes due to punishment. Instead, it provides indirect support that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when comprehension is high and punishment is well-targeted.

- source: Engel, C., Kube, S., & Kurschilgen, M. (2021). Managing expectations: How selective information affects cooperation and punishment in social dilemma games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Selective pre-play information about others' cooperativeness can strongly affect initial beliefs, contribution behavior, and the effectiveness of punishment in repeated public goods games with punishment and counter-punishment. Unfavorable information (showing low cooperation in other groups) leads to lower initial and sustained contributions, and reduces the ability of punishment to increase cooperation. Favorable information has little effect in already cooperative environments (ceiling effect), but in less cooperative environments it can prevent further decline in cooperation and enhance the positive effect of pro-social punishment. The effect of information does not depend on whether it is public or private, but is strongest when the information is surprising to participants. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the strong effects on contribution rates imply likely effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation (and thus likely efficiency) is moderated by participants' expectations, which can be manipulated by selective pre-play information. In environments where baseline cooperation is high, punishment is effective unless unfavorable information is provided, which undermines both cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment. In less cooperative environments, favorable information can help maintain cooperation and enhance the effect of pro-social punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the baseline expectations and informational context (not just structural game parameters) are important moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.
