However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, predictions must be inferred from contribution data.

- source: Kocher, M., Martinsson, P., & Visser, M. (2012). Social background, cooperative behavior, and norm enforcement. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper reports on one-shot public goods games with and without punishment, conducted in four South African high schools representing different socio-economic and cultural backgrounds. The introduction of punishment increased contributions significantly only in the white community, and only modestly or insignificantly in the others. Punishment was more frequent and severe in the African communities, leading to lower average earnings in the punishment condition for those groups. The study finds that social background variables (such as trust and household violence) explain much of the variation in cooperation and punishment. Overall, the efficiency impact of punishment is mixed: in some groups, punishment increases contributions but also reduces earnings due to the cost of punishment, sometimes resulting in no net efficiency gain.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency in one-shot, three-player public goods games with MPCR=0.5 and a punishment technology of 1:3 (cost:impact) is highly context-dependent. In groups with low baseline contributions, punishment may increase contributions and potentially efficiency, but in groups with already high contributions, punishment may not increase cooperation and can reduce efficiency due to punishment costs. The presence of anti-social punishment (punishing high contributors) can further reduce efficiency. Control efficiency (without punishment) is a strong moderator: if baseline contributions are high, punishment may not improve and can even worsen efficiency. Social background and group norms are important moderators, but are not directly encoded in the game design dimensions. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will reliably increase efficiency, especially in one-shot or low-trust settings.

- source: Engel, C., & Kurschilgen, M. (2013). The Coevolution of Behavior and Normative Expectations: An Experiment. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) both norm elicitation and legal framing increase cooperation in a repeated public goods game, but do not differ significantly in the absence of punishment; (2) introducing a costly peer punishment option (1:1 cost-to-impact) increases cooperation and reduces free-riding, but the effect is strongest when the norm is embedded in a legal frame; (3) in the presence of punishment, the legal frame prevents the crowding out of intrinsic motivation and leads to higher contributions and more stable norm perceptions; (4) average earnings are higher in the Law-S (legal frame with sanctions) treatment than in other sanctioning treatments, indicating that punishment can improve group welfare when combined with legal framing. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings per treatment are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 30 rounds, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio, enabling punishment increases group earnings and cooperation, especially when the norm is legally framed. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and most pronounced when combined with a legal frame. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than in the punishment-enabled treatments, particularly Law-S. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on the presence of legal/normative framing. The results are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no chat, full information about others' contributions, and a 1:1 punishment cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Hopfensitz, A., & Miquel-Florensa, J. (2017). Mill ownership and farmer's cooperative behavior: the case of Costa Rica coffee farmers. *Journal of Institutional Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a public good game, Costa Rican coffee farmers selling to cooperatives do not contribute more than those selling to private mills when paired with their own group. However, cooperative farmers contribute significantly less when paired with private mill farmers, while private mill farmers' contributions are unaffected by partner background. When a punishment mechanism (penalizing lowest contributors, with partial redistribution to the group) is available, contributions by cooperative farmers increase substantially, especially when paired with private mill farmers (over 100% increase in contribution). The effect of punishment on private mill farmers is negligible. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the structure of the game allows inference that group welfare would increase with higher contributions under punishment, especially for cooperative farmers in mixed groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a public good game can substantially increase contributions, especially among cooperative farmers interacting with out-group partners. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the increase in contributions. The punishment mechanism is costly (1 unit per participant), and only a fraction of the fine is redistributed, so efficiency gains may be less than the increase in contributions. The design is a one-shot or single-decision public good game with 4 players, MPCR 0.375, and a punishment mechanism that is not peer-to-peer but an impartial rule penalizing the lowest contributor. The findings are most informative for predicting contribution changes (and thus likely efficiency changes) when punishment is enabled in similar settings, especially for groups with strong in-group/out-group distinctions. The lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation for precise prediction.

- source: Hopp, D., & Süss, K. (2024). How altruistic is indirect reciprocity? - Evidence from gift-exchange games in the lab. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that when a single group member is given punishment power in a repeated public goods game, most punishers abuse their power by punishing others for contributing more than themselves. Abuse is highest when punishment power is high and transparency is low. High transparency reduces abuse only in the high-power condition. Contribution rates of non-punishers increase over time, especially when transparency is high, but punishers consistently contribute less than non-punishers except in the high-power, high-transparency condition. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how centralized punishment power and transparency affect behavioral outcomes like abuse of power and contribution norms in public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that centralized punishment can lead to norm enforcement but also to widespread abuse, especially when power is high and transparency is low. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it shows that punishment can increase contributions (especially among non-punishers), but also that punishers may undercontribute, potentially reducing group efficiency. The interaction between power and transparency is important for behavioral outcomes, but the net effect on efficiency remains unreported.

- source: Bruttel, L., & Friehe, T. (2014). Can short-term incentives induce long-lasting cooperation? Results from a public-goods experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment used a repeated linear public goods game with punishment, fixed groups of four, and 20 rounds. Treatments manipulated the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and punishment cost for the first 10 rounds, then reverted to a common, less cooperative-friendly setting for rounds 11-20. High mpcr or low punishment cost increased contributions in rounds 1-10, but after reverting to baseline parameters, contributions in these groups declined rapidly, often below the control. The baseline (constant, moderate mpcr and punishment cost) showed stable contributions. The study finds no evidence that temporary strong incentives create persistent cooperation; instead, their removal can backfire. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with punishment, temporary improvements in incentive structure (either via higher mpcr or lower punishment cost) do not produce lasting increases in cooperation once incentives revert to baseline. For the prediction task, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment (or from temporarily improving punishment effectiveness or mpcr) is not persistent if the incentive is not maintained. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect and must be inferred from contribution patterns. The results caution against expecting lasting efficiency improvements from short-term incentive changes.

- source: Weber, T. O., Schulz, J. F., Beranek, B., Lambarraa-Lehnhardt, F., & Gächter, S. (2023). The behavioral mechanisms of voluntary cooperation across culturally diverse societies: Evidence from the US, the UK, Morocco, and Turkey. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that differences in cooperation across culturally diverse societies are mainly explained by differences in beliefs about others' cooperativeness, not by differences in cooperative preferences or punishment behavior. In the presence of punishment, only participants in the US and UK significantly increase their contributions compared to the no-punishment condition. Actual punishment of free riding is similar across all four countries, and antisocial punishment is low everywhere. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment affects contribution rates in one-shot public goods games across different societies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive in settings similar to the US and UK (where contributions increase with punishment), but negligible in settings similar to Morocco and Turkey (where contributions do not increase). However, because efficiency is not directly measured, this inference is indirect and based on contribution behavior. The game design dimensions are well specified, allowing for mapping to prediction inputs, but the lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation.

- source: Kirchkamp, O., & Mill, W. (2020). Conditional cooperation and the effect of punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: a baseline public goods game (no punishment), a treatment where the conditional decision maker (CDM) can be punished by an unconditional decision maker (UCPun), and a treatment where the CDM can punish an unconditional decision maker (CPun). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (i.e., more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), and actually increases the slope of conditional cooperation due to a responsibility effect. There is evidence for substitution between punishment and cooperation in punishment behavior, but not in contribution behavior. The overall effect of punishment is to increase conditional cooperation, but with some increase in free riding. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases the slope of conditional cooperation, but also increases the number of free riders. The net effect on cooperation is positive, but the effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, but may have ambiguous effects on efficiency if the increase in free riding offsets the gains from stronger conditional cooperation. The design features—such as costless punishment, no communication, and a one-shot game—should be considered when generalizing to other settings. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, J., Riyanto, Y. E., & Zhang, R. K. (2020). Firing the right bullets: Exploring the effectiveness of the hired-gun mechanism in the provision of public goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The hired-gun mechanism, a centralized punishment system targeting the lowest contributor(s), is highly effective at increasing contributions in public goods games when both unilateral and tie punishments are positive. The effect is robust to increasing the choice set (larger endowment). However, if the unilateral punishment is removed (set to zero), contributions decay over time, and the mechanism fails to sustain high cooperation, even if the tie punishment remains. Theoretical predictions suggest a range of punishment parameters should work, but empirically, zero unilateral punishment is insufficient. The main outcome is contribution rate, not direct efficiency or group payoff, but high contributions imply high efficiency in this context. The study also finds that punishment can be softened (reduced in size) to a degree without loss of effectiveness, but not eliminated.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks relating to efficiency in public goods games with centralized punishment, this paper provides strong evidence that the hired-gun mechanism (centralized, automatic punishment of the lowest contributor) is highly effective at increasing contributions (and thus likely efficiency) when both unilateral and tie punishments are positive. The effect is robust to larger choice sets and group size of 4. However, if the unilateral punishment is zero, the mechanism is ineffective, and contributions decay. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the near-full-contribution rates in the treatment with punishment strongly suggest near-maximal efficiency. Thus, when mapping from game design to expected efficiency, the presence and size of both punishment components are critical moderators. The absence of unilateral punishment is a strong negative moderator. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with centralized, automatic punishment and group size 4, with full information about others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Visser, M., & Burns, J. (2015). Inequality, social sanctions and cooperation within South African fishing communities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contribution rates in both equal and unequal endowment groups, with a larger effect in unequal groups. Low endowment players contribute a higher share of their endowment than high endowment players. Punishment is used less in unequal groups, but is more effective at raising contributions. Demand for punishment does not differ significantly between high and low endowment players, despite higher relative costs for low endowment players. Punishment is mainly targeted at free-riders, with antisocial punishment more common in equal groups. Socio-economic status and social attitudes (e.g., distrust in formal institutions) influence punishment behavior. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment increases contribution rates in linear public goods games, especially in groups with unequal endowments. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the findings about contribution rates and punishment behavior are informative for predicting behavioral responses to punishment. For efficiency prediction, the paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase efficiency in similar settings, especially in unequal groups, but the magnitude cannot be inferred. The absence of payoff/efficiency data is a key limitation for direct prediction.

- source: Ones, U., & Putterman, L. (2007). The ecology of collective action: A public goods and sanctions experiment with controlled group formation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment confirms that allowing punishment in a public goods game sustains high contribution levels over time, in contrast to the typical decay seen in standard VCMs without punishment. Punishment is mostly directed at low contributors, but a significant fraction is 'perverse' (aimed at high contributors), which can reduce efficiency. Grouping high contributors and strong punishers together leads to higher average contributions, while groups with perverse punishers contribute less. Individual tendencies to contribute and punish are persistent. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total earnings, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not quantified.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment sustains high contributions in repeated public goods games, and that group composition (especially the presence or absence of perverse punishers) is a key moderator. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are most useful for predicting contribution rates rather than efficiency per se. The qualitative discussion suggests that punishment can increase efficiency when perverse punishment is minimized, but can reduce efficiency when perverse punishment is common. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment generally increases cooperation, but the efficiency effect depends on the nature of punishment (targeted vs. perverse) and group composition.

- source: Hoeft, L., & Mill, W. (2017). An experimental investigation of designated punishment behavior in public goods. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated public goods game with a single designated punisher, the punisher contributes significantly less than non-punishers but enforces higher contributions from others through punishment. This double standard grows over time. The results are robust to a full-information treatment where all contributions are public. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the presence of a designated punisher increases, decreases, or leaves unchanged the overall efficiency of the group.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral effects of designated punishment in public goods games, specifically that punishers can enforce higher contributions from others while themselves contributing less. However, because the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, information structure) are well specified and may be useful for matching to other studies, but the lack of efficiency or payoff data limits the direct predictive value for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Kocher, M. G., Martinsson, P., & Visser, M. (2008). Does stake size matter for cooperation and punishment?. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the monetary stakes in a one-shot public goods game, both with and without punishment, does not significantly affect the average contribution rate or the frequency and intensity of punishment. The presence of a punishment stage increases average contributions compared to the no-punishment condition, but this effect is not moderated by stake size. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the design of public goods games with and without punishment, including group size, MPCR, punishment cost and technology, and the absence of communication. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does confirm that, in this design, punishment increases average contributions, and that this effect is robust to stake size. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment increases cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Romaniuc, R., Farrow, K., Ibanez, L., & Marciano, A. (2016). The perils of government enforcement. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that peer disapproval (private enforcement) increases contributions to the public good compared to baseline. Adding a mild, centralized monetary punishment (government enforcement) to peer disapproval stabilizes contributions but does not significantly increase average contributions compared to peer disapproval alone. Critically, if government enforcement is removed after being in place, the effectiveness of peer disapproval is undermined: low contributors become unresponsive to disapproval, and average contributions drop significantly below the level observed when only private enforcement was ever present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results on contributions imply that punishment can have both stabilizing and crowding-out effects depending on the sequence of enforcement mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the sequence and combination of enforcement mechanisms (private and government) can have important effects on cooperation (as measured by contributions) in public goods games. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings suggest that enabling punishment can stabilize or increase contributions, but that removing punishment after it has been in place can lead to a sharp decline in cooperation, even below the level observed with private enforcement alone. For prediction tasks, this implies that the history of enforcement mechanisms and their removal are important moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Krawczyk, M., & Szczygielski, K. (2019). Do professions curb free-riding? An experiment. *European Journal of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing peer punishment in a standard linear PGG leads to typical patterns: punishment is directed at low contributors and can increase subsequent contributions. However, the main treatment—group homogeneity—does not significantly affect investment or punishment. Homogeneous groups (same profession) do not cooperate more; if anything, they may cooperate less. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, in this design, punishment works similarly regardless of group composition, and that group homogeneity does not enhance cooperation when punishment is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a clear example of a standard PGG with punishment, but does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it confirms that punishment is implemented in a typical way (costly to punisher, more costly to target, anonymous, with summary feedback), and that the presence of punishment supports cooperation (as in prior literature). The lack of efficiency data limits its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes, but the behavioral results are consistent with the literature showing that punishment can sustain cooperation. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, etc.) are well specified and can inform parameter mapping for prediction tasks.

- source: Bicskei, M., Lankau, M., & Bizer, K. (2016). Negative reciprocity and its relation to anger-like emotions in identity-homogeneous and -heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when peer punishment is available in a one-shot PGG, the likelihood and intensity of punishment for low contributions is significantly lower in identity-homogeneous (in-group) groups than in identity-heterogeneous (out-group) or random groups. Anger-like emotions (anger, contempt, irritation) are a strong driver of punishment, but their effect is moderated by group identity: they lead to more punishment in out-groups than in-groups. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but argues that higher punishment in out-groups, being costly, likely reduces social welfare relative to in-groups, where punishment is less frequent and less intense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on behavior in PGGs is moderated by group identity: punishment is less frequent and less intense in in-groups, and anger-like emotions drive punishment more in out-groups. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that in settings with identity-heterogeneous groups, punishment is more likely to be used, which (given the costliness of punishment) may reduce efficiency relative to in-groups. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Engel, C., Beckenkamp, M., Glöckner, A., Irlenbusch, B., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Kube, S., Kurschilgen, M., Morell, A., Nicklisch, A., Normann, H. T., & Towfigh, E. (2014). First impressions are more important than early intervention: Qualifying broken windows theory in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with and without punishment, the authors find that first impressions (i.e., the initial level of cooperation in the group) have a strong and lasting effect on subsequent contributions. The opportunity to punish freeriders (costly punishment) stabilizes cooperation and slows the decay of contributions over time, compared to games without punishment. However, the effect of early punishment (punishing in the first round) is small and fades over time, especially when punished players can retaliate. The main driver of sustained cooperation is good first impressions, with punishment playing a secondary, stabilizing role. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoffs, but higher contributions in the punishment condition imply higher efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard linear public goods games, enabling punishment increases and stabilizes contributions compared to no-punishment baselines, especially when initial group behavior is cooperative. While the paper does not report efficiency directly, the close link between contribution rate and efficiency in this design means the results are highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes. The effect of punishment is positive but depends on initial conditions; early punishment per se is less important than the overall opportunity for punishment. The findings are most applicable to 4-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGGs with standard punishment technology and no communication.

- source: Morgan, S. N., Mason, N. M., & Shupp, R. S. (2019). The effects of voice with(out) punishment: Public goods provision and rule compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Comments alone do not significantly increase contributions or compliance with the MCR compared to control. (2) Enforcement (punishment) alone increases contributions and compliance, but the effect is significantly larger when comments are combined with enforcement. (3) The comment with enforcement treatment leads to the highest and most sustained contributions and compliance. (4) Rule-makers exposed to comments set higher MCRs, which in turn increases contributions. (5) The effect of comments on contributions is partly mediated by the higher MCRs set by rule-makers. (6) The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the increased contributions and compliance in the comment with enforcement treatment imply higher efficiency in that condition.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that in a linear public goods game with a probabilistic punishment mechanism, adding a comment-based participation mechanism (private, one-way comments to a rule-maker who sets a minimum contribution rule) increases contributions and compliance above punishment alone. While efficiency is not directly measured, the structure of the game (MPCR = 0.4, group size 5, continuous contributions, no chat, no rewards) means that higher contributions and compliance will translate into higher efficiency. The effect of punishment alone is positive but smaller than the effect of punishment plus comments. The paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency, so predictions should be based on the observed contribution and compliance patterns. The findings are most relevant for games with third-party rule-making, probabilistic exogenous punishment, and no peer-to-peer punishment or reward.

- source: Carpenter, J., Bowles, S., Gintis, H., & Hwang, S. H. (2009). Strong reciprocity and team production: Theory and evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing costly punishment in a public goods game prevents the typical decay in contributions over time, with average contributions rising or remaining stable across rounds. Punishment is widely used and is targeted at those who contribute less. The amount of punishment assigned increases with the degree of shirking and the harm imposed by shirking (i.e., higher MPCR), and decreases with group size. Individuals with higher reciprocity are more likely to punish, while those with higher unconditional altruism punish less. Punishment leads to increased contributions by shirkers, but the direct material return to the punisher is less than the cost incurred. The paper does not directly report group efficiency or total earnings, but the sustained high contributions suggest improved group welfare relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly peer punishment in public goods games increases and sustains cooperation (contribution rates) compared to the standard game without punishment. The effect is robust across different group sizes (4 and 8) and MPCRs (0.3 and 0.7), but the magnitude of punishment and contributions is sensitive to these parameters: punishment and contributions decrease as group size increases, and increase as MPCR increases. While the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, the sustained high contributions in the punishment condition imply higher efficiency than in the control. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially in smaller groups and with higher MPCR, but the effect is likely attenuated as group size increases.

- source: Farrow, K., & Romaniuc, R. (2019). The stickiness of norms. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both peer disapproval and face-saving mechanisms increase contributions relative to baseline. When a temporary monetary sanction is introduced and then removed, peer disapproval groups experience a significant drop in cooperation (contributions fall below baseline), indicating a negative post-intervention spillover. In contrast, face-saving groups maintain or even increase cooperation after the sanction is removed, showing no negative spillover. The results suggest that the type of informal norm enforcement moderates the legacy of formal sanctions, with face-saving mechanisms supporting more robust cooperation after sanctions end.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the type of informal norm enforcement present. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the contribution data imply that punishment can have negative, neutral, or positive legacy effects depending on whether peer disapproval or face-saving is salient. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and type of informal norm enforcement (especially observability/social image vs. anonymous peer disapproval) are important moderators of punishment's effect. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be interpreted as applying primarily to contribution rates.

- source: Cheung, S. L. (2014). New insights into conditional cooperation and punishment from a strategy method experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both conditional cooperation and punishment in a public goods game are sensitive to the full distribution of others' contributions, not just the average. When punishment is available, more subjects contribute and a substantial fraction are willing to punish, with punishment increasing as the target's contribution falls below the punisher's and as the third player's contribution rises. However, the study does not directly report or analyze group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings as a function of punishment availability. The main findings are about the behavioral patterns of contribution and punishment, and the heterogeneity of types (conditional cooperators, free-riders, others).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment affects contribution and punishment behavior in a one-shot, three-player public goods game with a strategy method. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does support the qualitative expectation that punishment increases contributions and that the effect depends on the distribution of others' contributions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about mechanisms and behavioral responses, but not about the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Guilfoos, T., & Suter, J. F. (2025). Time pressure and motivated delay effects on sanctioning in a social dilemma: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that cognitive manipulations (time pressure and motivated delay) do not affect the probability that groups vote to adopt sanctions in a public goods game. However, motivated delay reduces average contributions to the public good, and when sanctions are adopted, they are more effective at increasing contributions in the motivated delay condition. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results. The findings suggest that while cognitive mode does not affect rule adoption, it can affect the behavioral response to sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a linear PGG with endogenous voting on a fixed-cost, non-deterrent sanction, the adoption of sanctions is not affected by cognitive manipulations (time pressure or motivated delay). While the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly reported, the results suggest that sanctions can increase contributions, especially under motivated delay, but the cost of sanctions may offset these gains. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that the presence of a costly, group-level sanction can increase contributions, but efficiency effects depend on whether increased contributions are sufficient to cover sanction costs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.

- source: Kamei, K. (2014). Conditional punishment. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that individuals' punishment of non-cooperators increases with the amount of punishment assigned by other group members. Most subjects are 'free-riders' who do not punish, but about 23% are conditional punishers who increase punishment as others do. There is also a distinction between pro-social and anti-social conditional punishers. The study provides evidence that conditional punishment types are heterogeneous and that these types can predict unconditional punishment behavior. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed behavioral evidence on the heterogeneity and conditionality of punishment in a standard PGG with punishment, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and types of punishment behavior that may underlie efficiency effects in similar games, but cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that the presence of conditional punishers may amplify punishment effects when others also punish, but the net effect on efficiency is not measured.

- source: Billinger, S., & Rosenbaum, S. M. (2023). On the limits of hierarchy in public goods games: A survey and meta-analysis on the effects of design variables on cooperation. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that, among various design variables in hierarchical PGGs, only punishment reliably sustains higher contribution rates over time compared to baseline. The effect size for punishment is notably higher (d=0.75) than for other mechanisms. Rewards, monitoring, and other variables have positive but short-lived effects. The effect of punishment is stronger when it is free for the punisher than when it is costly. Group stability (partner matching), monitoring, and asymmetric information also increase cooperation, but not as durably as punishment. The analysis is based on contribution rates, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in hierarchical PGGs increases and sustains contribution rates, which, in standard PGGs, is closely linked to higher efficiency. However, the meta-analysis does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so predictions about efficiency must be inferred from the contribution results. The effect of punishment is robust across studies and is stronger when punishment is free. Other design variables (rewards, monitoring, group stability) have positive but less durable effects. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when punishment is not costly to the punisher, but the exact magnitude for efficiency must be inferred indirectly.

- source: Sylwester, K., Hellmann, B., & Bryson, J. J. (2013). Homo Homini Lupus? Explaining Antisocial Punishment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews and interprets evidence that antisocial punishment—punishing cooperators at a cost to oneself—is a widespread phenomenon in public goods games, especially in certain cultural and ecological contexts. It argues that antisocial punishment is often driven by status competition, aggression, or negative reciprocity, and is more prevalent where the cost of punishment is low, legal institutions are weak, and social competition is high. The review notes that antisocial punishment can undermine the cooperation-enhancing effects of punishment, and that its prevalence varies widely across cultures. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment; instead, it focuses on behavioral mechanisms and cross-cultural patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical and contextual background for understanding when and why punishment may fail to increase efficiency in public goods games, especially due to antisocial punishment. It highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be negative in environments with high antisocial punishment. However, because the paper is a review and does not report new or meta-analyzed efficiency or payoff data, it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for identifying moderators (e.g., punishment cost, anonymity, cultural context) that should be considered when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, and for warning that punishment can sometimes reduce, rather than increase, group welfare.

- source: Schunk, D., & Wagner, V. (2021). What determines the willingness to sanction violations of newly introduced social norms: Personality traits or economic preferences? evidence from the COVID-19 crisis. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on punishment in public goods games, emphasizing that while punishment can sustain cooperation, the presence of antisocial punishment (punishment of cooperators) can undermine this effect. It highlights the importance of reputation, group size, and social context in moderating the effectiveness of punishment. The paper does not present new data or direct analyses of efficiency or group payoff, but provides a conceptual overview of when and why punishment may or may not improve cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual background on the mechanisms by which punishment can affect cooperation in public goods games, including the role of antisocial punishment and reputation. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions affect the efficiency impact of punishment. Its value for prediction is mainly in highlighting potential moderators (e.g., group size, reputation systems, presence of antisocial punishment), but it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes from design parameters.

- source: Dugar, S. (2013). NON-MONETARY INCENTIVES AND OPPORTUNISTIC BEHAVIOR: EVIDENCE FROM A LABORATORY PUBLIC GOOD GAME. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the introduction of non-monetary, costless disapproval points (social sanctions) after each round leads to significantly higher group contributions and group earnings (efficiency) compared to a baseline with no such mechanism. Approval points (social rewards) alone have a small, weakly significant effect on efficiency. The combination of approval and disapproval points produces the highest efficiency, with group earnings and contributions both significantly higher than in any other treatment. The effect is robust over 15 rounds, though some decay is observed. The study demonstrates that even costless, payoff-irrelevant social sanctions can substantially improve group welfare in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a costless, non-monetary punishment mechanism (disapproval points) in a standard linear public goods game increases efficiency (group earnings) relative to a baseline with no such mechanism. The effect is larger than that of a costless reward mechanism (approval points), and the combination of both is most effective. Because the punishment is costless and does not reduce group payoffs, all efficiency gains are realized. For prediction tasks, this suggests that introducing a non-monetary, costless punishment mechanism in a repeated, anonymous, four-player, 15-round linear PGG with MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency substantially over the control. The effect is likely to be even larger if both approval and disapproval are available. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with costless, anonymous, non-monetary sanctions, and may not generalize to costly or monetary punishment.

- source: Maier-Rigaud, F. P., Martinsson, P., & Staffiero, G. (2010). Ostracism and the provision of a public good: experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that allowing group members to ostracize (exclude) others by majority vote in a repeated linear public goods game leads to a substantial and statistically significant increase in group contributions and average earnings (efficiency) compared to the standard game without ostracism. This effect is robust except in the first and last periods, where contributions are similar across treatments due to initial uncertainty and end-game effects. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors, and after initial exclusions, remaining members tend to converge to high contribution levels. The positive effect on efficiency is observed both when ostracism is introduced first or second in the session, and is higher than in the control. The design ensures that the only difference between treatments is the possibility of exclusion, and there is no direct monetary punishment or cost to voting. The results suggest that ostracism is an effective endogenous sanctioning mechanism for sustaining cooperation and increasing group welfare in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ostracism (exclusion by majority vote) in a repeated linear public goods game with fixed partners, full information on others' contributions, and no cost to voting, leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency (average earnings as a fraction of the social optimum) compared to the same game without ostracism. The effect is robust across session orderings and is not offset by the reduction in group size due to exclusion. For prediction tasks, the presence of an ostracism mechanism (even with no direct monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially in games with similar design parameters (6 players, 10 rounds, continuous contributions, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no reward, no punishment cost, anonymous feedback on contributions). The effect is strongest in the middle rounds and may diminish at the end due to end-game effects.

- source: Fatas, E., Morales, A. J., & Ubeda, P. (2010). Blind justice: An experimental analysis of random punishment in team production. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implements a public goods game with a novel random punishment mechanism: after each round, each group member is excluded from the group benefit with probability 1-R, where R is the normalized group contribution. This 'blind' punishment does not target individual defectors. The experiment compares three conditions: standard VCM (no punishment), random punishment without redistribution (RP), and random punishment with redistribution (RPR). Results show that both RP and RPR significantly increase contributions compared to VCM. However, only RPR increases group earnings (efficiency), as RP's punishment cost outweighs the contribution gains. The effect of random punishment is similar whether introduced after a baseline or from the start. Behavioral analysis shows that high contributors reduce their contributions if punished, but overall, conditional cooperation persists and the net effect is positive for contributions. The mechanism does not fully prevent the decline in contributions over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that random, non-individually-targeted punishment can increase contributions in a standard public goods game, but its effect on efficiency depends on whether the penalty is redistributed. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism that is not individually targeted but is probabilistically linked to group performance will likely increase contributions, but may not increase efficiency unless the punishment cost is recycled within the group. The effect is robust to order and does not depend on communication or knowledge of individual contributions. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is 0.5, and the group size is 4. The findings suggest that, for similar designs, enabling random punishment will increase contributions and may increase efficiency if the punishment cost is not lost to the group.

- source: Dannenberg, A., Haita-Falah, C., & Zitzelsberger, S. (2020). Voting on the threat of exclusion in a public goods experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of 5, the option to exclude (ostracize) members is introduced either costlessly (B10) or with a 20% endowment cost to all (B8). Groups can vote to implement the exclusion institution (endogenous) or have it imposed (exogenous). The exclusion institution leads to significantly higher contributions in all treatments. When exclusion is costless (B10), average payoffs (efficiency) are higher with exclusion than without. When exclusion is costly (B8), average payoffs are lower with exclusion than without, despite higher contributions. The exclusion mechanism is used almost exclusively to remove the lowest contributors, and the threat of exclusion increases cooperation among those who remain. The effect of exclusion on efficiency is positive when costless, but negative or neutral when costly. Endogenous and exogenous implementation yield similar results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment (here, exclusion/ostracism) on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the costliness of the punishment institution. When exclusion is costless, enabling punishment increases both contributions and efficiency. When exclusion is costly (reducing endowments), enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve efficiency and may reduce it. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), group size, and the presence/absence of communication are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction tasks. The results are robust to endogenous vs exogenous institution choice. For prediction, the key moderator is whether the punishment institution imposes a cost on all group members; if so, efficiency gains are not realized even if cooperation rises.

- source: Peeters, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2013). IMMATERIAL REWARDS AND SANCTIONS IN A VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION EXPERIMENT. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing costless, immaterial feedback (smilies/frownies) does not significantly increase average group contributions or payoffs in a voluntary contribution public goods game. Under partner matching, feedback treatments show higher variance in group contributions, with some groups sustaining high contributions, but the average effect is not significant. Under stranger matching, feedback treatments do not outperform control and may even reduce contributions. The effect of feedback on future contributions is context-dependent: under partner matching, sending/receiving emoticons can reduce future contributions; under stranger matching, receiving a frowny can increase future contributions for some participants. Overall, immaterial feedback does not reliably improve efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costless, immaterial punishment or reward mechanisms (such as emoticons) do not significantly improve efficiency or group payoff in standard public goods games, regardless of matching protocol. For prediction tasks, the presence of immaterial, non-costly, non-monetary punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. The effect of such feedback is highly variable and context-dependent, with no robust positive effect on group payoff or efficiency. The findings suggest that, unless punishment or reward is material (i.e., has real payoff consequences), enabling such mechanisms is unlikely to improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Hugh-Jones, D., & Perroni, C. (2017). The logic of costly punishment reversed: Expropriation of free-riders and outsiders. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the threat of group expropriation (a form of punishment) can increase public goods contributions, but only when the expropriation mechanism is sufficiently difficult to coordinate (i.e., requires a large group to act together). When expropriation is too easy (requires only one or two people), it is less targeted at free-riders, more arbitrary, and leads to lower contributions and more frequent expropriation, reducing efficiency. In the baseline (punishment enabled, M=3), contributions are significantly higher than in the control (no expropriation), and expropriation is mostly targeted at unique lowest contributors. When the minimum group size for expropriation is reduced (M=2 or M=1), expropriation increases and contributions fall. In heterogeneous groups, minorities are more likely to be targeted in some treatments, especially when ambiguity or a history of targeting exists, and may respond less to punishment. However, in the main treatments, overall group efficiency is not significantly different between homogeneous and heterogeneous groups. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (especially the ease of coordination required for punishment) is a critical moderator of its effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the punishment technology, specifically the minimum group size required for successful punishment (expropriation). When punishment is difficult to coordinate (higher M), it is more likely to be used as a credible threat against free-riders, increasing contributions and efficiency. When punishment is too easy (low M), it is used more arbitrarily, contributions fall, and efficiency is reduced. For prediction, the dimension 'punishment_tech' (minimum group size for punishment, or ease of expropriation) is a key moderator: higher coordination requirements for punishment (higher M) are associated with more positive effects of punishment on efficiency, while lower requirements (lower M) can make punishment detrimental. Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with well-targeted, hard-to-coordinate punishment. Heterogeneity (minority status) can moderate targeting and behavioral response, but does not robustly reduce efficiency unless ambiguity or a history of targeting is present. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially punishment_tech, and provide indirect evidence for the importance of targeting and coordination in punishment mechanisms.

- source: Galbiati, R., & Vertova, P. (2014). How laws affect behavior: Obligations, incentives and cooperative behavior. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Obligations (minimum required contributions) significantly increase average contributions to the public good, even in the absence of incentives. Non-binding incentives (probabilistic, low-stakes rewards/punishments) alone do not affect contributions, but when combined with obligations, they substantially increase contributions beyond the effect of obligations alone. The effect of obligations and incentives is complementary. The mechanisms are traced to changes in beliefs about others' contributions and in individuals' willingness to cooperate. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the design implies that higher contributions increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot linear public good game with 6 players and no communication, introducing a minimum contribution requirement (obligation) increases average contributions, and adding even weak, non-binding incentives (probabilistic punishment/reward) further increases contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the structure of the game means that higher contributions correspond to higher efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of obligations and (even weak) punishment/reward mechanisms can be expected to increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither, especially when both are present. However, the absence of direct efficiency data means that quantitative predictions must be inferred from the reported contribution rates.

- source: Orr, S. W. (2001). The economics of shame in work groups: How mutual monitoring can decrease cooperation in teams. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds, through theoretical argument and reference to motivation crowding theory, that mutual monitoring and social sanctions (including social disapproval) in public-goods-like group incentive schemes can decrease cooperation among normatively motivated individuals due to crowding out of intrinsic motivation. The effect is non-linear: some monitoring may initially increase cooperation, but excessive or controlling monitoring leads to suspicion and a shift from normative to gain frames, reducing contributions. For individualistic types, monitoring may crowd in normative motivation and increase cooperation. The overall effect of mutual monitoring or social sanctions on group performance is thus frequency-dependent on the motivational composition of the group. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that in groups with many normatively motivated members, mutual monitoring may reduce cooperation and thus likely reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution that enabling mutual monitoring or social sanctions (punishment) in public-goods-like environments may not always increase efficiency or cooperation, especially in groups with many normatively motivated members. The effect of punishment or monitoring is predicted to be non-linear and frequency-dependent: it may increase cooperation among individualists but decrease it among cooperators if perceived as controlling. For the prediction task, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on group composition and the perceived nature of monitoring. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting possible negative or mixed effects of punishment mechanisms, especially social (rather than material) sanctions.

- source: Carpenter, J., & Seki, E. (2011). DO SOCIAL PREFERENCES INCREASE PRODUCTIVITY? FIELD EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE FROM FISHERMEN IN TOYAMA BAY. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study uses a field experiment with fishermen and students, employing a repeated public goods game (VCM) with a costly, non-material 'social disapproval' mechanism (unhappy face) in the last five rounds. Pooling fishermen (who share catch and income) are more productive in real life, and this is linked to higher social preference measures from the experiment. The social disapproval mechanism increases contributions among poolers, and social preference measures (conditional cooperation, propensity to disapprove, response to disapproval) are significant predictors of both experimental contributions and real-world fishing productivity. The effect of social preferences on productivity is substantial, and the productivity advantage of pooling boats is largely explained by these preferences. The 'punishment' in the experiment is not material, so the findings are most directly about social (rather than monetary) sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that social preference mechanisms, even when implemented as costly but non-material signals (social disapproval), can increase both experimental and real-world efficiency in public-goods-like environments. However, the 'punishment' here is not material: it is a costly signal with no direct monetary penalty. The findings suggest that enabling even non-material, costly social sanctions can increase efficiency, especially among groups with strong social ties or cooperative institutions. For prediction tasks, the presence of a social disapproval mechanism (even without material punishment) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly in groups with pre-existing cooperative norms. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is non-material but salient, and less directly informative about material punishment effects.

- source: Charness, G., & Yang, C. L. (2014). Starting small toward voluntary formation of efficient large groups in public goods provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous group formation with exit, exclusion, and merger options produces very high contribution rates and group efficiency in public goods games. In the main treatment, where the marginal per-capita return (MPCR) increases with group size, groups tend to grow large and stable, and efficiency is maximized. When the efficiency gain from larger groups is capped, groups remain smaller and less stable, and efficiency is lower, but still substantially higher than in fixed-group baselines. Exclusion (a form of sanction) is used to remove low contributors, which protects cooperators and sustains high efficiency. Redemption (the ability for excluded individuals to rejoin) further boosts efficiency. The results suggest that voluntary group formation with exclusion and merger mechanisms can solve the free-rider problem and achieve high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games, mechanisms allowing voluntary group formation, exit, exclusion (as a sanction), and merger can dramatically increase efficiency compared to fixed groups. The effect is especially strong when economies of scale are present (i.e., when MPCR increases with group size). The exclusion mechanism functions as a sanction, but is not a direct costly punishment; rather, it is a group-level sanction that removes low contributors. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of exclusion/exit/merger mechanisms and increasing MPCR with group size are strong positive moderators of the efficiency effect of sanction-like mechanisms. However, the effect is not directly about costly punishment, but about exclusion as a sanction. The results are less informative for standard costly punishment, but highly informative for exclusion-based sanctions and endogenous group formation. Control efficiency (fixed group) is much lower than in the endogenous group treatments.

- source: Otto, P. E., & Bolle, F. (2016). The advantage of hierarchy: Inducing responsibility and selecting ability?. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three regimes: standard PGG (voluntary contributions), AG (randomly assigned allocator determines all contributions), and PAG (principal selects allocator each round). Both AG and PAG significantly increase group production and average incomes relative to PGG. PAG (principal selection) yields the highest efficiency, with further increases in satisfaction and fairness ratings. The effect is not due to learning or order effects. The mechanism works by centralizing responsibility and leveraging selection, not by explicit punishment. In treatments with heterogeneous productivity, the most productive worker is more likely to be appointed allocator, but does not exploit the position to reduce own effort. The findings show that hierarchy can substitute for punishment in sustaining high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing hierarchical control (an allocator or principal-allocator structure) in a public goods game can substantially increase efficiency, even in the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a central authority who can determine or influence contributions should be expected to yield efficiency gains similar to or greater than those typically attributed to punishment. The results are robust across group size (n=3), number of rounds (10 per regime), and both homogeneous and heterogeneous productivity. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is not available but hierarchy is, efficiency can be high. For prediction, if a game design includes a mechanism where one player can set others' contributions (with or without selection by a principal), expect efficiency to be much higher than in standard PGG, even if punishment is absent.

- source: Nosenzo, D., & Tufano, F. (2017). The effect of voluntary participation on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three main treatments: Baseline (forced participation), Unconditional (voluntary participation before knowing partner's contribution), and Conditional (voluntary participation after seeing partner's contribution). In Baseline and Unconditional, average contributions and efficiency are nearly identical (about 22% of endowment contributed; efficiency ~22%). In Conditional, average contributions and efficiency are much higher (47% of endowment; efficiency up to 81.9% among those who agree to play, and 46.6% overall). The positive effect of voluntary participation on efficiency is only observed when participation can be used as a threat against free-riding. There is little evidence that unconditional voluntary participation leads to assortative matching of cooperators, even when the outside option is increased. The main mechanism for increased efficiency is the threat of non-participation disciplining free-riders, not selection. However, frequent withdrawals can reduce realized efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person one-shot PGG, enabling voluntary participation only increases efficiency if players can condition their participation on knowledge of the partner's intended contribution (i.e., if non-participation is a credible threat against free-riding). Unconditional voluntary participation (without information) does not improve efficiency, even with a higher outside option. For prediction, the presence of a conditional participation mechanism (a form of endogenous partner exclusion or exit threat) is a strong moderator of punishment-like effects on efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling conditional exit can substitute for explicit punishment in raising efficiency, but unconditional exit does not. Control efficiency (forced participation) is a good predictor of efficiency in unconditional exit games, but not in conditional exit games, where efficiency is much higher.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Gavrilets, S., Qin, C. Z., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). Kinship can hinder cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
