  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods and multi-player PD games with heterogeneous cost-benefit ratios, increasing genetic relatedness does not always increase cooperation or efficiency. For low relatedness, direct reciprocity (punishment via GRIM) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency. For high relatedness, cooperation is a dominant strategy and efficiency is high. However, for intermediate relatedness, some individuals become unconditional cooperators (AllC), which undermines the effectiveness of punishment and allows others to defect (AllD), making full cooperation and maximal efficiency unsustainable. This results in a non-monotonic, U-shaped relationship between relatedness and efficiency. The findings are supported by both subgame perfect equilibrium analysis and agent-based evolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public goods games with heterogeneity in cost-benefit ratios, the effect of mechanisms analogous to punishment (i.e., direct reciprocity via GRIM) on efficiency depends critically on the population structure and relatedness. When relatedness is low, punishment can sustain high efficiency; when relatedness is high, cooperation is self-sustaining. However, at intermediate relatedness, the threat of punishment is undermined, and efficiency drops. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity and the degree of relatedness (or analogous parameters) can cause non-monotonic effects of punishment on efficiency, and that punishment is most effective at low relatedness. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated PGG-like games with heterogeneity, but less so for one-shot games or games with explicit costly punishment mechanisms.

- source: Brick, K., Visser, M., & Van der Hoven, Z. (2016). Cooperation and Climate Change: Can Communication Facilitate the Provision of Public Goods in Heterogeneous Settings?. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Communication (chat) between group members in a heterogeneous public good game increases average contributions and the likelihood of meeting a collective target, but does not fully solve the free-rider problem. Even with communication, many groups fail to reach the target, and free-riding remains common, especially among high marginal cost players. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests, based on prior literature, that punishment opportunities may be necessary to ensure compliance and improve efficiency, especially in heterogeneous settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of communication (chat) in a heterogeneous public good game, but does not provide any experimental evidence on punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly informative, as no punishment treatment is implemented. However, it does provide a useful control case: in a 4-player, one-shot, linear PGG with MPCR=0.42, no punishment, and no feedback on others' contributions, average group contributions are well below the social optimum, and communication increases but does not maximize efficiency. The paper's discussion and literature review suggest that punishment is likely to further increase cooperation and efficiency, but this is not tested here. Thus, this paper is best used as a control or baseline for settings without punishment.

- source: Riedel, N., & Schildberg-Hörisch, H. (2013). Asymmetric obligations. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that both high and low minimum contribution obligations (even when only weakly enforced) increase contributions in the initial periods compared to no obligation, but this effect is not sustained in the long run. Asymmetric obligations are as effective as symmetric ones: for a given own obligation, the partner's obligation does not affect behavior. The effect of obligations is interpreted as arising from an emotional cost of disobeying, not from expectations about others' behavior. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that weakly enforced minimum contribution obligations can temporarily increase contributions in public goods games, regardless of whether obligations are symmetric or asymmetric. However, since the study does not include a standard punishment institution (where players can assign costly punishment to others), and the 'punishment' here is a very weak, probabilistic, externally imposed fine for not meeting a minimum, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that expressive law (weak obligations) can have a short-term effect on cooperation, but do not inform about the efficiency effects of endogenous peer punishment. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of a standard punishment treatment limits direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under peer punishment.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2015). INCOMPLETE INFORMATION STRENGTHENS THE EFFECTIVENESS OF SOCIAL APPROVAL. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling subjects to express social approval (assigning 0-10 stars to each group member after observing contributions) significantly increases contributions to the public good compared to a control with no approval. This effect is stronger when subjects have only partial information (see only others' contributions) than when they have full information (see both contributions and endowments). The study isolates the consumption value of social approval by using stranger matching and anonymity, ruling out reputation effects. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings are reported; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-monetary, symbolic social approval can increase contributions in public good games, but the effect is sensitive to the information structure. However, because the study does not include monetary punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is otherwise closely matched to standard PGGs, so the behavioral findings may be relevant for understanding how non-monetary incentives (like approval) interact with information, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from punishment interventions.

- source: Hassan, M., Amin, E., Mansour, S., & Voigt, S. (2023). Incentivizing cooperation against a norm of defection: Experimental Evidence from Egypt. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that (1) pride primes can increase high contributors' willingness to contribute, but with a lagged effect, and (2) appeal messages (religious, patriotic, social harm, or custom) sent by high contributors to low contributors increase the recipients' subsequent contributions by 2–5 points on average. However, most high contributors choose not to send appeals, and there is no direct monetary punishment or reward. The study does not report group efficiency or total group payoff, so the effect of these interventions on efficiency is not directly assessed. The interventions are non-monetary and adjacent to punishment, as they are costly to the sender but do not reduce the recipient's payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that non-monetary, costly appeals can increase individual contributions in a public goods game, especially in a context with a strong norm of defection. However, because the interventions do not reduce the recipient's payoff and group efficiency is not reported, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of monetary punishment on efficiency. The design is most informative for settings where costly communication or social sanctions (not payoff-reducing punishment) are available. The results suggest that such appeals can increase contributions, but the effect on efficiency remains unquantified. For prediction tasks focused on monetary punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Gächter, S., & Fehr, E. (1999). Collective action as a social exchange. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that social approval opportunities alone (i.e., the ability to express approval/disapproval after the game) do not significantly increase cooperation in a standard public goods game among strangers. However, when minimal group familiarity is introduced (via a brief pre-game interaction), the combination of social approval and familiarity leads to a large and significant increase in contributions. The effect is not observed for social approval or group familiarity alone. The main outcome is the contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that social approval can be a powerful motivator for cooperation, but only when social distance is reduced. The study does not test or report on material punishment or pecuniary sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of material punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of non-pecuniary social incentives (approval/disapproval) and their interaction with group familiarity, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that social mechanisms can increase cooperation, but only under certain social conditions. For the downstream prediction task (punishment-enabled efficiency), this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency as a primary outcome.

- source: Koukoumelis, A., & Levati, M. V. (2019). An experiment investigating the spillover effects of communication opportunities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that recipients of unfair offers in a mini dictator game reduce their contributions in a subsequent public goods game. However, if they are allowed to send a message to the dictator, this reduction is significantly smaller—only in treatments without belief elicitation. When beliefs about the partner's contribution are elicited, the messaging opportunity does not significantly affect contribution changes for the full sample, but does for the subsample of recipients who actually reduce their contributions. The mechanism appears to be emotional relief from having a 'voice,' not a change in beliefs about partner behavior. The study does not analyze group efficiency or total payoff, and there is no monetary punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the effects of non-monetary, symbolic communication (emotional expression) on cooperation, but not for monetary punishment or efficiency outcomes. The design and findings may be informative for adjacent questions about communication, emotion, and cooperation, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Guillen, P., Fatas, E., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2010). Inducing efficient conditional cooperation patterns in public goods games, an experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a grim trigger strategy threat (via possible automata) leads to a substantial increase in average contributions (by 40% before a surprise restart and 50% after) compared to a standard repeated public goods game. Contributions in the threat treatment are highly polarized (either full or zero), and the pattern of conditional cooperation changes: in the threat treatment, the minimum contribution in the previous round predicts current contributions, consistent with grim trigger logic. However, contributions still decline over time, and the effect is driven by belief manipulation rather than actual punishment or sanctions. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the higher contributions imply higher group payoffs, net of the absence of punishment costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that belief in the possibility of strict conditional cooperation (grim trigger) can substantially increase contributions in repeated public goods games, even without actual punishment. However, because there is no real punishment mechanism (no cost, no assignment of punishment, no reduction of others' payoffs), the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The results suggest that the mere threat or belief in sanctions can have large effects on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the efficiency impact of actual punishment mechanisms, especially considering the cost of punishment. The design dimensions are well specified, but the absence of real punishment means the findings should be used as indirect evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Guo, J. D. R. (2025). Doing good in the digital world. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both identification and attribution of individual contributions increase average contributions in a finitely repeated public goods game. The effect is strongest when both are present, but even imprecise identifiers (numbers, avatars) and partial attribution (not perfectly linking actions to identifiers) increase contributions relative to baseline. The effect is primarily behavioral: more subjects contribute their full endowment when individual contributions are revealed. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the game, and the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Theoretical analysis considers the possibility of punishment outside the game, but the experimental data do not support a punishment-based explanation for the observed effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how identification and attribution affect contribution behavior in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Since no punishment or reward mechanism is present, the findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with punishment enabled. However, the results do suggest that increasing transparency and reducing anonymity can increase contributions, which may interact with the effects of punishment in other designs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence on the effects of information and identification, but not on punishment or efficiency.

- source: Wei, C. (2020). Can job rotation eliminate the Ratchet effect: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that ethnic dominance (caste hierarchy within groups) has a large negative effect on contributions in a public goods game, while ethnic diversity alone does not. Individuals display strong in-group bias, increasing their contributions in response to co-ethnic peers but not to others. The experiment did not include punishment or reward options, and all quantitative results are about contribution levels, not payoffs or efficiency. The authors discuss antisocial punishment and discrimination as real-world mechanisms but these are not present in the experimental design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms were present in the experiment. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition (especially ethnic dominance) can affect baseline cooperation rates, which may be relevant for interpreting control group efficiency in similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the effect of enabling punishment, but it does highlight that group structure (e.g., ethnic dominance) can strongly affect baseline cooperation, which could interact with punishment effects in other studies.

- source: Sutter, M., & Untertrifaller, A. (2020). Children's heterogeneity in cooperation and parental background: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that allocating taxes and rewards based on memory reputation generally increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games, especially in environments with high taxes or high synergy factors. In low-tax, low-synergy environments (favorable to defectors), equal allocation of rewards and taxes is more effective at sustaining cooperation. The model shows that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms depends on the balance between tax and reward costs, memory length, and the synergy factor. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the structure of incentive mechanisms (tax-funded rewards, reputation-based allocation) can affect cooperation rates, and that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on environmental parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive incentive mechanisms interact with game design dimensions.

- source: Gächter, S., Herrmann, B., & Thöni, C. (2004). Trust, voluntary cooperation, and socio-economic background: survey and experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in a one-shot public goods game (n=3, MPCR=0.5), both students and non-students contribute substantial amounts, with non-students contributing more on average. However, after controlling for socio-economic background, these differences disappear. Trust attitudes—specifically, beliefs that others are fair, helpful, and that strangers can be trusted—are significantly positively correlated with higher contributions. The widely used GSS trust question is not significantly correlated with contribution. Socio-economic background affects trust attitudes but not contribution behavior. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the analyzed experiment, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as the analyzed experiment does not include a punishment condition and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. It does, however, provide informative context about baseline contribution behavior in a standard one-shot public goods game (n=3, MPCR=0.5) with a diverse subject pool. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper can inform the expected baseline (control) contribution rates in the absence of punishment, but not the treatment effect of punishment. The paper also notes (in passing) that punishment can reduce the trust problem in repeated games, but this is only discussed, not tested.

- source: Colasante, A., Morone, A., Nemore, F., & Tiranzoni, P. (2024). Self-love, growth, and competition in a public good game. *Kyklos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing intragroup competition for higher MPCRs in a public goods game, especially when combined with dynamic endowments (growth), leads to significantly higher contributions and much greater group wealth compared to growth alone. The CG treatment (competition + growth) more than doubles average group wealth compared to growth-only (G) treatment. Inequality (Gini coefficient) is lower in the short term in the CG treatment than in G, but converges over time. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or tested in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games. It is not informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as no punishment mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of dynamic endowments and intragroup competition, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Gürerk, Kaçamak, Y., & Kart, E. (2024). How to increase and sustain cooperation in public goods games: Conditional commitments via a mediator. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The conditional commitment mechanism, when it enables full contribution (FULL treatment), leads to almost universal agreement and very high efficiency: average group payoffs are close to the social optimum. In the HALF treatment (commitment to half contribution), agreement rates and efficiency are lower, and some subjects attempt to lead by contributing more than the default, but this does not fully overcome the free-rider problem. There is no punishment or sanctioning in any treatment. The study demonstrates that a well-designed commitment device can substitute for punishment in achieving high efficiency in public goods games, but only if the commitment outcome is itself efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency of a standard linear public goods game (VCM) with and without a strong commitment device. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is best used as a control/baseline for efficiency in the absence of punishment, and as evidence for the potential of alternative mechanisms (commitment) to achieve high efficiency. It does not inform the marginal effect of punishment relative to control, but does provide precise efficiency data for a 4-player, 10-round, MPCR=0.4, no-punishment, no-reward, no-chat, continuous contribution VCM.

- source: Kocher, M. G., Tan, F. F., & Yu, J. (2018). PROVIDING GLOBAL PUBLIC GOODS: ELECTORAL DELEGATION AND COOPERATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares a standard linear public goods game (Baseline) to a Delegation treatment where each group elects a delegate who determines the contributions of all group members. Delegation leads to higher average contributions and efficiency than the Baseline, but both fall short of the social optimum. Over time, contributions and efficiency decline in both treatments, but less so in Delegation. Delegates rarely exploit their group members, typically assigning equal contributions. However, intergroup conditionality and lack of coordination across groups lead to a downward spiral in contributions and efficiency, a phenomenon the authors term 'P-inefficiency.' The study provides direct evidence on how delegation affects efficiency in public goods provision, but does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of electoral delegation (but not punishment) on efficiency in a linear public goods game. It is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments with and without delegation, but not for environments with punishment or sanctioning. The design dimensions (player count, group size, rounds, MPCR, information, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment.

- source: Noussair, C., & Soo, C. (2008). Voluntary contributions to a dynamic public good: Experimental evidence. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a dynamic public goods game where the MPCR increases or decreases based on group contributions, many groups are able to sustain high contributions and achieve high efficiency, even in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms. The average efficiency is .671 in the high initial MPCR treatment and .632 in the low initial MPCR treatment, both substantially above the efficiency that would result from full free-riding. There is substantial heterogeneity: some groups converge to high contributions and high MPCR, while others decline to low contributions. The initial MPCR does not significantly affect long-run outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a baseline for the efficiency of dynamic public goods games without punishment or reward. It shows that, in such settings, efficiency can be substantially higher than in standard static public goods games, due to the dynamic feedback of contributions on the MPCR. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in dynamic PGGs, but it does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed experimentally. Thus, it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, but it does suggest that dynamic feedback mechanisms can themselves sustain high efficiency in some groups.

- source: Cadigan, J., Wayland, P. T., Schmitt, P., & Swope, K. (2011). An experimental dynamic public goods game with carryover. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing dynamic carryover (either via endowment or MPCR) can increase first-stage contributions in public goods games, but this does not reliably translate into higher efficiency. Efficiency, defined as the share of maximal cooperative gains captured, is not significantly different from the baseline in most treatments, except when MPCR is increased. The main determinants of contributions and efficiency are endowment and MPCR, consistent with prior VCM literature. There is no evidence on punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are not present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how dynamic features like carryover in endowment or MPCR affect efficiency and contributions in public goods games without punishment. It shows that increasing contributions via dynamic incentives does not necessarily increase efficiency, especially if the maximum possible surplus also increases. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency is primarily determined by MPCR and endowment, and that dynamic features alone do not guarantee higher efficiency. The paper does not inform about the effects of punishment, so it should not be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment in similar games.

- source: Benchekroun, H., & Van Long, N. (2008). The build-up of cooperative behavior among non-cooperative selfish agents. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with no punishment or sanctions, but where agents can condition their behavior on a public 'stock of cooperation' (social history), a continuum of Markovian Nash equilibria exist. These equilibria can support steady-state contribution and welfare levels above the static Nash equilibrium but below the social optimum. The social optimum cannot be stably achieved without punishment. The model provides explicit analytical characterizations of these equilibria and shows that the more patient the agents (lower discount rate), the closer the best equilibrium can approach the social optimum. The paper emphasizes that, unlike models with punishment, the highest sustainable efficiency is strictly less than the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games without punishment or sanctions, especially when social history or trust mechanisms are present. It provides strong theoretical evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be improved above the Nash baseline if agents coordinate on social-history-based strategies, but the social optimum is not achievable. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment (which is absent here) would allow efficiency to reach or exceed the best equilibrium described in this model, and potentially reach the social optimum. The paper is not informative about the effect of punishment per se, but it provides a theoretical upper bound for efficiency in the absence of punishment, given social history mechanisms.

- source: Chessa, M., & Loiseau, P. (2024). Enhancing voluntary contributions in a public goods economy via a minimum individual contribution level. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that introducing a minimum individual contribution level (while still allowing for full free riding) in a voluntary public goods game can strictly increase the total contribution and social welfare at equilibrium, provided the minimum is not set too high. The analysis covers both heterogeneous and homogeneous cost cases, and characterizes the equilibrium structure as a function of the minimum. The results show that there is an optimal range for the minimum contribution: if set just above the lowest equilibrium contribution in the baseline game, it increases total contributions without causing contributors to drop out. If set too high, some contributors become free riders and total contributions can decrease. The paper provides operational guidance for setting the minimum but does not address punishment, reward, or other sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions. However, it is highly informative about the effects of restricting the contribution set by imposing a minimum, which is a different intervention. The findings may be useful for understanding how changes to the action space (such as minimums or defaults) affect efficiency, but cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bartos, V. (2021). Seasonal scarcity and sharing norms. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that neither making ethnic identity salient nor priming participants with memories of past ethnic violence significantly affects cooperation or generosity in the public goods or dictator games. There is a modest negative effect of violence priming on coordination with in-group partners in the stag hunt game, but this does not generalize to the public goods game. No significant differences are found in trust or trustworthiness by treatment. The results suggest that, in this context, ethnic divisions and reminders of conflict do not meaningfully alter pro-social economic behavior in standard experimental games. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it provides no evidence on the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any of the games. It does, however, provide a well-powered null result for the effect of ethnic identity salience and conflict priming on cooperation in a two-player public goods game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant. For baseline efficiency in two-player PGGs without punishment, the results suggest that neither ethnic matching nor conflict salience meaningfully affect average contributions or pro-social behavior.

- source: Chakraborti, R., Maloney, M., Roberts, G., & Shogren, J. F. (2018). Social capital and the voluntary provision of public goods. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Both structured (team-building) and unstructured (free-form) communication interventions increase contributions in a linear public goods game. Free-form communication leads to higher and more persistent contributions than team-building when group composition is stable. When groups are reallocated after the intervention, team-building's effect on contributions persists, but the effect of free-form communication is reduced, though still positive. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its findings are relevant for understanding the impact of communication and social capital interventions on contribution behavior, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

- source: Martinsson, P., Medhin, H., & Persson, E. (2019). MINIMUM LEVELS AND FRAMING IN PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a standard linear public goods game, contributions are higher in the give frame than in the take frame. When a minimum contribution level (20% of endowment) is introduced, contributions decrease in the give frame (crowding out) but increase in the take frame (crowding in). The effect is driven by more low contributors being forced to increase in the take frame, while in the give frame, some higher contributors reduce their contributions to the minimum. The distribution of contributor types is stable across frames and minimum level conditions. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it provides informative evidence about the effects of minimum contribution requirements and framing on contribution behavior. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For tasks involving minimum contribution levels or framing, the results suggest that minimum levels can have opposite effects depending on the frame: crowding out in give, crowding in in take. Since efficiency is not reported, the findings should not be used to predict efficiency outcomes of punishment-enabled games.

- source: Dorner, Z., Tucker, S., & Hassan, G. M. (2024). Heterogeneous productivity stabilizes public good contributions under certainty, uncertainty and ambiguity. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous productivity (certain, uncertain, or ambiguous) into a repeated public goods game leads to more stable contributions over time compared to the standard homogeneous productivity baseline, where contributions typically decline. Under certain heterogeneity, contributions remain stable and end higher than in the homogeneous case. Under uncertainty or ambiguity, contributions start lower but are also stable. The effect is driven by high-productivity types contributing more, which supports higher group contributions. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, and all outcome measures are behavioral (contribution levels), not payoff-based (efficiency, group earnings, or welfare).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in productivity affects contribution dynamics, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Bazart, C., Lefebvre, M., & Rosaz, J. (2022). Promoting socially desirable behaviors through persuasion and commitment: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing anonymous, open-ended communication (exhortations) in a public goods game leads to substantially higher contributions compared to a baseline with no communication. The effect is attributed to cultural norms and prior experience with exhortations, not to reputation-building, as anonymity precludes reputational effects. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on contribution rates and the content/effect of messages.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling anonymous communication (chat/exhortation) increases cooperation (contributions) in public goods games, even in the absence of reputation effects. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is informative about the effect of the 'chat' dimension (present/absent) on contribution rates, but does not inform about the effect of punishment or about efficiency per se. It is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but supports the importance of communication as a moderator of cooperation.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Molis, E., & Neyse, L. (2021). Exposure to inequality may cause under-provision of public goods: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that making subjects aware of inequality in returns between groups (by informing them about other groups' mpcr) leads to a significant reduction in contributions among low-mpcr groups and a non-significant increase among high-mpcr groups, but only when the inequality is large (mpcr difference of 0.5). When the inequality is small (mpcr difference of 0.25), the effect of social information is negligible. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that transparency about inequality can undermine cooperation in public goods provision, especially for those at a disadvantage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment. However, it does provide informative evidence about how information about inequality (social comparison) can affect contribution rates, which may indirectly affect efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight that contextual factors like perceived inequality and information can strongly moderate contribution behavior. The game design dimensions are well specified (player count, mpcr, number of rounds, information structure), but the absence of punishment means the findings cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kok, L., Oosterbaan, V., Stoker, H., & Vyrastekova, J. (2020). In-group favouritism and social norms: Public goods experiments in Tanzania. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that making sub-village identity salient (In-group treatment) does not increase public goods contributions compared to mixed sub-village groups (Out-group treatment). In fact, contributions are significantly higher in the Out-group treatment. This is explained by the activation of a village-level social norm of cooperation in the Out-group context, while the In-group context triggers more selfish, conditional cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that social context and group identity salience can affect contribution behavior, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing direct evidence on punishment or efficiency effects.

- source: Fellner-Röhling, G., Kröger, S., & Seki, E. (2020). Public good production in heterogeneous groups: An experimental analysis on the relation between external return and information. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in public goods games with heterogeneous external returns, the level and structure of information about group heterogeneity and contributor type significantly affect contribution behavior. When no information about heterogeneity is provided, both high- and low-ability types contribute similarly. When heterogeneity is known but contributions cannot be linked to type, low-ability types contribute more than high-ability types (a 'poisoning-of-the-well' effect). When contributions can be linked to type, high-ability types contribute more. Overall, more information about heterogeneity and contributor identity increases average contributions, but the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present and efficiency is not measured. However, it provides informative evidence about how information structure and heterogeneity in marginal returns affect contribution rates in public goods games. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, the results suggest that information about heterogeneity and contributor identity can increase contributions, but the translation to efficiency or payoff outcomes would require additional assumptions or data. The absence of punishment means the findings are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled.

- source: Yeo, J., & Zhuo, S. (2024). The usage of apologies and group cooperation. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing participants to send apologies (either public or private) after observing contributions in a repeated public goods game leads to significantly higher individual contributions compared to a control with no communication. The effect is stronger for public apologies. The increase in cooperation is mediated by more optimistic beliefs about others and group-level adherence to sincere apology norms. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings. All outcome measures are behavioral (contributions, beliefs, apology usage).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that simple, norm-laden communication (apologies) can increase cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially when made public. However, it does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its findings may be useful for understanding the role of communication and social norms as alternative or complementary mechanisms to punishment, but it cannot be used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment.

- source: Krause, J. S., Brandt, G., Schmidt, U., & Schunk, D. (2023). Don't sweat it: Ambient temperature does not affect social behavior and perception. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that ambient temperature in the range of 18°C to 28°C does not affect social behavior or perception in incentivized economic games, including the Public Goods Game. All behavioral outcomes (contribution, sharing, trust, etc.) and social perception measures (communion, agency, social distance, empathy) are statistically equivalent across temperature conditions. No evidence is found for either the 'warmth-prosociality' or 'heat-aggression' hypotheses in this context. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, as punishment is not present in any game and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed. However, it does provide high-quality evidence that ambient temperature in the tested range does not affect cooperation or related social behaviors in a standard two-player Public Goods Game. For prediction tasks, this suggests that temperature (within this range) is unlikely to be a relevant moderator of punishment effects or efficiency in similar lab settings. The absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper is not directly informative for the core prediction task.

- source: Martinangeli, A. F. M. (2021). Do what (you think) the rich will do: Inequality and belief heterogeneity in public good provision. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games with unequal endowments, both rich and poor participants expect the rich to contribute more. Individual contributions in the repeated game are more strongly correlated with beliefs about the rich than about the poor. Group cohesion interventions (allowing chat in a team-building task) do not significantly increase overall contributions, though there is some evidence of increased contributions among poor participants. There is no significant difference in the proportion of endowment contributed between equal and unequal endowment conditions. The paper does not analyze efficiency or total group payoff, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it provides informative context about how endowment heterogeneity and belief formation affect contribution behavior. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of beliefs about specific group members (especially the rich) in driving cooperation, and in showing that group cohesion interventions may have limited impact in the absence of feedback or punishment.

- source: Chaudhuri, A., Paichayontvijit, T., & Smith, A. (2017). Belief heterogeneity and contributions decay among conditional cooperators in public goods games. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that belief heterogeneity among conditional cooperators explains persistent differences in contribution levels and the observed decay in contributions over time. In treatments without feedback, contributions remain stable and reflect initial beliefs. When feedback is provided, optimists reduce their contributions more than pessimists increase theirs, leading to overall decay. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind contribution decay but do not directly inform the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how information feedback and belief heterogeneity affect contribution dynamics. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution decay in the absence of punishment.

- source: My, K. B., & Chalvignac, B. (2010). Voluntary participation and cooperation in a collective-good game. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing voluntary participation (exit option) in a repeated public-good game does not increase average contributions compared to the baseline with compulsory participation, unless the exit option is highly attractive. In the VP-16 treatment (exit payoff = 16), the decay in contributions over time is counteracted, and in some groups, contributions even increase in later rounds. However, average contributions are not significantly higher than in the baseline. The less attractive exit option (VP-11) does not have a positive effect. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-good games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, provide informative context about the baseline (no-punishment) efficiency and contribution dynamics in a standard linear PGG with and without voluntary participation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only useful for understanding the control (no-punishment) condition, not the treatment (punishment-enabled) condition. It does not inform the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Sonnemans, J., van Dijk, F., & van Winden, F. (2006). On the dynamics of social ties structures in groups. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in repeated public good games, social ties between group members develop and are influenced by the relative contributions of each member. Groups with more positive mutual ties tend to have higher average contributions, while groups with more negative or neutral ties have lower contributions. Social value orientation and affective responses play a significant role in the formation of these ties. The study provides evidence that group dynamics and interpersonal attitudes can lead to substantial variation in cooperation, even when incentives and endowments are held constant. However, the paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or their effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. It does, however, highlight the importance of social ties and group dynamics in driving contribution behavior, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation levels in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is mainly useful for understanding control (no-punishment) environments and the role of social preferences, but it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Liu, J., Sonntag, A., & Zizzo, D. J. (2022). Information defaults in repeated public good provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated linear public goods game (n=4, 10 rounds), the default for seeing group contribution information (opt-in vs. opt-out) does not affect the proportion of subjects who actually obtain the information. However, when the default is no information, the decline in contributions over time is slower, suggesting that cooperation unravels more slowly. The price of information (cost to see group contribution info) has a strong effect: when information is free or incentivized, almost all subjects get it; when costly, about a third still pay for it. Subjects adjust their contributions toward the group average, using beliefs when information is not available. There is no direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of information architecture (defaults and cost of information) on contribution dynamics. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For tasks involving the effect of information availability or defaults on contribution (not efficiency), the finding that a default of no information slows the decline of cooperation may be useful. However, since efficiency is not measured or analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the core prediction task.

- source: Kölle, F., & Quercia, S. (2021). The influence of empirical and normative expectations on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game, most people are conditional cooperators, increasing their contributions as others do. When there is strategic uncertainty (simultaneous play), normative expectations (what is socially appropriate) and empirical expectations (what others will do) diverge, and actual contributions are lower than the social optimum. When players can condition on others' contributions (sequential/strategy method), empirical and normative expectations are more closely aligned, and contributions are higher and more predictable. The paper provides direct evidence for the existence of a social norm of conditional cooperation, but does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation and the role of expectations in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified for a standard PGG, but the absence of any punishment or sanctioning mechanism means this paper cannot be used to predict treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Kim, D. G. (2018). Population uncertainty in voluntary contributions of public goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that population uncertainty can increase voluntary contributions in public goods games when the production function is nonlinear and concave, but has no significant effect when the production function is linear. The main outcome measured is the average (percentage) contribution to the group account. No punishment or sanction mechanisms are present in the design. The results suggest that the effect of population uncertainty depends on the shape of the production function and the nature of warm-glow utility, but do not provide evidence about punishment or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency under punishment, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence about how population uncertainty and production function shape affect contribution rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes under punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a control for understanding baseline contribution behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Oprea, R., Charness, G., & Friedman, D. (2014). Continuous time and communication in a public-goods experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a challenging public-goods environment (MPCR = 0.3, 4 players), continuous time by itself does not significantly increase contributions compared to discrete time. However, when free-form chat is allowed, continuous time enables groups to coordinate and sustain full contributions, with median rates at 100%. Discrete time with chat also increases contributions, but less so and with more heterogeneity. Limited communication (pre-programmed messages) does not improve contributions over no communication. The findings suggest that the combination of continuous time and rich communication is critical for achieving high cooperation, but the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms means the results are specific to communication as a coordination device.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward, enabling free-form communication (chat) in a public-goods game with continuous time can dramatically increase contribution rates, which likely translates to higher efficiency. However, without communication, continuous time alone does not improve efficiency much over discrete time. The results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without chat, and with continuous versus discrete time, but do not inform about punishment effects. The findings are less relevant for settings where punishment or reward is available, as those mechanisms are not present in this study.

- source: Alventosa, A., Antonioni, A., & Hernández, P. (2021). Pool punishment in public goods games: How do sanctioners? incentives affect us?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The ratchet-up mechanism (contributions cannot decrease) reduces cooperation rates compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. Adding a binding collective minimum contribution requirement (lowest common denominator) increases cooperation rates by about 25 percentage points compared to the ratchet-up mechanism alone, but does not reach the social optimum. A non-binding minimum has no significant effect. The difference is explained by conditional cooperation: non-compliance with the non-binding minimum leads to negative reciprocity and declining cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so findings are about contribution/cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public goods game with a ratchet-up mechanism, adding a binding collective minimum contribution requirement increases cooperation rates, while a non-binding minimum does not. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning means the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and results are highly relevant for understanding how minimum contribution rules (binding vs. non-binding) affect cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment per se.

- source: Zhang, Z., Zhang, X., & Putterman, L. (2019). Trust and cooperation at a confluence of worlds: An experiment in Xinjiang, China. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both Uyghur and Han students contribute more in public goods games when grouped with coethnics than with outgroup members, and least with anonymous group members. However, allowing face-to-face communication in mixed-ethnicity groups leads to the highest cooperation levels, even exceeding those in homogeneous groups. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution, trust, trustworthiness), and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed by condition. The results suggest that communication can be a stronger facilitator of cooperation than ethnic homogeneity, and that social identifiability (even of outgroup members) increases cooperation relative to anonymity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it provides informative evidence about the effects of group composition and communication on cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation levels in the absence of punishment, and for considering the effects of communication and social context as moderators. The absence of punishment means it cannot inform the treatment-control efficiency difference for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Morales, A. J., & Walker, J. M. (2018). Varying experimental instructions to improve comprehension: Punishment in public goods games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, mechanisms that allow individuals to preferentially imitate high-reputation neighbors (high reputation reasoning ability) promote the emergence and stability of cooperation. However, when reputation is updated using second-order rules (taking into account both the individual's and the worst neighbor's actions), cooperation is inhibited compared to first-order rules, especially when the magnitude of reputation change is large. Heterogeneity in reasoning ability (both endogenous and exogenous) can reduce cooperation, particularly when the variance or degree of heterogeneity is high. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate; average payoffs are discussed in relation to strategy and reputation clusters but not as a primary outcome. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation-based social learning and information structures affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution, and some average payoffs by type) are not directly translatable to group efficiency as defined in the prediction task. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive social mechanisms (like reputation) affect cooperation.

- source: Martinsson, P., Nam, P. K., & Villegas-Palacio, C. (2013). Conditional cooperation and disclosure in developing countries. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that public disclosure of individual contributions in a one-shot public goods game leads to a small, statistically insignificant increase in average unconditional contributions in both Colombia and Vietnam. The distribution of contributor types (free-riders, conditional cooperators, etc.) is largely stable across disclosure and no-disclosure treatments, with only minor shifts. The effect of disclosure is much smaller than the effects typically found for monetary punishment in the literature. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence that public disclosure (a form of social image incentive) has only a small and statistically insignificant effect on average contributions in highly collectivist developing country contexts. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the efficiency effects of punishment, but it does suggest that non-monetary social incentives (like disclosure) may have limited impact on contributions and, by extension, on efficiency in similar settings.

- source: Ranehill, E., Weber, R. A., & Wu, K. Y. (2024). Does redistribution affect cooperation and trust?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that neither progressive nor regressive redistribution policies have significant effects on cooperation, trust, or trustworthiness among group members, as measured in a public good game and a trust game. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes for the public good game. Exploratory analyses suggest some effects of redistribution on productivity in a real-effort task, but these are not the main focus and do not pertain to public good game efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes for the public good game. The design and findings are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Casari, M., & Plott, C. R. (2003). Decentralized management of common property resources: experiments with a centuries-old institution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a common-pool resource environment with 8 players and continuous contribution/appropriation choices, the addition of a decentralized punishment mechanism (where agents can pay to inspect and fine others for overuse, with fines paid to the inspector) leads to large increases in group efficiency. Without sanctions, efficiency is low (28.4%), below the Nash equilibrium prediction. With weak sanctions, efficiency rises to 57.2% gross (48.3% net after inspection costs). With strong sanctions, efficiency reaches 94% gross (76.9% net). The classical model cannot explain the effect of weak sanctions, but a model with heterogeneous, linear other-regarding preferences (spiteful and altruistic agents) fits the data well. The mechanism channels spiteful behavior into socially useful monitoring. The effect is robust across subject experience and is not due to repeated game effects or communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling decentralized punishment in a common-pool resource game (close variant of a public goods game) can substantially increase efficiency, even when the punishment is relatively weak and should not be effective under standard selfish Nash equilibrium predictions. The effect is robust to subject experience and is not driven by communication or repeated game effects. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are: player_count=8, num_rounds~27-33, no chat, continuous contribution, no default, non-linear MPCR (parabolic then exponential decay), punishment cost and tech specified, no reward, number of rounds not known in advance, summary info provided, and punishment identity not revealed. The control (no-sanction) efficiency is low (28.4%), and the treatment (punishment enabled) efficiency is much higher (48-76% net, 57-94% gross depending on punishment strength). This paper should be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency gains from punishment in similar decentralized, continuous-action, no-communication environments.
