- source: De Geest, L. R., & Stranlund, J. K. (2019). Defending public goods and common-pool resources. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 2x2 lab experiment (PG/CPR x Theft/No Theft), groups of four insiders played 15 rounds, with or without two outsiders who could steal surplus. Insiders could use costly sanctions (1:3 cost:impact) against outsiders. The main findings are: (a) In the CPR setting, the threat of theft increased cooperation and surplus creation, leading to higher group profits and surplus net of theft compared to the no-theft control. In the PG setting, the threat of theft did not significantly affect cooperation or surplus creation, and group profits decreased. (b) Sanctions were used more often in the PG setting, but were not effective at deterring outsider theft in either setting. (c) The presence of punishment did not restore efficiency to the fully cooperative benchmark, as insiders could not coordinate sanctions well enough to fully deter outsiders. (d) The introduction of theft increased variation in group outcomes, especially in the PG setting. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it does not reliably increase efficiency, and its effectiveness depends on the game structure and the ability of insiders to coordinate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with an external threat (outsider theft). The design allows for direct comparison of efficiency (group profit, surplus net of theft) with and without punishment enabled, under known game parameters. The results show that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency: in the CPR setting, the threat of theft (with punishment available) increases surplus creation and efficiency, but in the PG setting, efficiency decreases. In both cases, punishment is not effective at fully deterring theft, and group variation increases. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is contingent on the type of social dilemma (PG vs CPR), the presence of an external threat, and the group's ability to coordinate sanctions. Control efficiency (no-theft, no-punishment) is higher than treatment efficiency (theft, punishment) in the PG setting, but lower in the CPR setting. The paper is highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in environments with external threats and endogenous punishment.

- source: Vollan, B., Pröpper, M., Landmann, A., & Balafoutas, L. (2019). Self-Governance and Punishment: An Experimental Study among Namibian Forest Users. *Economic Development and Cultural Change*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a framed field/lab-in-the-field experiment with Namibian forest users, using a threshold public goods/extraction game with two punishment treatments: internal (peer) and external (random monitoring). Both punishment regimes reduced extraction compared to the baseline, but neither increased group or individual earnings (efficiency). Internal punishment was more effective at disciplining high extractors, especially those of low status, due to more targeted and severe peer punishment. However, internal punishment also led to high levels of antisocial punishment, which, together with the direct costs of punishment, resulted in lower overall earnings than both the external punishment and the no-punishment baseline. Ethnic heterogeneity increased antisocial punishment but did not reduce cooperation or prosocial punishment. The study concludes that while self-governance with peer punishment can discipline free riders, it may also generate substantial welfare losses due to costly and vengeful punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a threshold public goods/extraction game with both internal (peer) and external (centralized) punishment, the introduction of punishment mechanisms does not necessarily increase efficiency (group or individual earnings) and may even reduce it, especially when peer punishment is available. The main reason is that the costs of punishment (both direct and indirect, including antisocial punishment) can outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (5 players, 20 rounds, MPCR 0.4, threshold/tipping point, no chat, anonymous punishment, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when peer punishment is allowed. The effect may be less negative or neutral with centralized punishment, but still not positive. The findings also highlight the importance of group composition (status, ethnicity) and the potential for antisocial punishment to undermine efficiency gains.

- source: Casari, M., & Tavoni, A. (2024). Climate clubs in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares several treatments, including a baseline public goods game (PGG), a version with punishment (PGGwP), and a 'climate club' treatment that combines conditional cooperation and mandatory punishment. The key finding is that adding punishment opportunities (and counter-punishment) does not significantly increase cooperation rates and leads to a statistically significant decrease in net earnings (efficiency) compared to the baseline. The climate club treatment increases cooperation rates but still results in lower net earnings than the baseline, due to the costs associated with punishment. The results are robust across different group compositions and treatments. The paper provides direct evidence that, in this design, punishment reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a three-player, 30-round, binary-contribution public goods game with costly punishment and counter-punishment, enabling punishment reduces group efficiency (net earnings) compared to the control without punishment. The effect is robust and statistically significant. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, under similar design parameters (binary choice, no chat, known rounds, punishment cost per unit = 1, punishment effectiveness varies by target, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The paper also shows that the presence of counter-punishment does not mitigate this effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Stranlund, J. K., & Spraggon, J. M. (2017). Deterring poaching of a common pool resource. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to observe and punish outsiders (poachers) has a non-monotonic effect on efficiency. With zero monitoring (no punishment possible), outsiders show some restraint and insiders achieve moderate efficiency. With partial monitoring (randomly able to punish one outsider), poaching increases over time, sanctions are low and random, and efficiency for insiders drops to Nash equilibrium levels or below. With full monitoring (all outsiders observable and punishable), poaching is lower and more stable, insiders coordinate better, and efficiency is highest, though still far from the social optimum. Sanctions are generally insufficient to fully deter poaching. The results suggest that imperfect punishment opportunities can backfire, reducing efficiency, while full monitoring and punishment can improve but not maximize efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment in common-pool-resource-like games depends critically on the monitoring structure. If punishment is only partially possible (imperfect monitoring), efficiency can decrease relative to no punishment at all, due to increased poaching and poor coordination of sanctions. If punishment is fully enabled (full monitoring), efficiency increases relative to both the control and partial monitoring, but does not reach the social optimum. For prediction, the presence and effectiveness of punishment, the monitoring structure, and the ability to coordinate sanctions are crucial moderators. Control efficiency (no punishment) is not always a lower bound: enabling punishment can reduce efficiency if monitoring is imperfect. Game design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, communication, and information structure (who sees what) are all relevant. The results caution against assuming that adding punishment always increases efficiency.

- source: Ntuli, H., Crépin, A. S., Schill, C., & Muchapondwa, E. (2023). Sanctioned Quotas Versus Information Provisioning for Community Wildlife Conservation in Zimbabwe: A Framed Field Experiment Approach. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that introducing a sanctioned quota system (with costly punishment for exceeding quotas) leads to significantly higher efficiency in resource use compared to a baseline with no intervention. Information about ecological thresholds also improves efficiency, but not as much as punishment. Combining both interventions does not yield efficiency gains beyond punishment alone. The results are robust across multiple measures (efficiency, group payoff, resource stock, depletion cases) and are supported by regression analysis controlling for individual and group characteristics. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in CPR games with similar design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling punishment (in the form of sanctioned quotas with costly, probabilistic enforcement) increases efficiency in a dynamic, multi-round, continuous-harvest CPR game with communication allowed. The effect is substantial: average efficiency rises from 0.42 (baseline) to 0.71 (punishment) and 0.77 (punishment+information). The design closely matches public-goods-game-like environments, though the context is a renewable resource with dynamic feedbacks. The results suggest that, for similar games with 4 players, communication, and continuous contribution/harvest, enabling punishment is likely to yield a large positive effect on efficiency, even when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The effect is robust to the presence of communication and does not depend on end-game knowledge. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and punishment cost/tech are important, but the main result is that punishment is highly effective in this context.

- source: Ostrom, E. (2006). The value-added of laboratory experiments for the study of institutions and common-pool resources. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper reviews a series of laboratory CPR experiments, including baseline (no punishment), communication, exogenous and endogenous sanctioning, and exogenous rule imposition. In baseline (no punishment) games, groups achieve low efficiency (e.g., 37% or less of optimal). When costly punishment is enabled, groups increase their efficiency (e.g., up to 39% of optimal in one set, and up to 94% in another with strong sanctions), but the net gain is reduced by the cost of punishment. Communication and endogenous rule-making (where groups set their own rules and sanctions) lead to the highest efficiency (up to 93% of optimal). Exogenous rules with imperfect monitoring can crowd out cooperation and reduce efficiency. The effect of punishment is sensitive to its cost and effectiveness, and is most positive when combined with communication or endogenous agreement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in CPR-like games increases efficiency relative to control (no punishment), but the magnitude depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment. If punishment is costly and not highly effective, net efficiency gains may be modest or even offset by sanctioning costs. If punishment is strong (high impact per cost) or is combined with communication or endogenous rule-making, efficiency can approach optimal levels. For prediction, the presence and parameters of punishment (cost, tech), communication, and endogenous rule-making are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is typically low, and the introduction of punishment generally increases efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and/or combined with communication.

- source: Robbett, A. (2019). Just ask? Preference revelation and lying in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares four conditions in a repeated public goods game with private, randomly assigned types: (1) baseline voluntary contribution (VCM), (2) Mechanism (report type to a mechanism that enforces the socially optimal contribution for the reported type), (3) Revelation (report type to group members, then contribute voluntarily), and (4) Punishment (as Revelation, but with a costly peer punishment stage). The main findings are: (a) Mechanism and Revelation both increase cooperation and group efficiency relative to VCM, but Mechanism's effect fades over time; (b) Punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency compared to VCM, and group profits are actually lower in the Punishment condition; (c) Punishment is mainly used to sanction obviously dishonest reports, not under-contribution, and is too weak to incentivize higher contributions; (d) The presence of punishment undermines the positive effect of truthful reporting on cooperation seen in the Revelation condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with private, heterogeneous incentives and observable reports/contributions, adding a peer punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio) does not increase efficiency or group payoffs compared to a baseline without punishment. In fact, efficiency is lower with punishment, likely due to underutilization and mis-targeting of sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (3 players, 10 rounds, no chat, private types, punishment available after observing reports/contributions, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency relative to control. The results also highlight that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the clarity of norms and the ability to identify free-riders; when these are ambiguous due to private information, punishment may backfire or be ineffective.

- source: Robbett, A. (2016). Sustaining cooperation in heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with heterogeneous, private or observable incentives, peer punishment (sanctions) does not significantly increase group contributions or efficiency above the baseline. This holds both when group members' types are private and when they are observable. In the private information setting, punishment is rarely used except against unambiguous free-riders, and the magnitude is too low to change incentives. When types are observable, punishment is more accurately targeted but still fails to reliably increase contributions, as some high-demanders resist increasing their contributions even when punished. In contrast, both unrestricted communication and a centralized bonus/fine mechanism (Falkinger) substantially increase efficiency, with communication allowing groups to coordinate on the socially optimal contribution vector. The results suggest that punishment is a weak tool for promoting efficiency in heterogeneous groups, especially when contribution norms are ambiguous.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with heterogeneous and possibly private incentives, enabling peer punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff above the baseline. The effect of punishment is weak or null, even when punishment is relatively effective (3:1 impact/cost ratio) and types are observable. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar heterogeneous PGG-like environments, one should expect little or no increase in efficiency relative to the control. The results also highlight the importance of communication and centralized mechanisms as more effective alternatives. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 3 players, 10 rounds, no chat (in punishment treatments), continuous contributions, and a non-linear payoff structure with interior optima.

- source: Congleton, R. D., & Vanberg, V. J. (2001). Help, harm or avoid? On the personal advantage of dispositions to cooperate and punish in multilateral PD games with exit. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in three-person repeated joint production games with exit, conditional cooperation and the ability to exit dysfunctional teams allow for higher average payoffs (efficiency) than unconditional defection. However, in short-lived enterprises, targeted punishment of defectors is necessary to sustain cooperation and high payoffs. When targeted punishment is strong enough, even in short-lived games, cooperation and efficiency are restored. The results are robust in both static and evolutionary (replicator dynamic) simulations. The study provides direct evidence that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that enabling targeted punishment in small-group repeated public goods games with exit increases efficiency, especially when the number of rounds is small. The effect of punishment is more positive when the punishment is strong (high impact per cost) and can be targeted at defectors. The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of targeted punishment, the number of rounds, and the ability to exit are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher when exit and conditional cooperation are possible, but enabling punishment further increases efficiency, especially in short games. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Noailly, J., van den Bergh, J. C. J. M., & Withagen, C. A. (2009). Local and Global Interactions in an Evolutionary Resource Game. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local punishment mechanisms in a spatial CPR game allow for the emergence and persistence of cooperative and mixed-strategy equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and efficiency than the all-defector equilibrium. The frequency of efficient equilibria increases with higher sanction levels and larger population sizes, and spatial clustering of cooperators and enforcers is crucial for sustaining cooperation. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and local enforcement can overcome the limitations of non-spatial models, leading to more diverse and efficient outcomes. Sensitivity analysis shows that increasing the cost or effectiveness of sanctions, or increasing population size, generally increases the prevalence of efficient (cooperative or mixed) equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and aggregate payoff, especially when punishment is local and sufficiently strong. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, the introduction of local punishment will often lead to higher efficiency, particularly in larger groups and with higher sanction levels. The results also highlight the importance of spatial structure and local interactions, which may moderate the effect of punishment compared to non-spatial models. The paper's parameter sensitivity analysis can inform predictions about how changes in punishment cost, effectiveness, and group size affect the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Noailly, J., Withagen, C. A., & van den Bergh, J. C. J. M. (2007). Spatial evolution of social norms in a common-pool resource game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a spatial CPR game, the introduction of enforcers (who punish defectors) leads to the emergence and persistence of cooperative equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and more efficient resource use. The frequency of such efficient equilibria increases with the sanction rate (punishment effectiveness) and is further enhanced when resource dynamics (feedback from resource depletion) are included. Simulations show that, for a wide range of parameters, punishment increases the likelihood of equilibria with cooperation and higher payoffs, compared to scenarios without punishment. The spatial structure (local punishment) is crucial for these results, as it allows cooperators and enforcers to protect each other and sustain cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in a spatial CPR (public goods-like) game increases the frequency of efficient, high-payoff equilibria, especially when the punishment is sufficiently strong and resource feedbacks are present. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with nontrivial cost and effectiveness), local interaction, and resource dynamics should be expected to increase treatment efficiency relative to control. The results are most directly applicable to settings with local punishment, spatial structure, and evolutionary imitation dynamics, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is robust across parameter sweeps. The paper does not provide precise quantitative predictions for efficiency, but shows that the effect is substantial and positive under a wide range of conditions.

- source: Kol'veková, G., Raisová, M., Zoricak, M., & Gazda, V. (2021). Endogenous Shared Punishment Model in Threshold Public Goods Games. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The endogenous shared punishment model, where agents vote on the level and cost of punishment, achieves higher mean payoffs (efficiency) than both the baseline (no punishment) and the cooperation (full punishment) models. In the standard scenario (group size 4, MPCR 0.5, threshold 60, endowment 30), the endogenous shared punishment model yields a weighted mean reward of 40.09, compared to 33.42 for the baseline and 35.04 for the cooperation model. The provision rate is 73.8% for the endogenous shared punishment model, 24.7% for the baseline, and 98.2% for the cooperation model. The endogenous shared punishment model achieves this efficiency at a very low cost of punishment, as pro-punishment votes and punishment costs decline over time as cooperation stabilizes. Adding 'activists' (altruistic contributors) further increases provision rates and mean payoffs. The model's results are robust to variations in group size, threshold, MPCR, and learning parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a low-cost, endogenous, shared punishment mechanism in threshold public goods games can substantially increase efficiency (mean group payoff) compared to both no-punishment and full-punishment regimes. The efficiency gains are achieved even when the provision rate is not maximized, due to the low cost of punishment and the stabilization of cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in TPGG-like environments, especially when punishment is endogenous, low-cost, and shared. The results are most applicable to games with group size 4, MPCR 0.5, and threshold 60, but the model explores a range of parameters. The paper's agent-based approach and focus on payoff outcomes make it highly informative for the prediction task.

- source: Sethi, R., & Somanathan, E. (2003). Understanding reciprocity. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment and reciprocity can support efficient outcomes in both repeated and sporadic interactions, but only under certain conditions. In repeated games, strategies that support cooperation (and thus efficiency) can be stable if complexity costs or implementation errors are considered, but many inefficient strategies can also be stable. In one-shot or sporadic interactions, punishment can induce cooperation and improve efficiency, but this stability is fragile: populations of punishers can be invaded by non-punishing cooperators or defectors, and the cost of punishment can erode its effectiveness. In public goods and common pool resource games, the presence of reciprocators willing to punish can lead to higher group payoffs, but only if monitoring and punishment costs are not too high and if group composition allows reciprocators to coordinate. The evolution of mixed populations (reciprocators and defectors) is common, and the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the game structure and population dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines, but only under specific conditions: punishment must be sufficiently effective (high impact per cost), not too costly, and the population must contain enough reciprocators or the ability to assort. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive; in some models, punishment does not prevent the evolution of inefficient outcomes, and the stability of efficient cooperation is sensitive to the cost and observability of punishment, as well as to the possibility of second-order free-riding. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and the ability to observe or assort by type. Control efficiency (without punishment) is often low, but the introduction of punishment can raise efficiency if these moderating conditions are met.

- source: Khadjavi, M., Lange, A., & Nicklisch, A. (2017). How transparency may corrupt - experimental evidence from asymmetric public goods games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an asymmetric public goods game (one official can embezzle, others can only contribute), transparency alone (without punishment) increases embezzlement by the official and reduces public good provision. Introducing a peer punishment stage (where group members can spend resources to reduce others' payoffs) increases public good provision and curbs embezzlement, but only if the official's identity is known. When both identity and actions are transparent (high transparency), punishment is more accurately targeted, reducing stigmatization and further increasing cooperation. Mean income is higher in punishment treatments, and income inequality between officials and citizens is reduced when punishment is possible and the official's identity is known.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling peer punishment in a public goods game with asymmetric roles (embezzlement possible for one player) increases group payoff and public good provision, especially when the official's identity is known. The effect is robust across both low and high transparency, but high transparency reduces erroneous punishment (stigmatization) and allows more targeted sanctions. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio), group size of 4, 10 rounds, and no communication, is associated with a substantial increase in efficiency-related outcomes compared to control. The effect is strongest when both identity and actions are transparent, but even low transparency with punishment is effective. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower under high transparency due to increased embezzlement. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in asymmetric PGG-like environments with similar design features.

- source: Guillen, P., Schwieren, C., & Staffiero, G. (2007). Why feed the Leviathan?. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that a centralized sanctioning mechanism (CSM) in a step-level public good game leads to a dramatic increase in the rate at which groups successfully provide the public good (i.e., reach the threshold). In the baseline (no CSM), groups almost never reach the threshold after a few rounds. With CSM, the threshold is reached in nearly 90% of rounds. After CSM is removed, groups with prior CSM experience continue to reach the threshold at much higher rates than baseline groups (AR: 65%, VR: 48%, BL: 0% in rounds 8-14). The effect is attributed to an 'educational' or coordination-facilitating role of CSM. Voting on CSM removal does not improve post-removal outcomes compared to automatic removal. Failures to reach the threshold during the CSM phase predict lower post-CSM success. The CSM is costly (3 units per player per round), so while it increases the likelihood of public good provision, it also reduces per-round payoffs when active. The paper does not directly report efficiency ratios, but the increased provision rate implies higher group welfare relative to baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a centralized punishment mechanism in a threshold (step-level) public good game increases the rate of successful public good provision, both during and after the punishment phase. The effect persists even after punishment is removed, supporting the idea of an 'educational' or coordination effect. The CSM is costly, so the net efficiency gain depends on the balance between increased public good provision and the cost of the mechanism. For prediction, the presence of a centralized punishment mechanism should be expected to substantially increase efficiency (proxied by provision rate) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially in step-level games with coordination problems. The effect is robust to whether removal is automatic or by vote. However, the paper does not provide direct efficiency ratios, so predictions must use provision rate as a proxy.

- source: Chavez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2025). Social context, framing, and compliance with the law: experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that higher monitoring probabilities increase compliance in all treatments. Law framing increases compliance only in the strategic (public good) environment, and actually decreases compliance in the non-strategic environment where the law does not benefit others. Compliance is not higher when noncompliance imposes an externality on third parties. Compliance is higher in public good (strategic) environments than in non-strategic ones, especially when monitoring is weak. The main outcome is compliance, not efficiency or group payoff, so the results are most directly informative about behavioral responses to punishment rather than efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how punishment (monitoring and sanctions) affects compliance rates in public goods game-like environments, especially under different social framings. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and results are highly relevant for predicting compliance behavior, and the strategic (public good) treatments are close analogs to standard PGGs with punishment. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective at increasing compliance (and thus likely efficiency) when the public good benefits group members, especially under weak enforcement. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Selles, J., Bonhommeau, S., Guillotreau, P., & Vallee, T. (2020). Can the Threat of Economic Sanctions Ensure the Sustainability of International Fisheries? An Experiment of a Dynamic Non-cooperative CPR Game with Uncertain Tipping Point. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing an endogenous economic sanction (a fixed cost triggered by resource depletion below a threshold) in a dynamic CPR game leads to more cooperative behavior, less overexploitation, and higher average resource stocks compared to a baseline with no sanction. The effect is even stronger when the location of the threshold is uncertain. However, while sanctions reduce the frequency of myopic (selfish) strategies, many groups still fail to avoid the threshold, and only a few achieve near-optimal resource management. Average group profit is lower and more variable in sanction treatments due to the high penalty for crossing the threshold, but overall, the threat of sanctions shifts behavior toward greater cooperation and less rent dissipation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (here, an economic sanction triggered by overexploitation) in a dynamic, non-cooperative CPR game increases cooperative behavior and reduces overexploitation, which should translate into higher efficiency relative to the baseline. However, the effect on group profit is nuanced: while sanctions reduce overexploitation, groups that cross the threshold incur large losses, and only a minority achieve near-optimal outcomes. For prediction, the presence of a punishment/sanction mechanism is likely to increase efficiency on average, but the effect may be heterogeneous depending on group behavior and the risk of triggering the sanction. The game design here is a dynamic CPR with 3 players, no chat, continuous contribution (harvest), and a punishment that is endogenous and collective (not peer-to-peer). The findings are most informative for similar dynamic CPR or threshold public goods settings.

- source: Hayo, B., & Vollan, B. (2012). Group interaction, heterogeneity, rules, and co-operative behaviour: Evidence from a common-pool resource experiment in South Africa and Namibia. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: This field-lab experiment in rural South Africa and Namibia used a repeated common-pool resource game with 5-person groups over 20 rounds. After 10 rounds, groups voted to implement one of three rules: penalty (punishment), reward, or communication. The introduction of any rule led to a significant reduction in resource extraction (i.e., more cooperation), but there was no significant difference between the effects of penalty, reward, or communication rules on extraction levels after accounting for group dynamics. Group composition (e.g., gender, wealth heterogeneity) and dynamic social interactions had larger effects on cooperation than the specific institutional rule. Payoff-related outcomes (individual and group payoffs) were analyzed as predictors of behavior, but efficiency (group payoff relative to optimum) was not directly reported. The main behavioral outcome is the number of sheep chosen, which is inversely related to cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment (penalty) rule in a repeated common-pool resource game with 5 players and 20 rounds improves cooperation (reduces extraction), thus likely increasing efficiency relative to the control (no-rule) condition. However, the effect of punishment is not significantly different from reward or communication rules after accounting for group dynamics. The efficiency gain from punishment is thus positive, but the magnitude is similar to that of other rule-based interventions. Group composition and social context are strong moderators. For prediction, the presence of a punishment rule should be expected to increase efficiency compared to no rule, but the effect size may be modest and context-dependent.

- source: Kornhauser, L., Lu, Y. J., & Tontrup, S. (2020). Testing a fine is a price in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing a fine for non-compliance (either small or proportional) reduces compliance (performance) among pro-social subjects (crowding-out effect) and increases it among pro-self subjects (price effect). When the fine is framed as a penalty (collected by the experimenter, not the partner), the crowding-out effect disappears and pro-socials' compliance is restored. The overall mean performance across all subjects does not differ significantly between treatments with and without fines, due to the offsetting effects. The study provides strong evidence for the fine-is-a-price mechanism as the cause of crowding out, but does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment/fines on compliance in public-goods-like environments depends on the social value orientation of participants: fines crowd out compliance among pro-socials but increase it among pro-selfs. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that the net effect of punishment on efficiency may be neutral in mixed populations, but could be negative in highly pro-social groups and positive in highly pro-self groups. The design and manipulation of punishment (including framing as price vs. penalty) are critical moderators. For prediction tasks, the absence of explicit efficiency data limits direct applicability, but the behavioral patterns are informative for understanding potential efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Dave, C., Hamre, S., Kephart, C., & Reuben, A. (2020). Subjects in the lab, activists in the field: public goods and punishment. *Eurasian Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both lab and field (activist) subjects respond to incentives in a public goods context with punishment. Activists are more likely to punish non-cooperative behavior, even when it is costly and probabilistic, and are more likely to settle at the Nash equilibrium when firms increase their public good contributions. Higher MPCR leads to higher contributions and more frequent punishment. The main behavioral drivers are inequity aversion and negative reciprocity. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome, so the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how punishment is used in a public-goods-like environment, especially with a costly, probabilistic punishment mechanism. It shows that punishment is frequently used and is responsive to MPCR and fairness concerns. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the likelihood of punishment under different design parameters (e.g., MPCR, subject pool), but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Makowsky, M. D., & Wang, S. Y. (2018). Embezzlement, whistleblowing, and organizational architecture: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment with 6-player groups, participants played a common pool resource game with a whistleblowing option: after each withdrawal, any non-first-mover could choose to 'blow the whistle', setting all group payoffs to zero. The game was played under six different organizational structures, varying the number of sequential levels (from 1 to 6) and the arrangement of players (pyramid, inverted pyramid, flat, etc.). The main findings are: (1) Embezzlement rates are high in all structures, but increase with the number of sequential levels; (2) The probability of group-level punishment (whistleblowing) increases with the number of levels; (3) Average group income (efficiency) decreases as the number of levels increases; (4) Flatter (simultaneous) structures have higher efficiency than taller (sequential) ones; (5) Pyramid structures have lower embezzlement than inverted pyramids, but this does not translate into significant efficiency differences due to similar rates of group-level punishment. The punishment mechanism is highly costly and does not improve efficiency; rather, it reduces it further when triggered.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with a costly, group-level punishment mechanism (whistleblowing that sets all payoffs to zero), enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may reduce it, especially as the game becomes more sequential (taller). The efficiency loss is driven by both high embezzlement and the destructive nature of the punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, when the control (no-punishment) game already has low efficiency due to high embezzlement, adding a highly destructive punishment option (like whistleblowing) is likely to further reduce efficiency, particularly in games with more sequential moves or more levels. The design dimensions most relevant are the number of players, number of rounds, the structure of play (sequential vs. simultaneous), and the nature/cost of punishment. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is costly and group-level (not targeted), and less so to standard peer-to-peer punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Diekert, F., Eymess, T., Luomba, J., & Waichman, I. (2022). The Creation of Social Norms under Weak Institutions. *Journal of the Association of Environmental and Resource Economists*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that providing social information about high or low cooperation rates only leads to stable differences in cooperation when a weak social sanctioning institution is present. With sanctioning, cooperation rates remain high if cooperation is emphasized and low if defection is emphasized; without sanctioning, cooperation declines over time regardless of the message. The sanctioning mechanism is a group vote to exclude a strategy from a small bonus, and is used infrequently. The effect of social information and sanctioning is stronger for participants with higher social proximity to others in their session. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and beliefs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a (weak) social sanctioning institution is necessary for norm-based interventions (social information) to create stable high cooperation in repeated binary social dilemma games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is close to a public goods game (binary prisoner's dilemma with group returns), and the sanctioning is a group-level exclusion from a small bonus, not direct costly punishment. The findings suggest that, in similar binary games with weak sanctioning, punishment can stabilize cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. The results are most useful for predicting behavioral (cooperation) outcomes rather than efficiency.

- source: Solda, A., & Villeval, M. C. (2020). EXCLUSION AND REINTEGRATION IN A SOCIAL DILEMMA. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment investigates endogenous and exogenous exclusion (ostracism) in a club-good setting where group members can vote to exclude others who over-extract from the group resource. Exclusion is primarily targeted at those who withdraw more than the group average, supporting the idea that exclusion enforces group norms. Upon reintegration, previously excluded members tend to reduce their extraction, indicating some deterrent or corrective effect of exclusion. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total group payoff, or welfare, so the effect of exclusion on efficiency cannot be directly assessed. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses to exclusion and the use of ostracism as a sanction, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the behavioral effects of exclusion (ostracism) as a sanctioning mechanism in public-good-like environments. It shows that exclusion can deter norm violations (over-extraction) and that reintegrated members may adjust their behavior. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment (exclusion) on efficiency. The design dimensions (e.g., group size, exclusion rules) are described, but their effects are only analyzed in terms of behavioral outcomes, not efficiency. Thus, this paper is useful for understanding the mechanisms and behavioral pathways by which exclusion might affect efficiency, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Eckel, C., & Gintis, H. (2010). Blaming the messenger: Notes on the current state of experimental economics. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that a large body of experimental evidence supports the existence of other-regarding preferences, such as altruistic punishment, in games like the public goods game with punishment. It reviews findings that low contributors are frequently punished, even in late rounds and in stranger treatments, indicating that punishment is used to enforce cooperation. The authors also discuss the robustness of these behaviors to changes in stakes and context, and the importance of social norms. However, the paper does not present new data or directly analyze efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the behavioral mechanisms and theoretical implications.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and robustness of punishment and cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but does not allow for direct inference about efficiency or payoff changes.

- source: van der Heijden, E., Potters, J., & Sefton, M. (2009). Hierarchy and opportunism in teams. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares two institutions: equal revenue-sharing (control) and leader-determined shares (treatment, where one member can allocate output). In the control, free-riding leads to declining effort and efficiency, with teams achieving on average 61% of maximum possible earnings. In the leader treatment, most leaders reward contributors and exclude shirkers, resulting in average team earnings of 83% of maximum possible. The presence of a leader thus leads to a significant and substantial improvement in efficiency and group payoff. However, the benefit depends on the leader's behavior: teams with unfair or opportunistic leaders perform poorly. The results are explained by a reputation model where leaders may act fairly to sustain cooperation, especially in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a central authority with reward power (akin to a 'punishment' or sanctioning mechanism, though framed as reward) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, provided the leader uses their power to reward contributors and punish shirkers. The effect is robust in a repeated, all-or-nothing contribution game with four players and perfect monitoring. For prediction, the presence of a leader with discretionary allocation power should be expected to increase efficiency relative to equal sharing, especially when the leader can observe and condition rewards on individual contributions. However, the effect depends on the leader's willingness to use their power to enforce cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented via exclusion from rewards rather than explicit costly punishment, but the mechanism is closely related.

- source: Billinger, S., & Rosenbaum, S. M. (2019). Discretionary mechanisms and cooperation in hierarchies: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares a baseline PGG (no hierarchy) to four HPGG treatments varying in whether managers can contribute (discretionary contribution: present/absent) and whether managers can allocate returns (discretionary return: static/dynamic). All HPGG treatments yield higher average contributions and accumulated earnings than the baseline, indicating that hierarchy and managerial discretion can sustain cooperation and improve efficiency. However, when managers have full discretion over returns but cannot contribute (AD treatment), both contributions and worker earnings are lowest among HPGG treatments, showing that unchecked managerial appropriation undermines efficiency. When managers can contribute, both group earnings and cooperation are higher and more stable over time. Managers consistently earn more than workers, especially in dynamic return treatments, due to 'strategic luring'—managers initially reward workers to elicit contributions, then appropriate more in later rounds. The design of discretionary mechanisms thus crucially shapes both efficiency and the distribution of surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing hierarchical mechanisms (managerial discretion over inputs and/or outputs) in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency relative to a standard PGG baseline. However, the efficiency gains depend on the specific design: allowing managers to contribute and/or restricting their ability to appropriate returns leads to higher and more sustained efficiency. If managers can appropriate returns without contributing, efficiency and worker payoffs suffer. For prediction, the presence of hierarchy and the structure of managerial discretion are key moderators: enabling managerial contribution and limiting appropriation both support higher efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially in settings with hierarchical or leader-follower structures.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Nosenzo, D., & Sefton, M. (2017). Team incentives and leadership. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Introducing a leader with discretionary reward power in a repeated team production game (a close variant of the public goods game) increases group efficiency from about 30-33% to 72-76% of the maximum possible. Leaders use their power to reward high contributors and withhold rewards from low contributors, effectively incentivizing effort. This effect holds for both homogeneous and heterogeneous teams. However, the distribution of gains is highly unequal, with leaders capturing most of the surplus. When workers can choose how much power to delegate to the leader, they typically restrict it to less than half the team output, and the potential efficiency gains are not fully realized. The main reason is that workers do not benefit much from leadership, as leaders only compensate them for effort costs and keep most of the residual output.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VCM-like team production games, introducing a central authority with discretionary reward power (a form of positive sanctioning) can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when the leader is self-interested. The effect is robust to team heterogeneity. However, the efficiency gains are distributed unequally, and when delegation is endogenous, reluctance to empower the leader limits the realized gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader with reward power (as opposed to equal sharing) should be expected to increase efficiency substantially, but the effect may be moderated if the leader's power is limited or if workers can restrict delegation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where the 'punishment' dimension is implemented as positive reward power rather than costly punishment, but the mechanism (centralized sanctioning) is closely related.

- source: Cox, C. A., & Stoddard, B. (2024). Inequality and the allocation of collective goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment compares standard linear public goods games (equal-share) to treatments where a third-party allocator divides the collective good among contributors. The allocator mechanism increases efficiency in all cases, but the effect is strongest in homogeneous groups. When group members differ in endowments or productivity, the allocator's ability to boost efficiency is significantly diminished, though still positive relative to the equal-share baseline. Allocators generally reward contributors in proportion to their relative effort (percentage of endowment contributed), but in heterogeneous groups, conflicting fairness ideals make it harder to sustain high efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that flexible allocation mechanisms can substitute for punishment/reward institutions in promoting efficiency, but their effectiveness is moderated by group heterogeneity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for flexible allocation of the collective good (by a third-party allocator) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when groups are homogeneous. However, the efficiency boost is reduced in the presence of heterogeneity in endowments or productivity. For prediction tasks, the presence of an allocator (as a form of centralized sanctioning/reward) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to equal-share baselines, but the magnitude of the effect will be smaller in heterogeneous groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with allocation mechanisms, and indirectly relevant for understanding the limits of punishment/reward institutions in heterogeneous environments.

- source: Deffains, B., Espinosa, R., & Fluet, C. (2019). Laws and norms: Experimental evidence with liability rules. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a 'Liability Game' that is structurally similar to a public goods game but with a binary choice (harmful vs harmless action) and state-dependent payoffs, so that sometimes the harmful action is efficient and sometimes not. The main treatments compare no law (no liability), strict liability, and negligence rule, each with perfect (Severe) or imperfect (Mild, 50% probability) enforcement. The key findings are: (1) Both strict liability and negligence rules increase efficiency relative to no law, even when enforcement is imperfect and sanctions are not fully deterrent. (2) Strict liability is more effective than the negligence rule at deterring welfare-decreasing actions under imperfect enforcement, but also deters some welfare-increasing actions (over-deterrence). (3) The presence of liability rules increases the weight individuals place on group welfare in their decision-making, beyond the direct material incentives. (4) Social disapproval (punishment) is mainly targeted at those who cause harm, regardless of whether the action was efficient or inefficient. (5) Informal sanctions (disapproval points) further reduce harmful actions, but the main efficiency gains come from the liability rules themselves.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment-like liability rule in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency, even when enforcement is imperfect and the expected sanction is not fully deterrent. The effect is robust across two types of liability rules (strict and negligence), but strict liability is more effective at deterring welfare-decreasing actions (at the cost of some over-deterrence). The game design is a close variant of a PGG: 4 players, 10 rounds per treatment, binary choice, no chat, and state-dependent payoffs. The efficiency gains are not solely due to material deterrence but also to increased salience of group welfare (norm activation). For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment (liability) will increase efficiency relative to control, even if punishment is not fully effective or is only probabilistically applied. The magnitude of the effect may depend on whether the punishment is strict (always for harm) or negligence-based (only for inefficient harm), and on the enforcement probability.

- source: Vyrastekova, J., & van Soest, D. (2008). On the (in)effectiveness of rewards in sustaining cooperation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: In a repeated CPR game with groups of 5 over 15 rounds, the introduction of net positive rewards (1:3 impact ratio) leads to significantly lower extraction (closer to the social optimum) and higher CPR-stage efficiency compared to both the unregulated baseline and the transfer reward (1:1) treatment. Transfer rewards do not affect extraction or efficiency. However, total group efficiency (including the reward stage) is similar across treatments, as the efficiency gains in the CPR stage with net positive rewards are offset by welfare losses from not using all reward tokens. The study demonstrates that the effectiveness of rewards in sustaining cooperation and improving efficiency depends critically on the reward's impact ratio.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of peer-based incentives in public-goods-like environments depends on the technology of the incentive: net positive rewards (where the recipient's gain exceeds the giver's cost) can increase efficiency, while transfer rewards (1:1) do not. Although the study uses a CPR game (public bad) rather than a standard public goods game, the structure is closely related. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling a reward mechanism, the reward impact ratio (reward_tech) is a critical moderator. The absence of punishment means the results are not directly about punishment, but the logic may transfer to the design of positive incentives. For prediction, the control (no-reward) efficiency is not improved by transfer rewards, but is improved by net positive rewards in the CPR stage. However, total efficiency may not increase if the reward mechanism itself is not fully utilized.

- source: Corgnet, B. (2012). PEER EVALUATIONS AND TEAM PERFORMANCE: WHEN FRIENDS DO WORSE THAN STRANGERS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that using peer evaluations to allocate team profits in a real-effort team task leads to lower team performance (measured as a percentage of maximum possible profits) than an equal split rule. This effect is statistically significant and is stronger in teams where members are acquaintances or friends. The mechanism appears to involve a crowding-out of intrinsic motivation and increased negative reciprocity when peer evaluations are used among friends. The majority of peer assessments follow equality norms, but a significant minority use equity (contribution-based) norms. The results suggest that peer evaluation mechanisms may not always improve efficiency, especially in contexts with strong interpersonal relationships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small real-effort team settings with face-to-face communication and high social proximity, introducing a peer evaluation mechanism for allocating payoffs (a form of endogenous sanction/reward) can reduce efficiency compared to a fixed equal split. This is contrary to standard public goods game findings where punishment or reward options often increase efficiency. The negative effect is especially pronounced when team members are friends or classmates. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of introducing peer-based sanction/reward mechanisms on efficiency is context-dependent: in environments with high social ties and communication, such mechanisms may reduce efficiency relative to control. The findings are most relevant for small teams with real-effort tasks, face-to-face interaction, and endogenous allocation of payoffs via peer assessment.

- source: Croson, R., Fatas, E., Neugebauer, T., & Morales, A. J. (2015). Excludability: A laboratory study on forced ranking in team production. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that excludability (the ability to exclude the lowest contributor from group benefits) dramatically increases both contributions and efficiency in linear public goods (VCM) and best-shot (BSM) games, but not in weakest-link (WLM) games. In the VCM, efficiency rises from 65% (baseline) to 83% (excludability) and 99% (excludability with redistribution). In the BSM, efficiency rises from 81% (baseline) to 86% (excludability), but redistribution reduces efficiency to 75%. In the WLM, excludability alone does not improve efficiency (52% in both baseline and excludability), but redistribution increases efficiency to 72%. Thus, the effect of excludability on efficiency is highly dependent on the production function, and redistribution is necessary for efficiency gains in environments with strong complementarities (WLM).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency depends critically on the production function. In standard linear public goods games (VCM), enabling exclusion of the lowest contributor increases efficiency substantially, and redistribution of the excluded share further boosts efficiency. In weakest-link games, exclusion alone does not improve efficiency, but redistribution is necessary for efficiency gains. In best-shot games, exclusion increases efficiency, but redistribution actually reduces it. For prediction tasks, the production function (average, minimum, or maximum) is a key moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment/exclusion. Control efficiency is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of exclusion is highly context-dependent.

- source: Gaudeul, A., Crosetto, P., & Riener, G. (2017). Better stuck together or free to go? Of the stability of cooperation when individuals have outside options. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment varied the ease of exit from a public project (barriers to exit) in a repeated, two-player, stochastic public-good-like game. When exit was easier (lower barriers), participants exited more frequently, but this did not increase efficiency or average payoffs. In fact, average individual welfare was lower in treatments with lower barriers to exit. The main reason was excessive exit, driven by pessimistic beliefs about the peer's likelihood of leaving, rather than accurate cost-benefit analysis. The study finds that exit is used both as a response to perceived exploitation and as a punishment for failure, but these mechanisms do not improve group efficiency. The findings suggest that making exit easier undermines group stability and reduces welfare, contrary to some theoretical expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-good-like environments with imperfect monitoring and stochastic outcomes, making exit easier (i.e., lowering barriers to exit) reduces efficiency and average payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of exit options (a form of sanction or punishment via exclusion/self-exclusion) on efficiency. The results suggest that, unless exit is tightly controlled or costly, its availability will likely decrease group welfare due to excessive and inefficient use. For prediction tasks, the presence and ease of exit should be treated as a negative moderator of efficiency, even when the control (no-exit) game already has moderate efficiency. The study does not test classic costly punishment (e.g., deduction points), but its findings on exit as a sanction are highly informative for settings where exit or exclusion is a possible response to non-cooperation.

- source: Kusterer, D. J., & Schmitz, P. W. (2020). Public goods, property rights, and investment incentives: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that ownership structure affects investment incentives and efficiency in public-good provision, but not always as predicted by theory. When the investor values the public good more, giving them ownership increases investment and group surplus. When the non-investor values the public good more, giving them ownership does not increase investment or surplus, because the investor-owner sometimes withholds the public good to punish the other party, reducing efficiency. When the possibility of such punishment is removed (by exogenously enforcing provision), investment and efficiency fall in line with theoretical predictions. Thus, the efficiency effect of ownership and the potential for punishment are intertwined, and the ability to punish by withholding provision can reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effect of institutional design (ownership allocation) in public-good games depends on whether players can punish by withholding provision. In environments where the owner can withhold the public good as punishment, efficiency may be lower than predicted by standard theory, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is high. If the design disables such punishment (e.g., by enforcing provision), efficiency aligns with theoretical predictions. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of punishment opportunities (even if not explicit costly punishment) is a key moderator. The findings are most relevant for two-player, sequential, bargaining-based public-good games with binary investment and provision decisions.

- source: Bolle, F., & Vogel, C. (2011). Power comes with responsibility-or does it?. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Transferring responsibility to a single allocator who determines the contributions of all group members leads to higher average group income (efficiency) than the standard public goods game with voluntary contributions. This effect is observed both when the allocator is randomly assigned and when elected, though the difference between these modes is small. Over repeated phases, allocators become more selfish, reducing their own contributions, but group income remains higher than in the standard PG game. No direct punishment or reward mechanism is present, but the allocator's power to assign contributions functions as a form of enforced cooperation. The study does not find evidence for indirect punishment (allocators do not systematically penalize previous free riders).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public goods environment, shifting from voluntary contribution to a regime where one player has the power to assign contributions (i.e., a strong form of centralized enforcement) increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to the standard game. While this is not a punishment mechanism per se, it is adjacent to the prediction task because it shows that enforced contributions (even without explicit punishment costs) can improve efficiency. The findings suggest that, in settings where voluntary cooperation is inefficient, introducing a mechanism for enforced contributions (or strong centralized control) can increase group payoff. However, the absence of explicit punishment or cost for enforcement means the results are not directly transferable to standard punishment treatments, but they are informative for understanding the upper bound of efficiency gains from strong enforcement.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., Puerto, S., & Velez, M. A. (2021). Regrouping to Reduce Overfishing: Evidence from a Series of Lab-in-the-Field Experiments in Mexico. *Marine Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a framed CPR lab-in-the-field experiment with real fishermen, regrouping participants by their past extraction levels (publicly ranking and then reassigning groups) leads to a significant reduction in individual and group extraction, both in the observation period (after announcement) and after regrouping. The effect is strongest for the most sustainable group but is present across all groups. Compliance with the quota increases, especially in the most sustainable group. Other interventions (social disapproval, venting, moral priming) do not reduce extraction. The control group shows no reduction in extraction.
