The results suggest that symbolic recognition and group reassignment can reduce overexploitation and improve compliance, indirectly improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that regrouping (a form of symbolic reward and group reassignment based on past behavior) can reduce overexploitation in CPR games, even in field-relevant settings. However, it does not test costly punishment or direct sanctioning mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of non-costly, symbolic, or reputational interventions rather than monetary punishment. The game design is close to a public goods game but is a CPR extraction game. Efficiency is not directly measured, but reductions in extraction and increased compliance imply improved group welfare. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that interventions leveraging social recognition and group identity can have positive effects on efficiency, especially when punishment is weak or absent.

- source: Stoddard, B. V., Cox, C. A., & Walker, J. M. (2021). Incentivizing provision of collective goods: Allocation rules. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: In a lab experiment with repeated collective good provision games, the introduction of a third-party allocator who can flexibly assign shares of the group fund (based on observed contributions) leads to higher group provision than a baseline with equal sharing. The two most flexible allocator mechanisms (PA-Flex and PA-Rules) both result in significantly higher provision than the baseline (PE-PE), and also outperform a less flexible allocator mechanism (PA-SWW). The highest levels of provision are observed in the most flexible mechanism (PA-Flex), though a small number of groups with ineffective allocators show low provision. The study does not directly report efficiency as a normalized payoff ratio, but group provision is closely related and higher provision implies higher group payoffs. The findings indicate that flexible allocation rules that allow for contribution-contingent rewards and punishments can strongly increase collective good provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where a third-party allocator can flexibly assign shares of the collective good based on contributions, group provision (and thus efficiency) increases substantially compared to equal sharing. The effect is robust across two flexible allocator mechanisms and is strongest when the allocator has maximal flexibility. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of introducing a flexible, contribution-contingent allocation mechanism (a form of endogenous reward/punishment), one should expect a large positive effect on group efficiency, provided the allocator uses the mechanism effectively. However, the effect may be heterogeneous if some allocators do not use the mechanism to incentivize contributions. The study does not use classic peer punishment, but the allocation mechanism serves a similar incentive function. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where a third party can allocate payoffs after observing contributions, rather than direct peer-to-peer punishment.

- source: Angelovski, A., Neugebauer, T., & Servátka, M. (2019). RANK-ORDER COMPETITION IN THE VOLUNTARY PROVISION OF IMPURE PUBLIC GOODS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Rank-Order-VCM, which rewards higher contributors with a larger share of the public good (via a higher personal multiplier), leads to significantly higher and more sustained contributions than a control treatment with random assignment of multipliers. In Rank-Order-VCM, median contributions are at the maximum in almost all rounds, while in the control, contributions decline to near zero. The mechanism avoids the efficiency loss of fixed-prize contests by not subtracting a prize from the pool. The results suggest that introducing rank-based competition for access to an impure public good can mitigate free-riding and move outcomes closer to the social optimum, increasing group welfare and implied efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based competition mechanism (where higher contributors receive a larger share of the public good) can dramatically increase contributions and implied efficiency compared to a control with random assignment of benefits. While the main reported outcomes are contributions, the payoff structure ensures that higher contributions translate into higher group payoffs and efficiency, especially since no costly prize is subtracted from the pool. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which create endogenous, contribution-dependent rewards (even without explicit punishment or costly rewards) can outperform standard VCMs and fixed-prize contests in terms of efficiency. However, the results are most directly applicable to impure public goods with preferential access, not to standard PGGs with explicit punishment or peer-sanctioning. The control efficiency (with random assignment) is much lower than in the treatment, so the predicted treatment efficiency should be substantially higher under similar design parameters.

- source: McIntosh, C., Sadoulet, E., Buck, S., & Rosada, T. (2013). Reputation in a public goods game: Taking the design of credit bureaus to the lab. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms (used for reassigning ejected players) increase average contributions in a repeated public goods game with ejection, compared to a random reassignment baseline. The effect size is about a 7.5% increase in average contribution (from ~75% to ~82% of the endowment). There is no significant difference in average contribution between group and individual information treatments. Ejection is more sensitive to individual contribution in the group information treatment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the contribution data can be mapped to efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly reduction of others' payoffs), but rather exclusion from play. The findings suggest that group-based reputation is at least as effective as individual-based reputation in sustaining cooperation, and that transitioning microfinance credit bureaus from group to individual reporting may not yield substantial gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with ejection (exclusion) as the sanction mechanism, both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms increase average contributions compared to random reassignment. The effect is similar for both information types. While the study does not report efficiency directly, the increase in average contribution implies a corresponding increase in efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly punishment), so the results are most relevant for environments where exclusion or ostracism is the main sanction. The findings suggest that, for games with endogenous group selection and ejection, introducing a reputation-based reassignment mechanism (either group or individual) can improve cooperation and implied efficiency, but the marginal benefit of individual-level over group-level information is minimal. The results are less directly informative for standard PGGs with costly punishment, but may be useful for predicting the effect of exclusionary sanctions.

- source: Secchi, S., & Banerjee, S. (2019). A dynamic semester-long social dilemma game for economic and interdisciplinary courses. *Journal of Economic Education*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that students, when given the opportunity, may devise and implement their own institutional solutions to the common-pool resource dilemma, including costly peer punishment. The game is effective for teaching about the dynamics of cooperation, cheating, and institutional innovation in resource management. However, the paper does not provide quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically compare outcomes with and without punishment. The main findings are qualitative and pedagogical, emphasizing the diversity of student-devised solutions and the context-dependence of group behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it confirms that peer punishment can emerge and is sometimes used by participants to address over-extraction, it does not provide quantitative or systematic evidence on its impact on efficiency or group payoff. The game design dimensions are described in detail, but the effect of punishment is only discussed anecdotally. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that allowing for endogenous punishment mechanisms can lead to creative solutions and potentially improved group outcomes, but does not provide data to calibrate the size or direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: D'Exelle, B., & van den Berg, M. (2014). AID DISTRIBUTION AND COOPERATION IN UNEQUAL COMMUNITIES. *Review of Income and Wealth*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a representative who allocates aid (selected as the highest contributor) increases group contributions compared to equal sharing, due to both competition for the representative role and anticipation of punishment (i.e., receiving less aid for low contributions). However, representatives keep a large share of the aid for themselves, so the increased total aid does not translate into higher payoffs for the rest of the group. Representatives do consider fairness, giving more to low-endowment and higher-contributing members, but still capture more than their proportional share. Endowment inequality increases contributions under equal sharing but not under representative sharing. The study does not report group efficiency or total group payoff as a ratio to the social optimum, so the effect of the representative mechanism on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, representative allocation with the ability to reward or punish via aid shares) on group contributions and the distribution of payoffs. The representative mechanism increases contributions (a proxy for cooperation), but the efficiency gain for the group as a whole is ambiguous because the representative captures a disproportionate share of the aid. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency (total group payoff relative to the maximum), this study suggests that mechanisms allowing targeted allocation can increase contributions, but may not improve average group payoffs for all members unless the allocation is constrained. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Tan, J. H. W., & Bolle, F. (2023). Intragroup punishment and intergroup conflict aversion weaken intragroup cooperation in finitely repeated games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that intragroup cooperation is sensitive to the presence of intergroup rivalry and the payoff structure. Cooperation is lower when it inflicts losses on rivals, even when socially efficient, and higher when synergy within the group is increased. Participants use dynamic strategies, such as punishing partner defection with unyielding defection, but do not respond similarly to rival actions. Outgroup altruism (conflict aversion) further reduces cooperation that would harm rivals. Cooperation rates decline over time due to these mechanisms. The study does not include a costly punishment stage or analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' discussed is behavioral (trigger strategies, unyielding defection) rather than a costly punishment mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how dynamic strategies and group-specific preferences affect cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to the introduction of a punishment option. The game structure is close to a PGG, but the absence of a costly punishment treatment and lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Torul, O., & Vostroknutov, A. (2020). Norm compliance, enforcement, and the survival of redistributive institutions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in the absence of enforcement, contributions to redistribution decay rapidly due to free riding, even among individuals with a high propensity to follow rules. Exclusion (random checks with exclusion for non-compliance) slows the decay but does not prevent it. Only full enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) sustains high contributions and reduces post-tax income inequality. Rule-following individuals initially try to follow the rules but eventually reduce contributions in response to free riding. Rule-followers prefer institutions with strong rules when enforcement is present, but switch to unregulated institutions when enforcement is absent and free riding is prevalent. The paper reports post-tax income and Gini coefficients, but does not directly analyze efficiency as group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum. The findings suggest that some form of enforcement is necessary to sustain redistribution and prevent the collapse of cooperative institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enforcement (a form of exogenous punishment) on group outcomes in public-goods-like environments. While it does not implement a classic costly punishment mechanism, it shows that enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) is necessary to sustain high contributions and reduce inequality; exclusion as a sanction slows but does not prevent decay. In the absence of enforcement, contributions and redistribution collapse. The results suggest that, in similar designs, enabling effective enforcement or punishment is likely to increase group welfare and reduce inequality, but the paper does not report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark. The findings are most informative for settings where enforcement is exogenous and not costly to the enforcer. The evidence is less directly applicable to classic peer punishment with explicit costs. Control efficiency (without enforcement) is low due to free riding; treatment efficiency (with enforcement) is likely much higher, but the exact ratio is not reported.

- source: Mitterbacher, K., Palan, S., & Fleiss, J. (2024). Intergroup cooperation in the lab: asymmetric power relations and redistributive policies. *Empirica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in an asymmetric intergroup setting, the threat or implementation of discriminatory redistribution (a form of institutionalized sanction) reduces cooperation by the disadvantaged group (migrants). When citizens choose equal sharing, migrants are more likely to contribute, and when migrants contribute, citizens are less likely to vote for discrimination. This reciprocity can lead to mutually beneficial cooperation, but discrimination or lack of cooperation can trigger downward spirals. The mere possibility of discrimination reduces cooperation compared to a baseline where only equal sharing is possible. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution and voting rates), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, discriminatory redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like games with asymmetric power. The findings suggest that institutionalized sanctions (discriminatory redistribution) can reduce cooperation and thus would likely reduce efficiency, especially when the disadvantaged group perceives the system as unfair. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The design and behavioral results are informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions may backfire in asymmetric settings, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Cavalcanti, C. (2020). On the Determinants of Denouncing Illegal Fishing: A Field Study in Artisanal Fishing Communities. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that fishermen who are more impatient (prefer immediate rewards in a lab task) and those who are more pro-social (contribute more in a public goods game) are more likely to report a willingness to denounce others for illegal fishing. The willingness to denounce is interpreted as a form of adaptive altruistic punishment, but is measured via self-report rather than observed behavior. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare are reported. The public goods game is used only as a measure of pro-sociality, not as a treatment or outcome. The study provides correlational evidence on individual differences in punishment propensity, not on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment as a game feature, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. Instead, it provides correlational evidence that individual differences in impatience and pro-sociality predict willingness to denounce norm violations in a real-world resource dilemma. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that individual differences may moderate punishment behavior, but not providing evidence on the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Bernard, M., Dreber, A., Strimling, P., & Eriksson, K. (2013). The subgroup problem: When can binding voting on extractions from a common pool resource overcome the tragedy of the commons?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares four treatments in a repeated static common pool resource (CPR) game: (1) Autonomy (individual extraction), (2) Global Democracy (all vote, binding for all), (3) Local Democracy (subgroup voting, binding within subgroup), and (4) Delegation (random subgroup leader votes, binding for all). Efficiency, measured by mean profit per round and mean extraction, is highest in Global Democracy and Delegation (close to social optimum), lowest in Autonomy (close to Nash equilibrium), and intermediate in Local Democracy (closer to Nash than to optimum, and deteriorating over time). The results show that only globally binding voting (direct or via delegation) reliably overcomes the tragedy of the commons, while local/subgroup voting is insufficient. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in CPR games with the design features described, introducing a binding group-level voting mechanism (even via randomly selected leaders) can dramatically increase efficiency compared to individual decision-making. However, binding voting limited to subgroups is less effective and does not achieve full efficiency. The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is the treatment of interest, but the findings are highly informative for predicting the effect of institutional design (voting scope) on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group-level binding rules (even without punishment) can substitute for or complement punishment in achieving high efficiency, while subgroup-level rules are less effective.

- source: Tambunlertchai, K., & Pongkijvorasin, S. (2020). The impacts of collective threshold requirements for rewards in a CPR experiment. *Environmental Economics and Policy Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Introducing collective-threshold-based rewards in a CPR game with villagers in Thailand leads to lower resource extraction and higher payoffs compared to the control (no reward) condition. The strict and moderate threshold treatments are most effective at reducing extraction and increasing payoffs, but differ in cost efficiency and equity: strict thresholds are most cost-efficient but induce free-riding and high payoff variance, while moderate thresholds are nearly as effective but yield more equitable payoff distributions. Relaxed thresholds are less effective. Increasing the reward size does not affect long-run extraction or payoffs, but accelerates the achievement of group targets. The study provides direct evidence on how reward design (threshold and size) affects efficiency and payoff outcomes in CPR settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in CPR games with collective-threshold-based rewards (but no punishment), efficiency and group payoffs increase relative to control. The effect is moderated by the threshold level: strict and moderate thresholds are most effective, with strict thresholds being most cost-efficient but less equitable. Reward size mainly affects the speed of cooperation, not long-run efficiency. For prediction, the absence of punishment and the presence of collective-threshold rewards should be expected to yield higher efficiency than control, especially under strict or moderate thresholds. The findings are directly relevant for settings with group-based positive incentives but do not inform about punishment effects.

- source: Messer, K. D., Suter, J. F., & Yan, J. B. (2013). Context Effects in a Negatively Framed Social Dilemma Experiment. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that pre-play communication and voting significantly increase contributions to the public account in a negatively-framed VCM. The effect of the status quo (initial endowment in private vs. public account) is reversed compared to positively-framed games: with communication, a private account status quo leads to higher and more persistent contributions than a public account status quo. Voting alone does not significantly affect contributions beyond communication. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and all quantitative results are reported as contribution rates, not direct efficiency or payoff measures. The findings highlight the importance of context and framing in social dilemmas, but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games or their variants. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as no punishment mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it does provide evidence on how communication, voting, and status quo framing affect contribution rates (and thus implied efficiency) in a negatively-framed VCM. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant. For tasks focused on communication, voting, or framing, it is informative.

- source: Espinosa, M. P., Fatás, E., & Ubeda, P. (2019). Linguistic diversity and out-group discrimination in bilingual societies. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the effect of ethno-linguistic diversity on efficiency in public goods provision is context-dependent. In Bilbao, diversity (mixed-language groups) leads to significantly lower contributions to the global public good and lower earnings, indicating reduced efficiency. In Valencia, diversity has a positive or neutral effect on global public good contributions and earnings, especially among Catalan speakers. The results are robust to controls for individualism/collectivism and are supported by belief elicitation in a follow-up experiment, which shows that negative effects in Bilbao are associated with normative tolerance for out-group discrimination, while positive effects in Valencia are linked to optimistic expectations about out-group cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment, the effect of diversity on efficiency in public goods games depends on the level of underlying group conflict. The nested public goods game is structurally similar to a standard PGG but with a local/global allocation choice. The findings suggest that, when predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) games, group composition and social context (e.g., group conflict) are important moderators. However, because there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design, this paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of diversity, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Brown, C. L. (2024). Team production in endogenous networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a public-goods-like team production environment with endogenous network (team) formation, most efficiency loss is due to failures in the team formation stage, not the contribution stage. When both synergy heterogeneity and high link costs are present, teams are smaller, less productive, and network efficiency loss is highest. Contribution levels within teams are generally close to equilibrium predictions, and contribution inefficiency is relatively minor compared to network inefficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning means the results speak to the baseline efficiency of endogenous team formation, not to the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on efficiency outcomes in a public-goods-like environment with endogenous team formation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide a strong baseline for efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in settings where team formation is endogenous and costly. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency of the control (no-punishment) condition in similar networked public goods games, but cannot inform the treatment effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, Y., & He, L. F. (2021). Theory and experiments on network games of public goods: inequality aversion and welfare preference. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that in network public goods games with a dominant strategy, subjects systematically contribute more than the self-interest level, resulting in higher group payoffs and welfare. This over-contribution is best explained by a welfare preference model, where individuals care about the payoffs of their neighbors. Inequality aversion explains some behavioral patterns but is less predictive overall. The effect of social preferences is moderated by network structure (star, circle, complete) and by the level of economic return: welfare preference dominates in star networks and at low returns, while self-interest becomes more important in circle networks and at high returns. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. However, it provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be higher than predicted by self-interest due to social preferences, especially welfare preference. The findings suggest that network structure and the level of economic return moderate the extent of over-contribution and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in networked public goods games, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Caria, A. S., & Fafchamps, M. (2019). Expectations, network centrality, and public good contributions: Experimental evidence from India. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that central players do not contribute more than others, despite being much more efficient at generating public good. This results in only about half of the potential efficiency being realized. Disclosing group expectations (elicited from participants) increases the likelihood that central players match those expectations, but does not increase average contributions or efficiency overall, except in groups with weak social ties, where a significant positive effect is observed. The dominant behavioral pattern is conditional cooperation, even when efficiency and equality would require higher contributions from the centre. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, even highly efficient central players do not contribute more than others, resulting in substantial efficiency losses. The findings suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is limited by conditional cooperation norms, not by the potential for high-impact contributions. The effect of expectation disclosure is context-dependent and does not generally increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper provides a clear control case for games with no punishment: efficiency is about 50% of the potential maximum, even with strong efficiency incentives for some players. The absence of punishment is critical—adding punishment would likely change these outcomes, but this paper does not provide direct evidence on that.

- source: McEvoy, D. M., Cherry, T. L., & Stranlund, J. K. (2015). Endogenous Minimum Participation in International Environmental Agreements: An Experimental Analysis. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when the efficient coalition size is the full group (6 of 6), groups adopt the efficient minimum membership requirement about 77% of the time, and efficient coalitions form in over 90% of those cases, resulting in high efficiency (87.4%). When the efficient coalition is a subset (3 of 6), the efficient threshold is also adopted about 77% of the time, but efficient coalitions form only about half the time, and efficiency is lower (79.6%). Blocking of efficient coalitions is observed, especially in the partial coalition treatment, and is attributed to inequality aversion. The endogenous minimum participation mechanism is effective at facilitating cooperation and efficiency when full participation is required, but less so when partial participation allows for free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, so it does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it provides high-quality evidence on the effects of endogenous minimum participation requirements as a coordination device. The results suggest that such mechanisms can achieve high efficiency, especially when the efficient outcome requires full participation. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative institutional mechanisms for promoting cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Brekke, K. A., Konow, J., & Nyborg, K. (2017). Framing in a threshold public goods experiment with heterogeneous endowments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that high-endowed subjects contribute more in absolute terms than low-endowed subjects, but both groups contribute similar proportions of their endowments except when the contribution metric is framed in absolute terms, in which case low-endowed subjects contribute significantly more (about 80% of their endowment). The vast majority of groups reach the threshold for public good provision, often in the first period. Framing effects are present for low-endowed subjects but not for high-endowed subjects. Distributive preferences explain some of the variation in contributions, especially for high-endowed subjects, but do not explain the framing effect for low-endowed subjects. No punishment or sanctioning is present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it does provide informative context on how framing and endowment heterogeneity affect contribution behavior and project success rates in threshold public good games with communication. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior and the role of framing in similar environments without punishment.

- source: Antinyan, A., Corazzini, L., & Neururer, D. (2015). Public good provision, punishment, and the endowment origin: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of two less privileged (LP) members—either with lower productivity (lower MPCR) or with no feedback information—reduces average group contributions compared to the baseline with no LPs. The type of LP affects the pattern of contribution decay and spillovers: less productive LPs contribute least and trigger lower contributions in the group, while LPs without feedback information are exploited by their neighbors but themselves contribute more. The positioning of LPs (close vs. distant) also affects contribution dynamics, especially in the lower productivity treatment. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare directly, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. Its findings on heterogeneity and feedback information may be contextually informative for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The main outcomes are contribution rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Rosenbaum, S. M., Billinger, S., & Stieglitz, N. (2012). Safeguarding Common-Pool Resources in Transition Economies: Experimental Evidence from Central Asia. *Journal of Development Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions to the public good are high and nearly identical in both Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan, despite differences in market integration and transition stage. The experiment is a one-shot, anonymous, five-player VCM with no punishment or reward options. The results suggest that, in the absence of sanctioning mechanisms, group size, anonymity, and market integration do not significantly affect contribution rates in this context. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism was present or analyzed. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline contribution rates in a one-shot, five-player, anonymous VCM with MPCR=0.4 and no communication or sanctions. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative only for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar game designs, but provides no evidence on the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Boosey, L. A. (2017). Conditional cooperation in network public goods experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a network public goods game, average contributions decline over time, with the rate and extent of decay influenced by the type of information feedback and the composition of the group. Feedback about payoffs (rather than contributions) leads to lower contributions. The presence of a free-rider accelerates the decay of contributions, especially for players closer to the free-rider in the network. Unconditional full contributors ('good eggs') can slow the decline in contributions for their immediate neighbors, but do not induce convergence to full cooperation. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is informative about the baseline dynamics of contribution decay and the influence of information feedback and group composition in networked PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper can help establish expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in networked environments, especially regarding the impact of feedback format and the presence of free-riders or full contributors. It does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Dugar, S. (2010). Nonmonetary sanctions and rewards in an experimental coordination game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling players to assign nonmonetary disapproval (sanction) points to others in a repeated minimum effort game leads to convergence on the efficient (payoff-dominant) equilibrium, with group earnings nearly doubling compared to the baseline. In contrast, enabling approval (reward) points does not improve efficiency and results in outcomes similar to or worse than the baseline. The effect of disapproval is not due to communication per se, but to the psychological impact of negative feedback. Sanctions are used more persistently and effectively than rewards, and their behavioral impact on raising effort is stronger. The findings are robust to initial group behavior and are supported by both aggregate and regression analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling nonmonetary, costless, and payoff-neutral sanctions (disapproval) in a repeated coordination game can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when the sanctions have no direct monetary effect. The effect is not observed for nonmonetary rewards (approval). For prediction tasks, the presence of a nonmonetary sanction mechanism (even if costless and payoff-neutral) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such mechanism, while a nonmonetary reward mechanism may not. The game is a minimum effort game (not a standard PGG), so transfer to PGGs should be cautious but is likely informative for adjacent coordination settings.

- source: Kamei, K. (2020). Group size effect and over-punishment in the case of third party enforcement of social norms. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma is effective at deterring defection as group size increases, because aggregate punishment increases even though individual punishment per punisher decreases only mildly. However, with large groups (e.g., 10 third party punishers), over-punishment leads to severe efficiency losses: defectors' payoffs become negative, and cooperators' payoffs are also reduced due to perverse punishment. When third parties are allowed to coordinate punishment levels democratically, over-punishment is mitigated, and efficiency is restored. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive at moderate group sizes but negative at large group sizes without coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on group size and the presence of coordination mechanisms. For small to moderate group sizes, enabling third party punishment increases efficiency by deterring defection. For large group sizes, punishment without coordination leads to over-punishment and large efficiency losses, reducing group payoffs below the social optimum. If coordination mechanisms (e.g., voting) are present, over-punishment is mitigated and efficiency is restored. Thus, when predicting treatment efficiency from game design, group size and the presence/absence of coordination mechanisms are key moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper provides expected payoffs for both control and punishment conditions, allowing for estimation of efficiency changes.

- source: Rigdon, M. (2009). Trust and reciprocity in incentive contracting. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that adding ex post punishment or reward options to a principal-agent game does not reduce efficiency or cooperation. Low-level incentives (1:1 cost-impact) have no significant effect compared to the control (implicit contract). High-level incentives (1:3 cost-impact), especially punishment, significantly increase both investment and returns, leading to higher efficiency (89.5% vs. 80% in control). High reward increases returns but not investment. Principals are more likely to use rewards than punishments, but the threat of high punishment is more effective at increasing efficiency. There is no evidence for 'motivation crowding out' or the 'Paradox of Organizational Trust' in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a bilateral trust/investment game with gains from exchange, enabling high-impact punishment (where the punishment impact is much greater than the cost to the punisher) increases efficiency relative to a control without punishment. Low-impact punishment or reward does not improve efficiency. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar games, the effectiveness of punishment (punishment_tech) is crucial: only high punishment_tech leads to efficiency gains. The control efficiency (80%) is increased to 89.5% with high punishment. The findings are most directly relevant to principal-agent or trust games, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Kidwai, A. H., & Portillo, J. E. (2022). Ours, not yours: Property rights, poaching and deterrence in common-pool resources. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to punish outsiders in a CPR setting increases efficiency by reducing poaching and aligning insider harvests with the social optimum. The effect of punishment is more pronounced when insiders have earned their property rights, as they coordinate more effectively to deter poaching through higher and more frequent punishment. However, even with punishment, insiders do not fully deter poaching, and some poaching persists. The presence of chat among insiders likely facilitates coordination. The study provides direct evidence on how punishment and the method of property rights allocation interact to affect group payoffs and efficiency in a CPR context with intergroup conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a CPR game with communication among insiders increases efficiency, especially when property rights are earned. The efficiency gain is due to reduced poaching and better coordination among insiders. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (PGG) but differs in the presence of outsiders and the poaching stage. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), with a larger effect if group identity is salient (e.g., earned rights). The effect is not absolute—some inefficiency remains due to incomplete deterrence. The results are most applicable to CPR games with insider/outsider structure, chat among insiders, and a punishment technology with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Kamei, K., Sharma, S., & Walker, M. J. (2025). Collective sanction enforcement: New experimental evidence from two societies. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that third-party punishment of defectors is common in both the UK and India, but is much stronger in the UK. In the UK, adding multiple third parties increases aggregate punishment, and enabling higher-order punishment (punishment of other third parties for failing to punish or for anti-social punishment) further increases aggregate punishment. This transforms the game from a prisoner's dilemma to a stag-hunt, increasing the potential for efficient cooperation. In India, punishment levels are lower, and neither group size nor higher-order punishment opportunities are sufficient to change the incentive structure; defection remains the dominant strategy. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus positive and substantial in the UK (especially with higher-order punishment), but weak in India. The results highlight the importance of cultural context and prevailing norms in determining the efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in social dilemma games depends on the number of punishers, the availability of higher-order punishment, and the cultural context. In environments similar to the UK, enabling higher-order punishment among multiple third parties can transform the incentive structure to support efficient cooperation, while in environments like India, the same institutional changes have little effect on efficiency due to weaker punishment norms. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by group size, higher-order punishment mechanisms, and societal norms. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency; the presence and effectiveness of punishment institutions and cultural context must be considered.

- source: Janus, T., & Lim, J. J. (2009). Sticks and carrots: Two incentive mechanisms supporting intra-group cooperation. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper proves that in a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma, the introduction of either collective punishment (Nash reversion by all after any defection) or reward mechanisms (transfers contingent on cooperation) can sustain full cooperation as a subgame perfect equilibrium, even when bilateral incentive compatibility constraints are violated. This means that group efficiency (welfare) can be maintained at the cooperative level under broader conditions than in the absence of such mechanisms. The results are general and apply to settings where players interact repeatedly and can observe each other's actions, and where the game is either infinite or has an unknown end point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency by sustaining cooperation, even when cooperation would not be sustainable without such mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially collective punishment with Nash reversion) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, provided players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor) and can observe actions. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma), so the results are most directly applicable to small-group, repeated, fully observable settings. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it does establish that the direction of effect is positive for efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Coffman, L. C. (2011). Intermediation Reduces Punishment (and Reward). *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that when a first mover can choose to act selfishly either directly or via an intermediary, third-party punishment for selfishness is significantly lower when the action is taken through the intermediary. This is true even when the intermediary is passive and cannot be blamed. As a result, the poorest player (receiver) earns much less on average (from $4.50 to $1.63), and group earnings fall (from $19.55 to $17.24) when intermediation is available. The effect is robust to various controls and alternative explanations (e.g., diffusion of responsibility, confusion, lack of reasoning, or mere presence of a third party). The reduction in punishment is not explained by standard models of fairness or responsibility diffusion. The paper also finds that even when subjects are aware that intermediation is used to avoid punishment and that it harms the poorest player, they still punish less. A follow-up study shows a similar effect for rewards: indirect charitable giving is rewarded less than direct giving.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of an intermediary (a form of indirectness) in a punishment-enabled environment can dramatically reduce the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining pro-social outcomes, leading to lower efficiency and welfare. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs allowing for indirect actions or intermediation—even with punishment enabled—will likely see much lower efficiency than comparable direct-action games. The control (no intermediary) game with punishment achieves high receiver payoffs and group efficiency, but adding the intermediation option sharply reduces both, even though punishment is still available and costless. Thus, the presence of intermediation is a strong negative moderator of punishment's positive effect on efficiency.

- source: Haag, M., & Lagunoff, R. (2006). Social norms, local interaction, and neighborhood planning. *International Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of repeated cooperation enforced by punishment depends critically on the network structure and the distribution of individual patience (discount factors). In maximally connected cliques, punishment is most effective due to perfect monitoring, and full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is sustainable if all individuals are sufficiently patient. However, when some individuals are impatient (low discount factor), cliques can lead to cascades of defection and lower welfare. In such cases, less connected networks (e.g., grids) can isolate the effects of uncooperative types, allowing more tolerant individuals to absorb free riding and maintain higher overall welfare. The optimal network design thus balances the benefits of effective punishment (which requires connectivity and observability) against the costs of social conflict from connecting intolerant and uncooperative types. The paper provides explicit formulas for expected welfare under different network designs and type distributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in repeated local public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma environments as a function of network structure, monitoring, and heterogeneity in patience. It shows that punishment is most effective (and efficiency is highest) in small, maximally connected groups with perfect monitoring and homogeneous patience. As heterogeneity increases, or as monitoring becomes more local/imperfect, the efficiency gains from punishment can be reduced or even reversed, with less connected networks sometimes yielding higher welfare. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by network connectivity, monitoring structure, and the distribution of patience among players. However, the results are theoretical and assume rational, forward-looking agents using trigger strategies; they do not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Schaefer, A. (2023). Polycentricity and adaptation: A multilevel selectionist approach. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated CPR lab experiment with three-person groups, the introduction of collective (pool) punishment (10P) does not significantly improve efficiency or payoffs compared to the baseline without punishment. Only a minority of groups in the 10P treatment maintain higher resource levels and payoffs. When second-order punishment (20P) is added (punishing both over-harvesters and tax evaders), the sanctioning institution persists, but efficiency and payoffs decline: subjects extract more to compensate for tax costs, and resource exhaustion becomes more frequent. The effect of punishment is thus context-dependent: collective punishment can promote conservation in some groups, but second-order punishment undermines efficiency overall. The paper provides direct evidence on group payoffs, resource exhaustion, and the mechanisms by which punishment affects behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in CPR-like environments is not uniformly positive and depends on the institutional design. Pool punishment (collective, voluntary tax-funded) does not reliably increase efficiency compared to no punishment, and second-order punishment (punishing tax evaders) can reduce efficiency by increasing extraction and resource collapse. For prediction, the presence of second-order punishment is a strong negative moderator for efficiency, while voluntary collective punishment has a mixed or null effect. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient to guarantee higher efficiency; the details of the punishment institution and group responses are critical. Control efficiency is a strong predictor, but the effect of punishment depends on whether it is voluntary, enforced, and how costs are distributed.

- source: Nigus, H. Y., Nillesen, E., Mohnen, P., & Di Falco, S. (2023). Markets and socially responsible behavior: do punishment and religion matter?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study uses a lab-in-the-field market experiment in Ethiopia to test the effects of punishment and religious priming on socially responsible behavior and welfare. In the market baseline (no punishment), only about 21% of purchases are fair (no negative externality), and average earnings are about 56 Birr. When punishment is introduced (observers can punish at a cost of 1 Birr to reduce others' earnings by 5 Birr), the proportion of fair purchases rises to 95%, and average earnings increase to 66 Birr. Punishment also reduces anti-social behavior in a subsequent joy-of-destruction game (money burning). Religious priming increases fair purchases to 32% but does not significantly improve welfare compared to the baseline. The results show that punishment mechanisms are highly effective at increasing efficiency (group welfare) in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a market game with negative externalities, adding a punishment mechanism (with a cost of 1 to reduce another's payoff by 5) substantially increases efficiency (average group earnings) compared to a baseline with no punishment. The effect is large and robust. The design is not a standard PGG but is adjacent: it is a market/trade game with externalities and third parties, and the efficiency measure is directly comparable (average earnings relative to maximum possible). The punishment mechanism is costly and effective. Religious priming has a much weaker effect. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar market or externality games, enabling punishment with a moderate cost-to-impact ratio will lead to a large increase in efficiency over the control. The effect is much larger than that of non-monetary priming.

- source: Roux, C., & Thöni, C. (2015). Collusion among many firms: The disciplinary power of targeted punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The availability of targeted punishment in Cournot oligopoly experiments leads to significantly higher group profits and lower total output (closer to the collusive benchmark) for all group sizes tested (2, 4, 6, 8). The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive and increases with group size beyond duopoly: in 6- and 8-firm markets, mean profits nearly double with punishment. Punishment is used in a targeted and proportional way, with defectors facing harsher penalties as group size increases. The study provides direct evidence that targeted punishment can sustain high-efficiency collusion even in large groups, contrary to standard theory and previous experimental results without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling targeted punishment in repeated Cournot (oligopoly) games leads to large increases in group efficiency (profit), especially as group size increases. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related in structure and in the punishment mechanism. The results suggest that, for games with similar punishment technology (high impact, targeted, known cost), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition, and the effect is larger for larger groups. The findings are highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions, especially for settings with targeted, high-impact punishment and no communication.

- source: Grimm, V., & Mengel, F. (2011). Matching technology and the choice of punishment institutions in a prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that a local, exogenous punishment institution can sustain high cooperation and high average payoffs (efficiency) when the matching structure (population viscosity) is high, i.e., when group members mostly interact with each other. As viscosity decreases (more random mixing), both cooperation and average payoffs decline. In the most separated case (x=0), average profits are 373 (out of a possible 400), while in the most mixed case (x=1), average profits drop to 230 (the mutual defection payoff is 200). A control with internalized punishment (punishment always applies) at low viscosity (x=2/3) yields higher average profits (243) than local punishment (230). Thus, punishment institutions can increase efficiency, but their effectiveness depends strongly on the matching structure and the scope of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on the matching structure (population viscosity) and the scope of punishment (local vs internalized). When predicting treatment efficiency from game design, the presence of a punishment institution will increase efficiency only if the environment allows for sufficient group separation (high viscosity) or if punishment is internalized (applies in all matches). In low viscosity or random matching, local punishment is much less effective. The control efficiency (without punishment) is the mutual defection payoff (200), and the treatment efficiency with punishment can approach the cooperative payoff (400) under high viscosity, but remains low under random matching. The paper also shows that the technical details of punishment (local vs internalized) matter for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Deng, X. Y., Wang, T., Xue, L., & Yang, S. (2025). Norm enforcement on minorities: Evidence from traffic violations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The laboratory experiment shows that the availability of peer punishment (especially when participants can choose whom to observe and punish) increases rule compliance, particularly among minority (outgroup) members who are more likely to be monitored. However, the introduction of punishment reduces total group payoffs compared to the no-punishment baseline, due to the direct costs of punishing and being punished. The highest compliance (lowest violation rate) occurs when both observability and punishment are present, but this comes with increased inequality: minorities earn significantly less than majorities or locals. The frequency of fully compliant (high-norm) groups is higher with punishment, but overall efficiency (average payoff relative to the social optimum) is lower in punishment treatments than in control. Punishment is applied non-discriminatorily, but minorities are more likely to be observed and thus punished, leading to a 'compliance minority effect' and a 'local premium' in payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities and peer punishment, enabling punishment increases compliance but reduces efficiency (total group payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline. The reduction in efficiency is due to the costs of punishment and increased inequality, especially for minorities who are more likely to be monitored and punished. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in similar designs will likely decrease average efficiency, even if compliance increases. The effect is strongest when observability is endogenous (players choose whom to monitor) and when group composition allows for minority/majority distinctions. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with peer punishment, endogenous monitoring, and negative externalities from non-cooperation.

- source: Matsushima, H. (2012). FINITELY REPEATED PRISONERS' DILEMMA WITH SMALL FINES: THE PENANCE CONTRACT. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that with a suitable contract that imposes a fine for deviation in the final period, full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) can be uniquely sustained in finitely repeated prisoners' dilemma games. The required fine can be small relative to total payoffs if the game is long enough. The penance contract is robust to renegotiation, unlike trigger contracts. The result generalizes to other two-player games with similar payoff structure. The findings are theoretical and show that the design of the punishment mechanism (timing, size, and contingency) is crucial for achieving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in two-player finitely repeated social dilemma games, the introduction of a well-designed punishment mechanism (specifically, a contract that imposes a fine for final-period deviation) can move the group from inefficient (all-defect) equilibria to fully efficient (all-cooperate) equilibria. The effect is robust to renegotiation if the punishment is contingent on final-period behavior and the fine is large enough to outweigh the gain from deviation. The result is directly relevant for predicting that enabling such a punishment mechanism will increase efficiency to the maximum possible, provided the contract is enforceable and the fine is sufficiently large. However, the result is limited to two-player, finitely repeated, contractible games and does not address larger groups or public goods games directly.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2017). Asymmetric social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games with heterogeneous players (random productivity), efficient outcomes may require some players to defect in certain matches. In such settings, standard public monitoring is insufficient to sustain efficiency, but contagious punishment strategies—where players switch to permanent defection after observing a deviation—can support high payoffs if the group is sufficiently patient, punishment is severe enough, and productivity differences are not too large. The paper provides explicit conditions under which these strategies are equilibrium and efficiency is achieved, and derives closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs. Thus, punishment (in the form of contagious, community-based sanctions) can enable high efficiency even in complex, asymmetric environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, contagious punishment) can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemma games, even when the efficient outcome involves some defections due to heterogeneity. The results are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random productivity, but the mechanisms and equilibrium conditions are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency in public-goods-like environments with heterogeneity. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to standard PGG parameters, but it does clarify that punishment can sustain high efficiency if players are patient, punishment is strong, and heterogeneity is moderate. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in heterogeneous repeated games is likely to increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently severe and the group is not too heterogeneous.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2021). Co-enforcement of Common Pool Resources to Deter Encroachment: Evidence from a Field Experiment in Chile. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The field experiment with Chilean fishers shows that co-enforcement (insider monitoring plus government sanctions) reduces poaching and increases insiders' harvests and earnings relative to a no-enforcement baseline. However, the costs of monitoring largely offset these gains, so net efficiency improvements are modest. Voting on monitoring investments and higher fines both help insiders coordinate to invest enough in monitoring to eliminate average poaching gains, but poaching persists and is not highly sensitive to the level of monitoring or fines. The results suggest that while co-enforcement can improve outcomes, the ongoing cost of monitoring is a major constraint on efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing punishment (in the form of exogenous government sanctions for poaching, combined with group monitoring) increases group efficiency/payoff relative to a no-enforcement baseline, but the net gains are limited by the costliness of monitoring. The design is not a standard public goods game but is a dynamic common pool resource game with poaching by outsiders. The findings suggest that, in similar CPR settings, enabling punishment/sanctions will likely increase efficiency, but the magnitude of the effect depends on the cost structure of monitoring and the ability of insiders to coordinate. Voting and higher fines can help achieve deterrence, but unless monitoring costs are low, efficiency gains may be small. For prediction, the control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than with punishment enabled, but the treatment effect is muted by monitoring costs.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2025). Cooperation in temporary partnerships. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random matching and temporary partnerships, combining direct punishment (within partnerships) and cyclical community punishment (across partnerships) can support full cooperation (efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, but only for sufficiently patient players (high discount factor). The milder cyclical punishment is less effective at deterring deviations in equilibrium (requires higher patience) but more effective at incentivizing punishment off-equilibrium (punishment is less costly, so more credible). In contrast, the classic grim punishment can sustain cooperation for less patient players when the temptation payoff is low, but fails when temptation is high.
