The length of partnerships (number of repeated rounds with the same partner) affects these thresholds: longer partnerships make cooperation harder to sustain for impatient players but make punishment more credible for patient players. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how the structure of repeated interactions (number of rounds per partnership, random rematching frequency), the type of punishment available (direct, community, grim), and player patience (discount factor) interact to determine whether punishment can sustain efficient (fully cooperative) outcomes. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling direct punishment (via temporary partnerships) can increase the set of conditions under which efficiency is sustainable, but only if players are sufficiently patient. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency depends on the interplay between the temptation payoff, the cost/structure of punishment, and the frequency of rematching. The results are most directly applicable to adjacent settings (repeated PD with random matching), but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or lab data, but its explicit equilibrium conditions and comparative statics are informative for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency.

- source: Aramendia, M. (2006). Asymmetric finite punishments in repeated games. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in repeated games, including social dilemmas and Cournot oligopoly, AFP strategies—where punishment is finite and asymmetric—can sustain any feasible and individually rational payoff profile when players are sufficiently patient. The punishment phase is designed so that nondeviators are always better off than the deviator, and after a finite number of periods, cooperation resumes. This approach is less restrictive than some standard equilibrium refinements and can support efficient outcomes (e.g., cartel profits) even in cases where other concepts (like WRP) cannot. The results are illustrated with examples including the prisoner's dilemma and Cournot oligopoly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that finite, asymmetric punishment strategies can sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes in repeated games, even when standard trigger or minimax punishments are not credible or too harsh. For the prediction task, it suggests that the design of the punishment phase (finite duration, asymmetry favoring nondeviators) can be critical for supporting high efficiency, especially as the number of players increases or when renegotiation-proofness is problematic. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide direct quantitative predictions for specific experimental public goods games, but they do indicate that efficiency can be maintained with appropriately designed punishment even in large groups or complex environments.

- source: Brandt, U. S., & Svendsen, G. T. (2019). How robust is the welfare state when facing open borders? An evolutionary game-theoretic model. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly punishment (by Willing Punishers) is essential for maintaining high average welfare in a society vulnerable to free riding. If the proportion of punishers falls below a threshold, defectors proliferate and average welfare collapses. Timely recognition of the threat and sufficient support (redistributive rewards) for punishers can reverse this trend and restore high welfare. The model identifies threshold effects: there is a minimum level of support for punishment required to sustain cooperation and high efficiency. Delays in shifting support to punishers or insufficient rewards for them lead to welfare loss or collapse. The model also shows cyclical dynamics, with periods of high and low welfare depending on the political narrative and group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The model suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only if punishment is sufficiently incentivized and timely. The effect is not automatic: if support for punishers is too low or delayed, efficiency gains are not realized and may even be reversed. The findings support the prediction that punishment will increase efficiency when the game design ensures adequate support for punishers and rapid response to defection. The model also highlights the importance of migration/inflow of defectors and the political/institutional context (ingroup favouritism) as moderators. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct mapping to specific experimental game parameters.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2008). Functional unpleasantness: the evolutionary logic of righteous resentment. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can be sustained in populations where some individuals are willing to punish cheaters, even if those punishers are themselves not cooperative. The evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the relative costs of punishing, being punished, and being cheated. When the cost of punishing is less than the cost of being cheated, punishment can support a stable mix of cooperators and punishers, increasing overall efficiency compared to a world with only cheaters. If punishment is too costly, cheaters dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the existence of punishment mechanisms—regardless of the punishers' motives—can improve group welfare and efficiency in collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in collective action games (including public goods game variants) can increase efficiency, as measured by average group payoff, provided the cost of punishing is not too high. The findings suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment. While the model is not an exact public goods game, its structure and payoff logic are closely related, and its results are directly relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency. The paper does not address empirical game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, or communication, but it does provide strong evidence that the cost and effectiveness of punishment are critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bolle, F., Breitmoser, Y., & Schlächter, S. (2011). Extortion in the laboratory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that when one player can punish another at low cost in a repeated extortion game, punishment is used frequently and harshly, especially as experience accumulates. The threat of punishment induces compliance with moderate demands, but actual punishment does not improve and may reduce future compliance. Over time, P's demands and income increase, while A's income decreases. The combined earnings of both players are about 80% of the maximum possible, indicating a notable efficiency loss compared to full cooperation. The results suggest that low-cost punishment leads to persistent, severe punishment and lower group efficiency, and that subject heterogeneity (different behavioral types) is important for understanding aggregate outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in repeated asymmetric games with low-cost, high-impact punishment, the introduction of punishment reduces group efficiency compared to the maximum possible. The threat of punishment increases compliance with moderate demands, but actual punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when punishment is cheap and effective, efficiency is likely to be lower than in a fully cooperative scenario, and the presence of punishment can lead to persistent efficiency losses. The results are most directly relevant to asymmetric, dyadic games rather than standard symmetric public goods games, but the mechanisms (punishment cost, effectiveness, behavioral response) are informative for predicting efficiency in related environments.

- source: Kamei, K. (2018). The role of visibility on third party punishment actions for the enforcement of social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when third party punishment is visible to another punisher, punishment of defectors becomes much stronger and punishment of cooperators is nearly eliminated. This shifts the incentive structure from a prisoner's dilemma to a coordination game, where mutual cooperation is a Nash equilibrium and cooperating yields a higher expected payoff than defecting. In the standard (low visibility) treatment, punishment is weaker and less well-targeted, and the incentive structure remains a prisoner's dilemma, with defection being more profitable. The findings suggest that increasing the visibility of punishment actions can enhance the effectiveness of third party punishment in promoting cooperation and improving group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the visibility of punishment actions (i.e., making punishment observable to other punishers) can substantially increase the effectiveness of third party punishment in promoting cooperation and improving expected payoffs. While the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanism is closely related. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment visibility should be predicted to increase efficiency (group payoff) in similar environments. The paper also provides quantitative data on expected payoffs under different punishment regimes, which can inform predictions about the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled and made visible.

- source: Tan, F. F., & Xiao, E. T. (2012). Peer punishment with third-party approval in a social dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments in a repeated prisoner's dilemma: (1) baseline, where players can directly punish each other, and (2) TPA (third-party approval), where a third party must approve any punishment before it is implemented. The TPA treatment results in significantly lower cooperation rates (0.47 vs. 0.70) and marginally significantly lower average earnings (23.52 vs. 25.30 tokens per period) compared to the baseline. Third-party approval reduces antisocial punishment but also reduces punishment of defectors, which appears to undermine the positive effect of punishment on cooperation. The study suggests that, in settings where antisocial punishment is rare, requiring third-party approval for punishment can reduce both cooperation and group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with peer punishment, introducing a third-party approval step for punishment reduces both cooperation and average earnings compared to direct peer punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that adding procedural hurdles or external approval to punishment mechanisms may decrease the efficiency gains typically associated with peer punishment, especially when antisocial punishment is infrequent. The findings are most directly relevant to adjacent settings (prisoner's dilemma rather than public goods), but the mechanism—peer punishment with and without third-party approval—maps closely to PGG-like environments. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than in the treatment with third-party approval, indicating a less positive effect of punishment on efficiency when third-party approval is required.

- source: Friebel, G., Kosfeld, M., & Thielmann, G. (2019). Trust the Police? Self-Selection of Motivated Agents into the German Police Force. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Police applicants in Germany are more trustworthy (return more in the trust game) and invest more in costly third-party punishment and reward than comparable high school students. The difference in trust is explained by risk preferences, but the difference in trustworthiness and norm enforcement remains significant after controlling for covariates. The willingness to punish and reward is measured as the average amount invested in these actions as a third party in a trust-game-based scenario. The study provides evidence for positive self-selection of intrinsically motivated, norm-enforcing individuals into the police force. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individuals who self-select into enforcement roles (police applicants) are more willing to engage in costly punishment and reward to enforce cooperation norms. However, the experimental design is not a public goods game or a repeated social dilemma, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the motivation to punish and enforce norms, but do not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect evidence about the types of individuals who may be more likely to use punishment, rather than as evidence about the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Fiedler, M., & Haruvy, E. (2017). The effect of third party intervention in the trust game. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a trust game, third party monitoring increases investment and return rates compared to no third party. Adding punishment or reward capabilities to the third party does not significantly increase aggregate cooperation or efficiency beyond monitoring alone, especially in random partner settings. In fixed partner settings, punishment may have a somewhat greater effect, but the difference is not large. Individual players are responsive to punishment and reward, but the overall aggregate effect is muted because the actual use of punishment/reward is infrequent and the absence of punishment/reward can itself act as reinforcement. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but the design allows for inference about total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in trust game environments (which are adjacent to public goods games), third party punishment does not substantially increase efficiency or aggregate payoff beyond what is achieved by third party monitoring alone, especially when partners are randomly matched. The effect of punishment is more pronounced in fixed partner settings, but still not dramatically higher than monitoring. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar trust/investment games, enabling punishment may not yield large efficiency gains over monitoring, particularly with random matching. However, the results may not transfer directly to public goods games, and efficiency is not directly measured.

- source: Pérez, R. L., & Kisst, H. J. (2012). Do People Accurately Anticipate Sanctions?. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that subjects are generally able to anticipate the sign and strength of sanctions (punishment/reward) in two-player allocation games, with moderate errors. Punishment is better anticipated than reward, and nonmonetary sanctions are anticipated with smaller errors than monetary ones. Systematic underestimation occurs mainly where rewards are prevalent. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the games are not public goods games or close variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The games studied are two-player allocation games with a sanctioning stage, not multi-player public goods games. The main outcomes are about belief accuracy, not efficiency or payoff. While the findings suggest that punishment is more salient and better anticipated than reward, and that errors in expectation are moderate, these insights are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The paper may inform mechanism design regarding the salience and predictability of sanctions, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gallo, E., Barak, D., & Langtry, A. (2023). Social distancing in networks: A web-based interactive experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Fines (punishment) for not practicing social distancing significantly increase the probability that participants choose to distance, compared to a baseline with no fine. Nudges (informational videos) have a smaller and less robust effect. The effect of fines is immediate and persistent, while the nudge effect is weaker and context-dependent. Network structure (star vs. complete) and position (superspreader vs. peripheral) influence distancing: superspreaders distance more. High disease contagiousness increases distancing. Political ideology, risk preferences, and social preferences also moderate distancing behavior. However, the experiment does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing fines (punishment) increases compliance with a costly pro-social action (distancing) in a networked environment structurally similar to a public goods game. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (distancing) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, binary action, fixed cost/benefit, punishment cost and impact, no reward, no feedback) are well specified, so the paper is informative about the likely direction of punishment effects on cooperation, but not directly on efficiency. Use this paper as supportive evidence that punishment increases pro-social behavior in similar environments, but do not use it for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: El-Bialy, N., Aranda, E. F., Nicklisch, A., Saleh, L., & Voigt, S. (2022). To cooperate or not to cooperate? An analysis of cooperation and peer punishment among Syrian refugees, Germans, and Jordanians. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that Syrian refugees in both Germany and Jordan are more likely to cooperate with other refugees than with host country nationals, indicating in-group favoritism in cooperation. However, punishment behavior (the likelihood of punishing a defector) does not differ significantly based on whether the target is an in-group or out-group member. Host country participants (Germans and Jordanians) do not show significant differences in cooperation or punishment based on the partner's group. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the behavioral effects of punishment opportunities in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with a punishment stage, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does show that the presence of punishment does not lead to group-differentiated sanctioning, and that cooperation rates are high overall, especially within in-groups. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not provide the necessary payoff-based outcome data.

- source: Charness, G., Cobo-Reyes, R., & Jiménez, N. (2008). An investment game with third-party intervention. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The possibility of third-party punishment increases the amount sent by first movers by over 60% compared to the baseline with no punishment. Responders also return more when third-party punishment is possible. When both punishment and reward are possible, first movers send similar amounts as with punishment only, but responders return slightly less. Third parties spend more on total intervention (punishment plus reward) when both are available, but spend more on punishment when it is the only option. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a trust/investment game increases trusting and reciprocal behavior (amounts sent and returned). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that punishment increases cooperation-like behaviors, but the lack of efficiency data limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Barriga, A., Ferguson, N. T. N., Fiala, N., & Leroch, M. A. (2023). Ethnic cooperation and conflict in Kenya. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing participants to switch partners (partner choice) increases cooperation rates compared to when switching is not possible. When switching is not possible, participants are more likely to punish defectors than to withdraw, but when switching is possible, punishment and withdrawal are used equally. Unexpectedly, punishment rates are higher when switching is possible. Importantly, punishment does not increase the likelihood that defectors will cooperate in the next round. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that partner choice is more effective than punishment at sustaining cooperation in this dyadic context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: in two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency as much as enabling partner choice (switching). Punishment does not increase subsequent cooperation, and its use is not associated with higher group welfare. The results suggest that, in dyadic games, punishment may not improve efficiency, and its effect may be limited or even negative if not paired with partner choice. However, because the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games is limited and should be interpreted with caution, especially for multi-player settings.

- source: Goeschl, T., & Jarke, J. (2016). Second and third party punishment under costly monitoring. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that when monitoring is free, almost all punishers are 'targeted punishers' who punish defectors more than cooperators. As monitoring becomes costly, the share of targeted punishers drops sharply, and more subjects either do not punish at all or punish indiscriminately. The average punishment for defectors falls substantially as monitoring costs rise, and the difference in punishment between defectors and cooperators (the 'incentive to cooperate') is much lower under costly monitoring. These patterns are similar for both second and third party punishment, with some subtle differences in how subjects switch between punisher types across roles. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the authors argue that lower monitoring costs should lead to more efficient outcomes by making punishment more effective at supporting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost of monitoring (i.e., the ability to condition punishment on observed behavior) is a critical moderator of the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemma games. When monitoring is costly, punishment is less targeted and less effective at creating incentives for cooperation. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that in games where monitoring is costly or imperfect, enabling punishment will have a weaker (or possibly negligible) effect on efficiency compared to settings with free or perfect monitoring. For prediction tasks, the presence and cost of monitoring should be considered a key dimension when estimating the efficiency impact of punishment. However, because the study does not report actual efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited.

- source: Boonmanunt, S., & Meier, S. (2023). The effect of financial constraints on in-group bias: Evidence from rice farmers in Thailand. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that being more financially constrained (before harvest) does not increase in-group bias in cooperation or norm enforcement. In-group bias in cooperation and punishment is observed only after harvest (less constrained), but these effects are not statistically significant in regression analyses. Sharing behavior is stable across financial conditions. The main outcomes are cooperation rates, punishment assigned, and sharing amounts, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that financial constraints do not inherently increase parochialism in cooperation or punishment among low-income rice farmers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it provides evidence that, in a prisoner's dilemma with third-party punishment (an adjacent but not exact PGG variant), the presence of punishment allows for the study of norm enforcement, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, cooperation rate) rather than efficiency. The design details (player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, but the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct value for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Galbiati, R., Schlag, K. H., & van der Weele, J. J. (2013). Sanctions that signal: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenous sanctions increase effort and beliefs about others' effort, with a stronger effect on effort than beliefs. Endogenous sanctions, where a principal chooses to sanction after observing first-round behavior, are less effective, especially for subjects who initially chose high effort. For low initial effort players, both types of sanctions have similar effects. The signaling effect—where the imposition of a sanction is interpreted as a signal that others are not cooperating—can counteract the incentive effect of sanctions for cooperators. The main analysis is on effort and beliefs, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that sanctions can increase effort in a minimum-effort game, but the effect depends on whether sanctions are exogenous or endogenous and on subjects' prior behavior. The signaling effect can reduce the effectiveness of sanctions for cooperators. For prediction, this suggests that the context and perceived informational content of punishment mechanisms may moderate their effect on efficiency, especially in coordination settings. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence.

- source: Bigoni, M., Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2013). Strategies of cooperation and punishment among students and clerical workers. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games for both students and workers. Students show a larger increase in cooperation and are more likely to adopt conditional cooperation strategies (e.g., forgiving or unforgiving trigger strategies) when punishment is available, while workers shift more toward unconditional cooperation. Workers are less likely to use personal punishment than students. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games, with the effect being more pronounced among student subjects. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates and strategy adoption, not efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games (pairwise PDs in groups of four with random matching), so caution is needed in generalizing to standard PGGs.

- source: Fabbri, M., & Carbonara, E. (2017). Social influence on third-party punishment: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that social information about peer punishment decisions leads third-party punishers to revise their own punishment levels toward the peer average. This effect is robust and stronger when conformity requires increasing punishment, even though this reduces the punisher's own payoff. The addition of a normative social influence channel (emoticon feedback) does not significantly change punishment behavior compared to informational social influence alone. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral conformity in punishment decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social information about peer punishment can substantially alter third-party punishment behavior, increasing conformity to peer norms. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The design is a modified dictator game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, conformity), not efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding how social information moderates punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Cooper, D. J., & Kühn, K. U. (2014). Communication, Renegotiation, and the Scope for Collusion. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that communication, especially when it allows explicit threats of punishment, is critical for sustaining collusion in repeated games with incentives to cheat. Limited message spaces (even those allowing contingent punishment messages) do not sustain collusion, while open chat does. Renegotiation (chat between periods) further increases collusion, likely due to the role of verbal (non-monetary) punishment. Explicit threats of punishment significantly reduce cheating, and sending promises of trustworthy behavior also reduces cheating, though to a lesser extent. The effect of communication is not simply due to increased trust or appeals to mutual benefit, but specifically to the ability to coordinate on credible punishment strategies. The results are robust to various econometric specifications.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the communication environment. Open-ended chat that allows explicit threats of punishment leads to higher and more stable cooperation (collusion) than limited or structured message spaces. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum group payoff. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible, its effectiveness is greatly enhanced by the ability to communicate explicit threats, and that the structure of communication channels is a key moderator. The results also highlight that behavioral outcomes (collusion, cheating) can diverge from what would be predicted by monetary incentives alone, due to the role of verbal and social punishment.

- source: Tan, F. F., & Xiao, E. (2018). Third-party punishment: Retribution or deterrence?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that group third parties are more likely to approve punishment for deterrence purposes (when their decisions are revealed before the PD game), while individual third parties' punishment decisions are not sensitive to whether punishment can deter. Groups are less likely than individuals to approve punishment overall, but the magnitude of punishment (when approved) is similar. Despite the presence of third-party punishment, cooperation rates in the PD game remain low across all treatments, and punishment is not sufficient to change the game into one where cooperation is the dominant strategy for most participants. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that the punishment institution as implemented does not substantially increase cooperation or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the behavioral effects of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD game, especially regarding the motives for punishment and the difference between group and individual third parties. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment (even when costless to the third party) is not always used to maximize cooperation or group welfare, and that group decision-making may lead to more deterrence-driven but less frequent punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of third-party punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency or cooperation, especially if punishment is not strong enough to change incentives. The design is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant.

- source: Engel, C., Goerg, S. J., & Yu, G. N. (2016). Symmetric vs. Asymmetric Punishment Regimes for Collusive Bribery. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is asymmetric (bribers punished less than recipients), bribers are much more likely to report to authorities if recipients accept the bribe but do not grant the favor. This threat is anticipated by recipients, who are then more likely to grant the favor, and bribers are more likely to offer bribes. These effects are robust across countries (Germany and China) and to framing (neutral vs. corruption). The main outcomes are behavioral (bribe offers, reporting, granting favors), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that asymmetric punishment, while intended to deter corruption, may actually facilitate collusive bribery by making enforcement of corrupt deals easier.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that lowering the cost of punishment (making it cheaper for one party to punish another) increases the use of punishment and, in this context, increases the frequency of cooperation in corrupt deals. However, because the game is not a public goods game or direct variant, and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators of behavioral outcomes, but the translation to efficiency is not directly tested here.

- source: Yin, X. L., Li, J. B., Li, D. H., & Chen, S. Y. (2023). When emotional responses conflict with self-interested impulses: A transcranial direct current stimulation study of cognitive control in cooperative norm compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) is higher when punishment is costless and right DLPFC activity is inhibited (cathodal tDCS), driven by stronger negative emotional responses to norm violations. When punishment is costly, neither tDCS nor emotional response significantly affects TPP. The results suggest that the cognitive control process for norm compliance involves a conflict between emotional impulses to punish and self-interested motives to avoid cost. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological mechanisms of third-party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma variant, especially the role of cost in moderating punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that costly punishment may be less frequent and less sensitive to emotional impulses, which could imply a limited effect on efficiency, but this is not measured. The design and findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is used, not its impact on group welfare.

- source: Meier, S., Pierce, L., Vaccaro, A., & La Cara, B. (2016). Trust and in-group favoritism in a culture of crime. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment mechanisms increase cooperation rates in one-shot prisoner's dilemma games in both high- and low-Mafia neighborhoods, but do not close the gap in trust or cooperation between the two. In high-Mafia schools, punishment intensifies in-group favoritism, leading to higher cooperation with in-group members but not with out-group members. In low-Mafia schools, punishment does not increase in-group favoritism and may even reduce it. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The main outcomes are behavioral: trust, cooperation, and in-group bias.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma increases cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not reported. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (binary PD, not multi-person PGG), so results may partially transfer. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in environments with strong in-group norms (high-Mafia), punishment increases in-group favoritism and does not resolve overall trust/cooperation deficits. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment may increase cooperation rates but not necessarily efficiency, especially in settings with strong parochial norms. The absence of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Takahashi, R., & Tanaka, K. (2021). Social punishment for breaching restrictions during the COVID-19 pandemic. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment against stores that breach social norms (COVID-19 restrictions), as evidenced by a significant reduction in donations to such stores at personal cost. The average reduction for norm-breakers was about 35 yen (11% of the average donation). However, when participants were told that norm-breaking stores adhered to safety guidelines or faced bankruptcy risk, the level of costly punishment was reduced to be statistically indistinguishable from the control (complier) group. The results suggest that punishment for norm-breaking is sensitive to the presence of competing or contrasting norms, but the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the willingness to punish at a cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of costly punishment in a norm-violation context, but does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as in public goods games. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why individuals engage in costly punishment, and how this can be moderated by information about other norms. However, because the experiment is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The main value is in showing that punishment can be reduced by introducing competing normative considerations.

- source: Im, C., & Lee, J. (2022). On the fragility of third-party punishment: The context effect of a dominated risky investment option. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when a third party in a dictator game is offered a dominated risky investment option in addition to the punishment and safe options, both the frequency and amount of punishment decrease significantly compared to when only punishment and safe options are available. The demand for investment increases when all options are available, indicating a substitution effect. The results are robust to risk attitudes and to making the investment option strictly dominated (negative expected return). The findings support the hypothesis that context effects (specifically, the compromise effect) can undermine third-party punishment, even when the alternative is unattractive in both material and psychological terms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of additional, even irrelevant, choice options can reduce the use of third-party punishment in dictator-game-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design of the choice set (e.g., presence of unrelated alternatives) can moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation or norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the fragility of punishment as an enforcement tool in the presence of context effects.

- source: Pan, X. F., & Houser, D. (2019). Why trust out-groups? The role of punishment under uncertainty. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds an asymmetric effect of punishment on trust: punishment opportunities increase non-social group investors' trust towards out-group trustees, but have no effect for social group investors or for in-group trust. Social group members are less likely to punish, and their trust in out-group members is higher even without punishment. The effect of punishment on trust is mediated by beliefs about trustee reciprocity. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare are reported, though the discussion suggests that social group formation may reduce the need for costly punishment and thus could improve efficiency in principle.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity formation and punishment opportunities interact to affect trust and punishment behavior in a trust game with hidden action. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms (trust, punishment, beliefs) rather than payoff-based outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, but not a direct PGG or linear public goods game. Use this paper for qualitative insight into how punishment and group identity interact, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Brandts, J., & Charness, G. (2011). The strategy versus the direct-response method: a first survey of experimental comparisons. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that, across a wide range of experimental games (including some with punishment and cooperation), the method of elicitation (strategy vs. direct response) usually does not significantly affect behavior. However, in games involving punishment, direct response tends to result in higher punishment rates than the strategy method. The paper notes that this difference is robust across several studies, but the effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The survey also finds that differences between methods are more likely when there are fewer contingent choices or in one-shot games, but these effects are not strong. Overall, the strategy method is generally a valid tool for studying behavior, but may understate punishment rates compared to direct response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment. It suggests that the elicitation method can affect observed punishment rates, with direct response yielding higher punishment. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed, the paper does not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that studies using the strategy method may understate the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, on efficiency. When synthesizing evidence, one should be cautious about comparing results across studies using different elicitation methods, especially for punishment-related outcomes.

- source: Kritikos, A., & Bolle, F. (2004). Punishment as a public good. When should monopolists care about a consumer boycott?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of consumers to punish a monopolist (e.g., via boycott) and thereby reduce prices depends on the number of consumers and the degree of solidarity among them. With few consumers, individual punishment can be effective and lead to lower prices. As the number of consumers increases, the incentive to individually punish diminishes (public good problem), and the monopolist can more easily charge higher (Cournot) prices unless solidarity is high. The model predicts that successful collective punishment (boycott) is more likely in small groups or when solidarity is strong, and less likely in large, uncoordinated groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how group size and solidarity affect the effectiveness of punishment in public-good-like environments, specifically in the context of consumer boycotts against monopolists. While it does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, it suggests that increasing group size without mechanisms for solidarity or coordination will reduce the effectiveness of punishment in improving group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is less likely to improve efficiency in large, uncoordinated groups, but may be effective in small groups or when solidarity is high. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its quantitative predictive value.

- source: Henrich, J. (2004). Cultural group selection, coevolutionary processes and large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cultural group selection, enabled by human-specific social learning mechanisms, can explain the evolution and stability of large-scale cooperation and punishment in humans. It theorizes that punishment, when stabilized by conformist transmission, can support high levels of cooperation even in large, anonymous groups, and that these group-beneficial equilibria can be selected for at the group level. The paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games, but it does argue that punishment and cooperation can be maintained at high frequency under plausible cultural evolutionary dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation and group-beneficial outcomes in large groups, especially when cultural transmission mechanisms are present. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, cultural transmission, and norm enforcement (punishment) as moderators of cooperation and efficiency, but it does not specify how game design dimensions quantitatively affect efficiency outcomes.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2018). When sanctions convey moral norms. *European Journal of Law and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that sanctions can have an expressive function, conveying moral norms and motivating compliance for moral reasons rather than purely instrumental ones. It reviews evidence that (1) retributive sanctions are more likely to induce moral norms than compensatory ones, (2) punishments are more effective than rewards at conveying obligatory moral rules, and (3) severe (but not unreasonably severe) sanctions are more likely to induce moral disapproval and compliance, especially when implemented by trustworthy and fair authorities. However, the paper also notes that sanctions can backfire, especially among those already engaging in the undesired behavior, and that excessive severity or unfairness can undermine moral norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and psychological context for how punishment may affect compliance and norm internalization in public-goods-like environments, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It suggests that the design and framing of punishment (retributive vs. compensatory, punishment vs. reward, severity, authority trustworthiness, and fairness) can moderate the effectiveness of sanctions in changing behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff data. Its main value is in highlighting psychological mechanisms and moderators that could influence the effectiveness of punishment in experimental designs.

- source: Klempt, C. (2012). Fairness, spite, and intentions: Testing different motives behind punishment in a prisoners' dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that cooperators and defectors differ in their motives for punishment. Cooperators punish defectors more when defection is intentional, suggesting a role for retaliation, but still punish unintentional defectors, indicating some concern for payoff inequality. Defectors punish both cooperators and defectors equally, regardless of intentions, consistent with spiteful motives. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or prisoners' dilemma games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the motives behind punishment and how they may depend on the intentionality of actions, but it does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except to suggest that punishment behavior may depend on player type and perceived intentions, which could indirectly affect efficiency in other designs.

- source: Duersch, P., & Müller, J. (2015). Taking punishment into your own hands: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial minority of subjects are willing to pay to personally punish an unfair allocator, even when punishment is guaranteed to occur regardless of who enacts it. Bids for personal punishment are significantly higher than for a dummy (non-punishment) auction, indicating a specific demand for personal retribution. Winning the right to punish personally increases self-reported happiness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game structure is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motives behind punishment, especially the value placed on personal retribution, but do not inform predictions about efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in PGG-like environments. The design is a one-shot real-effort allocation game with a punishment stage, not a repeated or multi-player public goods game. Therefore, it should not be used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Shen, J. Y. (2024). Preferences for punishments: explorations from a stated-choice experiment in Japan. *Applied Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that there is substantial heterogeneity in preferences for different types of punishment (e.g., length of must-attend meetings, number of reprimands, pay cut duration, scope of name disclosure) that could be imposed on uncooperative team members. Four latent classes of punishment preference are identified, ranging from those with positive preferences for all punishments to those with negative preferences for all. Socioeconomic characteristics such as age, employment status, management experience, fairness, and trust are associated with class membership. The study does not measure or report on actual cooperation, contribution, or efficiency outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe real punishment behavior in a public goods or similar game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It only reports on stated preferences for punishment types in a hypothetical scenario. No data is provided on actual game design dimensions, control efficiency, or treatment efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Masclet, D., & Pénard, T. (2012). Do reputation feedback systems really improve trust among anonymous traders? An experimental study. *Applied Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation feedback system (where players can assign costly positive or negative ratings to their partner after each round) to a repeated trust game significantly increases both trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (amount returned), resulting in higher average payoffs for both roles. The simultaneous rating system, which prevents players from knowing their partner's rating before making their own, produces the highest efficiency and welfare, as it minimizes strategic and retaliatory rating behavior. Sequential systems, where players can condition their rating on their partner's action, are less effective, and a system that prevents one side from punishing (as in the eBay-inspired treatment) does not improve and may even reduce efficiency. The results are robust across multiple sessions and are supported by both behavioral and payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costly punishment/reward (reputation feedback) system in a repeated trust game increases efficiency (average payoff) compared to a control without such a system. The effect is most pronounced when the feedback system is designed to prevent strategic and retaliatory use (i.e., simultaneous, secret ratings). The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment/reward system with moderate cost and effectiveness, especially when implemented in a way that minimizes strategic retaliation, should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. The results are directly relevant for trust-game-like environments and are likely to transfer to PGG-like settings with similar feedback mechanisms, though the mapping is not exact. The paper also provides evidence on the moderating role of feedback system design (timing, information structure) on the efficiency effect.

- source: Davies, E., & Fafchamps, M. (2021). When no bad deed goes punished: Relational contracting in Ghana and the UK. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In repeated gift-exchange games, UK subjects use wage reductions as punishment for low effort and wage maintenance/increases as reward for high effort, resulting in higher effort, higher employer payoffs, and higher efficiency. Ghanaian subjects do not systematically punish low effort or reward high effort, and punishment (wage reduction) does not deter shirking—in fact, it may increase it. As a result, efficiency and employer payoffs are much lower in Ghana. Competition and reputation mechanisms do not close this gap. The findings show that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is context-dependent and not universal.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated principal-agent or gift-exchange games is highly context-dependent. In environments where punishment is used and anticipated (as in the UK), enabling punishment increases efficiency and employer payoffs. In environments where punishment is not used or is counterproductive (as in Ghana), enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even worsen outcomes. For prediction, the paper suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism alone is not sufficient to guarantee higher efficiency; the effectiveness depends on whether participants use and respond to punishment as intended. Game design dimensions such as the ability to punish (via wage adjustment), the number of players, and the presence of reputation or competition do not guarantee efficiency gains from punishment unless the behavioral norms support its use.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., Quezada, F. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2023). The endogenous formation of common pool resource coalitions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous coalition formation, combined with the ability to monitor and sanction outsider poaching (via government-imposed fines), leads to larger coalitions, reduced resource exploitation, and higher individual and group earnings compared to open access. These effects are strongest when monitoring costs are low, enabling the grand coalition to form and fully deter poaching. When monitoring is not possible or is too costly, only small coalitions form and efficiency gains are minimal. Among fishers, coalition formation and monitoring are frequent and effective, while among students, coalitions are smaller, monitoring is rare, and efficiency gains are weak or absent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (here, government-imposed fines for poaching, triggered by coalition monitoring) increases efficiency in a CPR game, especially when monitoring costs are low. The effect is mediated by coalition size and the ability to deter outsiders. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the cost and effectiveness of monitoring and punishment, the ability to form coalitions, and the baseline efficiency under open access. The results suggest that, in environments where monitoring and punishment are feasible and not too costly, enabling punishment will substantially increase efficiency relative to control. However, if monitoring is impossible or prohibitively expensive, the efficiency gains from punishment are minimal. The findings are most applicable to real-world user groups with experience in collective management.

- source: Feinberg, Y., & Kets, W. (2014). Ranking friends. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of social interactions—specifically, the ranking profiles that determine the order in which players seek help—critically affects the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in repeated helping games. When punishment is available (either community or bilateral), full cooperation can be sustained if and only if incentive constraints are satisfied. These constraints are least restrictive (i.e., efficiency is most easily sustained) when expected costs are equalized across players (Latin square ranking profiles). Under bilateral punishment, even stricter symmetry (BFF) is required. The paper provides explicit formulas for the minimum discount factor required to sustain efficiency under each regime. Thus, the design of the interaction structure and the punishment regime jointly determine whether efficiency is achievable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated helping games with sequential requests and costly helping. It shows that the ability of punishment to sustain efficiency depends on the structure of interactions (ranking profiles) and the punishment regime. When the structure equalizes expected costs (Latin square), punishment is most effective at sustaining efficiency. If the structure is unbalanced, punishment is less effective, and efficiency may not be sustainable. The results are theoretical but provide explicit conditions and formulas that can be mapped to efficiency predictions in similar repeated public-goods-like environments, especially where helping is sequential and punishment is either community-wide or bilateral. However, the model is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for standard PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Normann, H. T., Rösch, J., & Schultz, L. M. (2015). Do buyer groups facilitate collusion?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: In repeated Cournot triopoly experiments, buyer groups that can exclude members (closed buyer groups) enable targeted punishment of high-output (deviating) firms, which facilitates collusion and reduces industry output toward the joint-profit maximizing (collusive) level. This reduces efficiency and welfare compared to baseline or open buyer groups (which cannot exclude). Communication, especially when used for explicit quantitative agreements, further increases collusion and reduces output. Open buyer groups without exclusion do not significantly differ from baseline in terms of output or efficiency. The exclusion mechanism is used mainly against high-output firms, consistent with punishment of deviations. Overall, mechanisms that enable punishment (exclusion) or communication (especially explicit agreements) reduce efficiency by facilitating collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling targeted punishment (via exclusion from a buyer group) in a repeated Cournot game reduces efficiency by facilitating collusion, as measured by lower industry output and welfare. The effect is more pronounced when communication is allowed, especially with explicit quantitative agreements. In the absence of exclusion or communication, buyer groups do not significantly affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (exclusion), communication, and the structure of the game (repeated, triopoly, continuous output) are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion or similar targeted sanctions, and less so for standard PGGs with costly punishment tokens.

- source: Choi, J. K. (2007). Trembles may support cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of trembles (errors) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games can support the evolutionary stability of conditional cooperation (punishment-based strategies), but only when the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation is not too high. Specifically, if the benefit of cooperation is less than or equal to twice the cost (b ≤ 2c), then the ALLT (all trigger) equilibrium is asymptotically stable for sufficiently high continuation probability and low error rate. If the benefit is too high (b > 2c), then the equilibrium becomes unstable, and unconditional cooperators can invade, undermining the effectiveness of punishment. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using explicit payoff calculations and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in supporting cooperation (and thus efficiency) in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments depends critically on the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation and the presence of errors. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment will only improve efficiency when the benefit-to-cost ratio is not too high and when errors are present but not too frequent. The results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated games with trigger strategies, but the logic may extend to public goods games with similar strategic structure. The findings highlight that increasing the benefit of cooperation does not always make punishment more effective; in fact, it can undermine stability if too large. The paper does not provide direct evidence for multi-player public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed are relevant for adjacent settings.

- source: Lumeau, M., Masclet, D., & Penard, T. (2015). Reputation and social (dis)approval in feedback mechanisms: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both private and public feedback mechanisms increase trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (return rate) in a repeated trust game compared to a baseline with no feedback. Public feedback (where ratings are observable to future partners) is more effective than private feedback (where ratings are only seen by the recipient) in increasing these behaviors. The difference is attributed to both a higher frequency of ratings and a stronger behavioral response to public ratings. When a cost is introduced for assigning ratings, the effects persist but are weaker, especially for private feedback. The study concludes that reputation mechanisms (public feedback) are essential for sustaining high levels of cooperation and implied efficiency in environments with imperfect information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a feedback mechanism (a form of non-monetary sanction or reward) increases cooperation and implied efficiency in trust-game-like environments. Publicly observable feedback (reputation) is more effective than private feedback (social (dis)approval only). The results are robust to the introduction of a small cost for assigning feedback. For prediction tasks, the presence and observability of feedback mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency, with public/reputational mechanisms having a larger effect than private ones. However, the results are from a trust game, not a PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative direction is likely similar.

- source: Brunner, M., & Ostermaier, A. (2018). Implicit communication in the ultimatum game. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
