  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated ultimatum game, disclosing responders' decisions to proposers (enabling implicit communication of disapproval/punishment) increases rejection rates for low offers. Proposers anticipate this and make higher offers, but not enough to fully offset the increased rejections. As a result, total group earnings (the sum of proposer and responder payoffs) are slightly lower with disclosure, though the difference is not statistically significant. The effect is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The study also finds that when offers are random (not chosen by proposers), disclosure reduces rejection rates, as responders avoid signaling envy. Overall, the main efficiency-relevant result is that punishment via rejection, when made salient through disclosure, does not increase group efficiency and may slightly reduce it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling punishment-like behavior (rejection of unfair offers) through increased transparency (disclosure) does not improve efficiency in a bargaining game adjacent to public goods games. Instead, it may slightly reduce efficiency, as the cost of increased rejections is not fully offset by higher offers. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar environments where punishment is enabled or made more salient, efficiency may not increase and could decrease slightly, especially if the punishment is costly and not perfectly anticipated by those being punished. The findings are most directly relevant to bargaining/ultimatum games, but the mechanism—costly punishment reducing group surplus—may generalize to PGG-like settings.

- source: Romaniuc, R., Dubois, D., Dimant, E., Lupusor, A., & Prohnitchi, V. (2022). Understanding cross-cultural differences in peer reporting practices: evidence from tax evasion games in Moldova and France. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling costly, anonymous peer reporting (whistleblowing) in a repeated tax evasion game leads to significantly higher tax compliance (i.e., a higher percentage of income declared) compared to a control condition where only social observation is possible. This effect is observed in both Moldova and France, even though Moldovan participants view whistleblowing as less socially acceptable and engage in it less frequently than French participants. The increase in compliance is attributed to the deterrent effect of the possibility of being reported, not just the actual frequency of reporting. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff directly, but the increase in compliance is a strong proxy for improved group outcomes in a public-goods-like context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costly, anonymous peer reporting mechanism in a repeated tax evasion game (a close variant of a public goods game) increases compliance rates, which is closely related to group efficiency. The effect is robust to large cross-cultural differences in the acceptability and frequency of reporting. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (here, in the form of peer reporting to a central authority) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to a control with no such mechanism, even if actual punishment is rare. The effect is not strongly moderated by cultural differences in the willingness to punish, at least in the settings studied. However, the outcome measured is tax compliance, not direct group payoff or efficiency, so the mapping is indirect.

- source: Johnson, D. B. (2016). (Please Don't) Say It to My Face! The Interaction of Feedback and Distance: Experiments with Vulgar Language. *Kyklos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The possibility of non-monetary punishment via unrestricted feedback increases proposer offers in the lab (where social distance is low), but not in online settings (where social distance is high). In the lab, known possible feedback leads to significantly higher offers, suggesting that the threat of negative feedback (punishment) can increase pro-social behavior and potentially improve monetary outcomes. However, in online/anonymous settings, the same feedback mechanism does not affect offers. The effect of punishment via feedback is thus moderated by social distance/proximity. The paper does not directly measure group efficiency as in public goods games, but the findings are relevant for understanding when non-monetary punishment can improve monetary outcomes in bargaining environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (here, non-monetary feedback) in increasing efficiency or pro-social monetary outcomes depends strongly on social distance and context. In environments with low social distance (e.g., lab, face-to-face), the threat of punishment can increase offers and thus improve monetary outcomes. In high social distance or anonymous environments (e.g., online), punishment has little or no effect on offers. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive only when social distance is low and the punishment is salient to the decision-maker. The findings are adjacent to public goods games but provide informative indirect evidence about the context-dependence of punishment effects.

- source: López-Pérez, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2010). On approval and disapproval: Theory and experiments. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: (1) control (standard PD, no feedback), (2) expectations (subjects state what they expect their co-player will think of their choice), and (3) feedback (after the PD, subjects can pay to send an approval/disapproval message to their co-player). The cooperation rate is highest in the feedback treatment (38.3%), intermediate in the expectations treatment (29.0%), and lowest in the control (22.4%), but only the difference between feedback and control is statistically significant. The study finds that the possibility of non-material sanctions (approval/disapproval) increases cooperation, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff. The effect is interpreted as arising from increased awareness of others' opinions and aversion to receiving negative feedback. The feedback mechanism is costly (10 ECU per message), but the messages have no material effect on payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the opportunity for non-material, costly approval/disapproval feedback can increase cooperation in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are adjacent to public goods games with punishment, as the game is a two-player PD and the 'punishment' is non-material and costly, with no direct effect on payoffs. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that enabling even non-material, costly feedback can increase cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not measured. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of a feedback/punishment stage, the cost of feedback, and the fact that feedback is non-material. The results suggest that both the psychological salience of being evaluated and the aversion to negative feedback are important for increasing cooperation, but the transferability to efficiency in PGGs is limited by the absence of group payoff data.

- source: Ansink, E., Tesfaye, A., Bouma, J., & Brouwer, R. (2017). Cooperation in watershed management: A field experiment on location, trust, and enforcement. *Resource and Energy Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The enforcement (punishment) treatment, which penalizes trustees for not returning at least the amount sent by trustors, increases the amount returned but does not increase trust (investment by trustors). There is no significant effect of enforcement on average payoffs. Framing the game in terms of soil conservation reduces trust. Trust (as measured in the game) is a strong predictor of actual soil conservation investments in the field. The study finds no evidence that enforcement increases cooperation or efficiency in this context, and suggests that enforcement may crowd out trust. The trust game is not a public goods game but is related as a social dilemma with externalities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment/enforcement mechanism in a trust game does not increase trust or average payoffs, and may crowd out intrinsic motivation for cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in trust-game-like environments with high baseline trust, adding punishment may not improve efficiency and could even reduce voluntary cooperation. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum possible group payoff. The enforcement mechanism is probabilistic and only applies to the trustee role. The findings are most relevant for settings where trust and reciprocity are central, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs.

- source: Schlapfer, A. (2024). Cross-cultural differences in retaliation: Evidence from the soccer field. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds, at a theoretical level, that polycentric governance structures are more likely to support the emergence and stability of group-beneficial norms, including costly punishment, by satisfying four key conditions: (1) minimizing within-group variance, (2) reducing within-group selective pressures against prosocial traits (e.g., via effective punishment), (3) increasing between-group selective pressures, and (4) maximizing between-group variance. It argues that punishment can transform altruistic traits into individually rational behaviors when punishment is widespread and coordinated, and that polycentricity supports the self-sorting and coordination needed for this. However, the paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment, when embedded in polycentric or self-sorting group structures, can enhance cooperation and group-beneficial outcomes. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency or welfare depends on the ability of groups to self-sort, coordinate, and monitor, and on the cost structure of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, self-sorting, and the scaling of punishment costs, rather than in providing parameterized estimates.

- source: Baumann, F., Benndorf, V., & Friese, M. (2019). Loss-induced emotions and criminal behavior: An experimental analysis. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that loss framing (compared to gain framing) increases negative emotions (anger, frustration) and decreases positive emotions (satisfaction, happiness) for both men and women. However, only men are more likely to take money from a charity (commit a norm violation) in the loss frame, and this behavior is positively correlated with their reported negative emotions. The punishment for norm violation is probabilistic: if caught, the subject pays a fine equal to the amount taken. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on individual norm-violating behavior and emotional states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it includes a punishment mechanism (probabilistic fine for norm violation), the design is not a public goods game and does not report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how emotional states and loss framing affect norm violations under the threat of punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The punishment mechanism is similar to those in economic crime experiments, so the paper may provide indirect context for how punishment and emotional states interact, especially with respect to gender differences, but it does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Landeo, C. M., & Spier, K. E. (2015). Incentive contracts for teams: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that long-term team interaction (infinitely repeated games) increases both the likelihood of team cooperation and the principal's payoff, especially in the absence of communication. Communication between agents also increases cooperation and the principal's payoff in short-term (one-shot) settings, but is a less effective substitute for ongoing interaction. Long-term teams enable the principal to induce cooperation with lower sharing rules, reducing the cost of cooperation and increasing efficiency. However, if the principal chooses the lowest possible sharing rule, agents may engage in costly punishment (shirking) as negative reciprocity, which can reduce efficiency. The effects are robust across endogenous (human principal) and exogenous (computer) environments, but negative reciprocity is stronger when the principal is a human.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ongoing interaction (repeated games) among team members increases efficiency and allows for cooperation at a lower cost to the principal, especially when communication is not available. Communication can substitute for repeated interaction to some extent in one-shot games, but does not further increase efficiency when repeated interaction is already present. The findings suggest that, in environments structurally similar to this experiment (team production with effort complementarity and binary effort choices), introducing repeated interaction or communication will increase efficiency, but the effect of communication is limited when repetition is already present. The results also highlight that punitive responses (shirking as punishment) can reduce efficiency if the incentive structure is perceived as unfair, especially with human principals. For prediction, the presence of repeated interaction (long-term teams) is a strong positive moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency, while communication is a weaker, context-dependent moderator.

- source: Ishida, J. (2009). Incentives in academics: Collaboration under weak complementarities. *Labour Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated game with voluntary team formation, optimal contracts may use high-powered team incentives and low-powered individual incentives to exploit peer monitoring and reputation effects. This can make collaboration sustainable and efficient even with weak complementarities, overcoming the free-rider problem present in static settings. However, when the opportunity to collaborate is frequent and agents are patient, the optimal contract may under-incentivize individual effort, leading to inefficiency when collaboration is not possible. Thus, repeated interaction and peer sanctions can both improve and harm efficiency, depending on parameter values (discount factor, frequency of collaboration opportunities, degree of complementarities).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling peer sanctions (punishment via reputation loss and exclusion from collaboration) in repeated public-goods-like environments depends on the structure of incentives and the frequency of collaboration opportunities. When peer sanctions are possible, efficiency can increase by overcoming free-rider problems, but if individual incentives are weakened too much to make peer sanctions more effective, efficiency can decrease when collaboration is not possible. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not unconditionally positive: it depends on whether the contract design balances team and individual incentives, the patience of agents, and the frequency of opportunities for collaboration. The model is adjacent to public goods games, with peer sanctions implemented via exclusion and reputation rather than explicit costly punishment.

- source: Jiang, S. G., & Villeval, M. C. (2024). Dishonesty as a collective-risk social dilemma. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated group lying game with a probabilistic collective sanction (if group claims exceed a threshold, all group members lose their period payoff with 60% probability), most groups fail to coordinate on honest reporting. As a result, average payoffs are significantly below the efficiency benchmark (full honesty). Lying is common, and collective sanctions are frequent. Preferences for truth-telling (moral costs of lying) reduce the frequency of collective sanctions by over 40% compared to a no-lying variant, but are not strong enough to sustain efficiency. Smaller groups have lower average claims and fewer collective sanctions, leading to higher efficiency than larger groups. Mindset priming (collectivist vs. individualist) has only a modest, short-lived effect on claims and no robust effect on efficiency. Risk aversion is associated with lower claims and higher efficiency. Experience with sanctions does not lead to learning or improved efficiency; in fact, some participants increase lying after sanctions to recover losses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that in a collective-risk social dilemma with group-level, probabilistic sanctions (not targeted punishment), the introduction of such a sanction mechanism does not sustain high efficiency: most groups earn less than the full-cooperation benchmark. Efficiency is higher in smaller groups and when moral costs of lying are present, but the effect is partial. The findings suggest that, in public-goods-like environments with only collective (not targeted) punishment, efficiency is likely to be low unless additional mechanisms (e.g., peer punishment, communication) are present. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in games with group-level, probabilistic punishment, especially regarding the negative effect of group size and the limited effect of moral costs or mindset priming. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with targeted punishment, but the efficiency loss from non-targeted, collective punishment is clear.

- source: Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2014). The Coordination Value of Monetary Exchange: Experimental Evidence. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated, anonymous helping game with four players, introducing a fiat money-like mechanism (tickets) does not increase the maximum attainable efficiency but changes how cooperation is coordinated. In the baseline (no tickets), realized efficiency is 48.2% of the maximum. With tickets, overall efficiency is 46.8%, not significantly different from baseline. However, when ticket transfer is feasible, cooperation and efficiency rise to 61.4%; when not feasible, they drop to 12.5%. The presence of tickets leads to the emergence of a monetary exchange strategy, increases the predictability of cooperative behavior, and redistributes surplus from frequent defectors to frequent cooperators. The main effect of the monetary mechanism is to facilitate coordination and reduce strategic uncertainty, not to increase aggregate efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a monetary-like mechanism (tickets) in a repeated, anonymous, decentralized helping game does not increase overall efficiency compared to a baseline with only decentralized punishment, but it does change the distribution and predictability of cooperation. The effect of the monetary mechanism is highly contingent on whether the mechanism is feasible in a given match. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a monetary or token-based exchange mechanism may not increase group efficiency if the baseline already allows for decentralized punishment and the efficiency frontier is not expanded. However, such mechanisms can facilitate coordination, increase the stability of cooperation, and redistribute surplus toward cooperators. The results are most relevant for environments where punishment is decentralized and impersonal, and where direct reciprocity and reputation are absent.

- source: Hoffmann, R., Kittel, B., & Larsen, M. (2021). Information exchange in laboratory markets: competition, transfer costs, and the emergence of reputation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that voluntary, costless information sharing among trustors in a repeated investment game leads to higher trustworthiness (trustees return more), higher trust (trustors send more), and higher payoffs for both trustors and trustees. When direct costs are imposed on information sharing, or when competition among trustors is introduced (making information sharing privately costly), information sharing drops sharply, and the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms disappear. The effect of direct costs is highly non-linear: even small costs cause a large reduction in information sharing and efficiency. Competition among trustors increases their risk-taking (sending more), but reduces information sharing and trustworthiness, so the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous or negative. The study provides direct evidence that barriers to information sharing (costs or competition) undermine the emergence of reputation and reduce market efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costless, voluntary information sharing (a reputation mechanism) in repeated trust/investment games increases efficiency (group payoffs) relative to a control with no information sharing. However, even small costs to information sharing, or the introduction of competition among information holders, sharply reduce information sharing and eliminate efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of a costless, voluntary reputation/information sharing mechanism is a strong moderator of the efficiency effect. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/investment games, which are adjacent to PGGs, and to endogenous, voluntary, non-institutionalized punishment/sanctioning via information sharing. The results suggest that in environments where information sharing is costly or where competition discourages sharing, the efficiency benefits of reputation mechanisms will be minimal or absent.

- source: Abdulkadiroglu, A., & Bagwell, K. (2013). Trust, Reciprocity, and Favors in Cooperative Relationships. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with private information, efficiency (total group payoff) can be increased above the autarky (Nash) level through favor-exchange relationships (dynamic reciprocity) or through symmetric punishment (negative reciprocity). The optimal equilibrium structure depends on the parameters: when information asymmetries are low, symmetric punishment can support higher efficiency than favor-exchange. Hybrid equilibria (honeymoon period followed by favor-exchange or punishment) can further improve efficiency in some cases. The paper provides explicit formulas for the efficiency achieved under each regime. Immediate reciprocity (punishment for non-reciprocation) is not always necessary; dynamic reciprocity (future favor) is often sufficient. The model is not a PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, trust, and efficiency under repeated interaction and private information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions for how efficiency in repeated cooperation games is affected by the availability and structure of punishment and favor-exchange mechanisms. While not a PGG, the model is adjacent and the efficiency formulas and comparative statics are informative for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment (symmetric, infrequent) is possible versus when only favor-exchange is available. The results suggest that, in some parameter regions, introducing symmetric punishment can increase efficiency above favor-exchange alone, especially when information asymmetries are low. However, the model is limited to two players and does not include standard PGG features like group size or marginal per-capita return. The findings are most useful for understanding the theoretical potential of punishment to enhance efficiency in repeated cooperation with private information, but direct quantitative prediction for PGGs would require caution.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Loukas, B., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2018). Trust and communication in a property rights dilemma. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing opportunities for free-form chat communication in a repeated property rights dilemma (where players can allocate tokens to production, theft, private or collective protection) nearly doubles the share of maximum efficiency gain achieved, compared to a no-communication baseline. Communication reduces theft and protection expenditures, and increases production and group earnings. The effect is robust to whether or not players can easily identify thieves, suggesting that punishment threats are not the main driver. Instead, trust, agreement on cooperative plans, and references to efficient outcomes in chat are strongly associated with higher efficiency. The effect of communication is much larger than that of institutionalized collective protection or voting. There is substantial cross-country variation, with higher trust societies benefiting more from communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication in social dilemma environments with appropriation (theft) and protection options can substantially increase efficiency, even when punishment is not easily implemented or monitored. For prediction, the presence of chat/communication is a major moderator of the efficiency effect, with communication nearly doubling the realized efficiency relative to control. The effect is robust to whether punishment is easy or hard to coordinate, suggesting that communication's main value is in building trust and agreement, not in facilitating punishment. The game is not a standard PGG, but the efficiency results are directly relevant for settings where appropriation and protection are possible. Control efficiency (no chat) is about 36.6% of the maximum possible gain; with chat, it rises to 77.2% (full info) and 70.5% (low info). Thus, for similar games, enabling chat should be expected to produce large positive effects on efficiency, especially in higher-trust populations.

- source: Rietz, T. A., Schniter, E., Sheremeta, R. M., & Shields, T. W. (2018). TRUST, RECIPROCITY, AND RULES. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper experimentally studies the effect of exogenously imposed minimum return rules in a one-shot trust game. Four treatments are compared: no rule (0%), and minimum return rules of 10%, 20%, and 30% of the tripled investment. The main findings are: (1) Low minimum return rules (10%) reduce trust (investment), reciprocity (voluntary returns), and economic efficiency (ROI, group payoff) compared to no rule. (2) As the rule becomes more restrictive (20%, 30%), investment and efficiency recover, but trust and reciprocity do not. (3) Only the most restrictive rule (30%) restores or improves efficiency, but this is due to mechanical risk reduction, not increased trust. (4) The relationship between rule restrictiveness and efficiency is U-shaped: gentle rules are worse than no rule, but strong rules can restore efficiency. (5) The presence of rules crowds out voluntary cooperation and learning about partner trustworthiness.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that introducing weak or moderate minimum return rules in trust-based games can reduce efficiency compared to no rule, due to crowding out of trust and reciprocity. Only highly restrictive rules (that nearly eliminate risk) can restore or improve efficiency, but at the cost of voluntary cooperation. The effect is non-monotonic: efficiency is lowest at low rule levels, recovers at high rule levels. This suggests that, in adjacent trust-game-like environments, the effect of punishment-like minimum standards on efficiency depends critically on the restrictiveness of the rule. The findings are most relevant for settings where the control (no-punishment) game already achieves moderate efficiency; in such cases, adding weak punishment or minimum standards may reduce efficiency.

- source: Kaplan, T. R., Ruffle, B. J., & Shtudiner, Z. (2018). Cooperation through coordination in two stages. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments of a two-player entry game: simultaneous moves (Now), endogenous timing (Wait), and exogenous sequential moves (Seq). In all treatments, players have private values and must decide whether to enter or exit, with payoffs depending on both players' choices. The main finding is that the Seq treatment yields the highest average pair profits (mean 170.2), followed by Wait (167.9), and Now (163.2). The difference between Seq and Now is statistically significant. The two-stage games allow cooperative pairs to coordinate more efficiently and achieve higher profits, but also allow uncooperative pairs to do worse, leading to a wider distribution of outcomes. The Wait and Seq treatments reduce double exit and increase efficient cooperation compared to Now, but also see a modest increase in double entry. The paper does not study punishment as a formal mechanism, but discusses how uncooperative behavior in the two-stage games can be met with retaliatory entry (a form of endogenous punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that changing the timing structure of a two-player entry game (from simultaneous to sequential or endogenous timing) can increase average efficiency, but with substantial heterogeneity: cooperative pairs benefit, while uncooperative pairs may do worse. The results are highly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the timing of moves, but less so for games where formal punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs and impacts) are present. The findings suggest that enabling sequential or observable timing can improve efficiency, especially when players can coordinate, but may also increase the risk of low efficiency if players are uncooperative. The paper does not manipulate or analyze formal punishment costs or technologies, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled public goods games is limited, but it is informative about the role of timing and endogenous retaliation.

- source: Flannery, T., & Wang, S. Y. (2023). Is the smoke-filled room necessary? An experimental study of the effect of communication networks on collusion. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a repeated three-player Bertrand game, the ability for all players to communicate (full network) leads to the highest group payoffs and cooperation rates. Treatments with only partial communication (either a leader or a pairwise link) do not improve payoffs over the no-communication baseline, and in some cases, payoffs are lower due to the use of punishment strategies (promise and threat) that are followed through after deviations. The structure of communication networks shapes both the strategies communicated and the realized efficiency: full communication supports more 'pure promise' messages and higher payoffs, while asymmetric or partial communication leads to more threats and lower efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that communication structure is a key moderator of the efficiency gains from communication/collusion in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated collusion games structurally similar to public goods games, the efficiency gains from enabling communication depend critically on the communication network structure. Full, symmetric communication among all players leads to the highest efficiency, while partial or asymmetric communication can result in lower efficiency than even the no-communication baseline, due to increased punishment and failed coordination. For prediction tasks, the presence and structure of communication (chat) is a key moderator: enabling chat among all players is likely to increase efficiency, but partial or asymmetric chat may not. The study does not include explicit monetary punishment or reward mechanisms, but does show that endogenous punishment via threat/follow-through in communication can reduce efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with communication as a design dimension.

- source: Ho, T. H. (1996). Finite automata play repeated prisoner's dilemma with information processing costs. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with boundedly rational agents (finite automata), cooperation and high efficiency can evolve even from hostile initial conditions, as long as there are no costs for strategy complexity. (2) Imposing a cost on the size of the automaton (i.e., the 'storage' or monitoring cost) destroys cooperation and reduces efficiency, with populations converging to simple, defecting strategies. (3) Imposing a cost on the frequency of switching states (i.e., 'retrieval' or attention cost) does not harm, and may even promote, cooperation and efficiency, especially if the cost is quadratic. (4) The results are robust to a range of simulation parameters. (5) The findings suggest that the form of cognitive or implementation cost is a critical moderator of whether punishment-like strategies (contingent defection) can sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player games structurally similar to public goods games (but not group PGGs), the ability to implement contingent punishment (e.g., Tit-for-Tat or more sophisticated automata) is crucial for sustaining high efficiency. However, if the game design imposes costs on monitoring or strategy complexity (analogous to high punishment cost or high cognitive burden), efficiency collapses and simple, non-cooperative strategies dominate. In contrast, costs on the frequency of switching (analogous to costs for changing behavior or attention) do not undermine efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that the presence and form of punishment, and especially the cost structure for implementing contingent strategies, are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The results are most directly relevant to two-player, long-horizon, repeated games with deterministic feedback and no communication.

- source: Espín, A. M., Correa, M., & Ruiz-Villaverde, A. (2019). Patience predicts cooperative synergy: The roles of ingroup bias and reciprocity. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the mean patience of group members predicts higher group cooperative synergy in a problem-solving task with intergroup competition. Both short-term and long-term patience measures are positively associated with group efficiency. Patient individuals are more likely to make efficiency-maximizing and less likely to make competitive/spiteful choices in social preference tasks. The effect of patience on group synergy is not due to ingroup bias but is linked to reciprocal fairness. The design does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but patient individuals are more likely to reward cooperative and punish uncooperative group members in allocation tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in patience (delay discounting) are a strong moderator of group efficiency in intergroup competition settings structurally similar to repeated social dilemmas. While not a standard PGG or punishment experiment, the findings suggest that group composition (patience) can be as important as institutional design for predicting efficiency. The absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most relevant for baseline or control conditions, or for understanding individual-level moderators. The study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, but it does show that patient individuals are more likely to engage in reciprocal (reward/punish) behaviors in allocation tasks, which may inform expectations about punishment effectiveness in similar environments.

- source: Ochea, M. I. (2013). Evolution of repeated prisoner's dilemma play under logit dynamics. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with a finite set of memory-one strategies, the long-run average payoff (efficiency) and the prevalence of cooperation depend critically on the mix of strategies present and the game parameters. In some ecologies, strategies like Pavlov (WSLS), which can be interpreted as implementing endogenous punishment (by defecting after being defected upon), can dominate and sustain high payoffs, especially when unconditional cooperators are absent. However, the introduction of unconditional cooperators (AllC) can destabilize cooperation and lead to the dominance of defectors (AllD), reducing efficiency. The dynamics can be complex, with cycles and chaos emerging for intermediate values of the intensity of choice parameter. The paper provides explicit formulas for average payoffs and analyzes bifurcations in the system as parameters change. Overall, the effect of 'punishment-like' strategies on efficiency is mixed and highly sensitive to the strategic environment and parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like strategies (e.g., Pavlov/WSLS) on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games is highly context-dependent. In environments without unconditional cooperators, such strategies can sustain cooperation and high payoffs, but their effectiveness is undermined when unconditional cooperators are present. The results suggest that the presence or absence of certain strategy types (especially unconditional cooperators) and the values of key parameters (benefit of cooperation, error rates, intensity of choice) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment (or punishment-like strategies) on efficiency cannot be determined solely by the presence of punishment, but must account for the broader strategic ecology and parameter settings. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated PD-like environments, but may inform predictions in PGG-like settings by analogy.

- source: Trejo, K. K., Juarez, R., Clempner, J. B., & Poznyak, A. S. (2023). Non-Cooperative Bargaining with Unsophisticated Agents. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing penalty mechanisms for deviation and time in bargaining games with myopic agents ensures convergence to efficient (Pareto optimal or Nash) outcomes, but at a cost: the process is slower and less efficient than with fully rational, forward-looking agents. The efficiency loss is quantifiable as the 'price of unsophistication,' and depends on the penalty parameters and the agents' behavior. The results are robust across a wide class of utility functions and game structures, including Markov processes. The paper does not study public goods games or peer punishment, but the penalty mechanism is analogous to centralized sanctions rather than decentralized peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of peer punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies bargaining games with centralized penalty mechanisms and myopic agents. However, it provides indirect evidence that introducing penalty costs can improve convergence to efficient outcomes even with unsophisticated agents, though always with some efficiency loss relative to the ideal. The results suggest that the design and magnitude of penalties (punishment costs) are critical for achieving near-efficient outcomes, but the context is bargaining, not public goods provision. The findings may be useful for understanding the general role of sanctions in promoting efficiency, but should be transferred to PGG-like settings with caution.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). Persistent parochialism: trust and exclusion in ethnic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that parochial networks can achieve higher efficiency (average payoff) than anonymous markets in social dilemma environments, due to better information and informal enforcement (via monitoring and exclusion). However, the relationship between parochialism and efficiency is non-linear: too little parochialism leads to poor information and low trust, while too much parochialism reduces trading opportunities and group size, also lowering efficiency. The model includes a monitoring/inspection strategy (analogous to punishment), which, when information is sufficiently accurate, supports a stable trust equilibrium with higher payoffs. The optimal efficiency is achieved at an intermediate level of parochialism, balancing enforcement and trading opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (monitoring, exclusion) on efficiency in social dilemma games depends on the information structure and group composition. Efficiency is maximized at an intermediate level of parochialism, where information is good enough to support trust and informal enforcement, but group size and trading opportunities are not too restricted. The model suggests that enabling punishment/monitoring can increase efficiency relative to a control (anonymous market) when information is sufficiently accurate and group size is within a viable range. However, excessive exclusion (high parochialism) can reduce efficiency by limiting opportunities. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment is implemented via monitoring and exclusion, rather than costly direct punishment as in standard PGGs.

- source: Auerswald, H., Schmidt, C., Thum, M., & Torsvik, G. (2018). Teams in a public goods experiment with punishment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The evolutionary IPD with choice and refusal of partners (IPD/CR) produces higher average fitness (group efficiency) than random partner matching, with most runs evolving to near full cooperation. The average fitness clusters around a few regions, with the highest corresponding to mutual cooperation. However, if the refusal payoff is high or players are highly intolerant of defection, populations can become trapped in low-efficiency wallflower states. Sensitivity analyses show that the refusal payoff, minimum tolerance level, and memory weight are key moderators: high refusal payoffs or high intolerance increase the risk of social isolation and lower efficiency. Increasing population size reduces the risk of wallflower ecologies. The findings are robust across different strategy representations and parameter sweeps.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling partner selection (choice and refusal) in repeated social dilemmas can increase group efficiency compared to random matching, especially when refusal payoffs and intolerance are not too high. For prediction tasks, the presence of a partner selection mechanism (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be expected to increase efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit structure of refusal does not incentivize social isolation. The results are most directly relevant to settings where agents can avoid defectors, but less so to standard public goods games with centralized punishment. The findings suggest that game design dimensions such as the cost of refusal, tolerance thresholds, and population size are critical moderators of the efficiency effect.

- source: Ognedal, T. (2016). Morality in the market. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing the fraction of honest sellers can reduce or increase social surplus depending on the initial prevalence of honesty and the elasticity of demand. When honesty is rare, more honesty can increase production costs and reduce welfare; when honesty is common, more honesty can improve efficiency. (2) Sanctions (punishment) are a more reliable tool for improving efficiency, as they do not distort the ranking of private and social costs. (3) The effects of honesty among buyers and sellers are asymmetric: honesty among sellers affects production efficiency, while honesty among buyers affects allocation efficiency. (4) Policies that increase individual compliance (e.g., moral suasion) may have no effect or even negative effects on market-level efficiency, depending on market structure and the distribution of honesty. (5) The relationship between individual-level compliance and market-level efficiency is complex and non-linear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency is more reliably positive than the effect of increasing intrinsic motivation (morality/honesty). In environments analogous to public goods games with market entry/exit and endogenous prices, punishment that is uniform across agents improves efficiency by aligning private and social costs, while increasing the fraction of intrinsically honest agents can have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially when honesty is rare. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (extrinsic sanctions) is more likely to increase efficiency than relying on intrinsic motivation, and that the effect of punishment may depend on the distribution of compliance and market structure. However, the model is not a direct PGG or experimental result, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Reiersen, J. (2017). TRUST AS A BOOSTER. *Journal of Business Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that trust is essential for enabling players to leave secure networks and engage in market transactions that offer higher potential payoffs but also greater risk. The model shows that the willingness to transact in markets depends on the expected probability of encountering trustworthy partners, the relative payoffs from network versus market transactions, and the cost of being cheated. When trust is high and/or the gains from market transactions are large relative to networks, players are more likely to leave networks and realize higher efficiency (total payoff). Conversely, when trust is low or the cost of being cheated is high, players remain in networks, foregoing potential efficiency gains. The model highlights the distinction between trust (needed for market transactions) and assurance (provided by networks through repeated interaction and informal sanctions).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how efficiency (expected group payoff) in environments with and without trust/assurance mechanisms depends on the structure of payoffs, risk, and expectations about others' behavior. While it does not directly model public goods games or experimental punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where players can choose between secure, repeated interactions (with informal sanctions) and riskier, anonymous interactions (requiring trust). The model suggests that efficiency gains from moving to market-like environments depend on the level of trust and the relative payoffs, and that mechanisms providing assurance (such as punishment or repeated interaction) can substitute for trust in supporting cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment in public goods games, so its relevance for predicting the effect of enabling punishment is indirect and primarily theoretical.

- source: Eckel, C. C., & Grossman, P. J. (1996). The relative price of fairness: Gender differences in a punishment game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when subjects can choose to reward a fair partner at a personal cost (i.e., accept a lower own payoff), a substantial fraction do so, especially when the cost is low. Women are more likely than men to sacrifice earnings for fairness when the cost is low, but their willingness drops sharply as the cost increases. Men are less sensitive to the cost and maintain a relatively constant (lower) rate of sacrificing for fairness. The presence of the punishment/reward option leads to lower average payoffs compared to always choosing the higher own-payoff option, indicating a negative effect on efficiency. The study does not use a repeated public goods game or a standard PGG, but the design is adjacent in that it involves costly punishment/reward decisions affecting both own and others' payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the availability of a punishment/reward option (even in a dictator-like setting) can reduce average efficiency, as some subjects are willing to accept lower payoffs to reward fairness or punish unfairness. The effect is moderated by the cost of punishment/reward and by gender. In environments where punishment/reward is costly and voluntary, efficiency may decrease relative to a control with no such option, especially if the cost is not prohibitively high. However, the design is not a repeated PGG, so direct quantitative prediction for standard PGGs should be cautious. The main implication is that costly punishment/reward options can reduce group efficiency, particularly when the cost is low and when participants are more likely to act on fairness preferences.

- source: Bernard, M., Fanning, J., & Yuksel, S. (2018). Finding cooperators: Sorting through repeated interaction. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that in the Baseline (replacement allowed), cooperation (measured by responder return) increases over time, surpassing the Fixed Partner treatment (no replacement) in later rounds, despite similar initial levels. The Contract Restrictions treatment (no replacement, no punishment) starts with lower cooperation but converges to the Fixed Partner level in later rounds. The ability to punish by reducing transfers does not significantly increase cooperation compared to the inability to punish. Instead, the ability to sort (replace uncooperative partners) is the key driver of higher long-run cooperation. Surplus sharing becomes more equal in long-term relationships, but overall efficiency is not maximized in any treatment. The findings suggest that sorting, not punishment, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated gift-exchange games (adjacent to PGGs), the ability to punish (reduce transfers) does not significantly increase efficiency or cooperation compared to when punishment is not possible. Instead, the ability to sort (replace partners) is more important for sustaining cooperation and improving surplus. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment alone (without sorting/replacement) may not increase efficiency, and that the structure of partner matching is a critical moderator. The findings are most relevant for environments where partner selection or replacement is possible, and less so for standard PGGs with fixed groups and explicit costly punishment. The paper's results should be used to caution against overestimating the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games without sorting opportunities.

- source: Müller, J., Schwieren, C., & Spitzer, F. (2022). How to prevent destruction - On the malleability of anti-social behavior. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper demonstrates that anti-social destruction of group resources in a laboratory setting is highly sensitive to contextual factors. When the destruction opportunity is presented in a transactional, market-like framing and is the only available activity, subjects destroy a large share of the group's resources, sharply reducing group payoff. However, if the destruction is framed as harming jointly owned property, or if subjects have a parallel, meaningful activity (real-effort task) to occupy them, destruction is almost eliminated and group payoffs are preserved. The effect of these interventions is large and robust. The study does not use a public goods game or punishment of free riders, but the findings are relevant for understanding how institutional design and framing can prevent anti-social, payoff-reducing behaviors in group settings.
  prediction_guidance: While this study does not directly test public goods games or punishment, it provides strong evidence that institutional context (such as framing and the presence of alternative activities) can dramatically affect the prevalence of anti-social, payoff-reducing behaviors. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in group games, the results suggest that interventions making the negative consequences of anti-social actions salient to all, or providing alternative meaningful activities, can greatly improve group efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where group resources can be destroyed at a cost, but the general principle—that context and framing can strongly moderate destructive or anti-social behavior—may inform predictions about punishment and efficiency in related environments.

- source: Rand, D. G., Fudenberg, D., & Dreber, A. (2015). It's the thought that counts: The role of intentions in noisy repeated games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with implementation errors, making intentions observable (explicit-intentions treatment) leads to significantly higher cooperation rates and group payoffs than when intentions are unobservable (implicit-intentions control). The cooperation and payoff levels in the explicit-intentions treatment are statistically indistinguishable from those in error-free games. Most subjects use simple, intention-based strategies, and the negative impact of noise on cooperation is eliminated when intentions are observable. The effect holds for both high and low benefit/cost ratios. Expected payoffs for the most common intention-based strategies are the highest among all strategies, and the overall group payoff is maximized in the explicit-intentions and no-error conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with noise, making intentions observable can fully restore efficiency (group payoff) to the level seen in error-free games. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the negative effect of noise on efficiency can be mitigated or eliminated if the game design allows players to observe each other's intentions. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with binary choices and noise. The paper does not include explicit punishment mechanisms, but the analysis of punitive and tolerant strategies provides indirect evidence about sanctioning behavior. The main design dimensions relevant for prediction are the presence of noise, observability of intentions, and the benefit/cost ratio. Other dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, reward, punishment cost) are not varied or present.

- source: Cordes, C., Richerson, P. J., & Schwesinger, G. (2010). How corporate cultures coevolve with the business environment: The case of firm growth crises and industry evolution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation within firms is sustainable up to a critical group size, after which opportunistic behavior spreads and performance declines. In innovative, uncertain environments, cooperative cultures provide a competitive advantage, but as firms grow, the influence of informal leadership wanes and cooperation collapses. In mature, stable environments, large firms can succeed by implementing monitoring regimes and realizing economies of scale, even with more opportunistic employees. The model links these dynamics to firm growth crises, industry shakeouts, and the evolution of market structure. While the model is not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, it provides theoretical insight into how group size, monitoring, and social learning affect cooperation and firm performance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that group size and the presence or absence of monitoring (analogous to punishment) are key moderators of cooperation and efficiency in group settings. It suggests that in small groups, informal cooperation can sustain high performance, but as group size increases, cooperation collapses unless formal monitoring (akin to punishment) is introduced. The model implies that enabling monitoring or sanctions in large groups can restore or maintain efficiency, especially in stable environments. However, the findings are not directly transferable to PGG lab experiments, as the context is organizational and the outcomes are modeled rather than measured. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and monitoring on efficiency, rather than for quantitative prediction.

- source: Salmon, T. C., & Serra, D. (2017). Corruption, social judgment and culture: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing social observability and allowing for social judgment (informal sanctions in the form of approval/disapproval messages) reduces the likelihood of corrupt behavior (offering or accepting bribes) among participants who identify with low-corruption cultures, but not among those who identify with high-corruption cultures. The effect is driven by differences in the likelihood of social disapproval: participants from high-corruption cultures are less likely to disapprove of corrupt acts. The Victim Knows treatment (where the victim is informed but cannot judge) has little effect. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the design is a one-shot, role-based bribery game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal social sanctions (social judgment) on norm-violating behavior in a one-shot, negative-externality game. While not a public goods game, it is relevant for understanding the limits of informal punishment/sanction mechanisms, especially their dependence on cultural context. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of informal sanctions in reducing norm violations (and thus potentially increasing efficiency) is highly contingent on prevailing social norms and expectations of disapproval. However, because the game is not repeated, does not feature voluntary contributions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for public goods game efficiency is limited. It is most informative for settings where informal social sanctions are the main enforcement mechanism and where cultural heterogeneity is a concern.

- source: Abbink, K., & Sadrieh, A. (2009). The pleasure of being nasty. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of subjects are willing to destroy the other player's endowment when their actions are hidden, but not when they are observable. The presence of anonymity increases anti-social behavior. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods or punishment game. The findings are relevant for understanding anti-social punishment and spite, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The game studied is a two-player, anti-social destruction game with no cooperation or group payoff structure. The main insight is that anonymity increases anti-social behavior, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The findings may be contextually relevant for understanding anti-social punishment or spiteful behavior, but do not inform the prediction task in a quantitative or directly comparable way.

- source: Leppänen, I., & Hämäläinen, R. P. (2017). Emotions in a Repeated Cournot Duopoly Game: A Psychophysiological Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that emotional responses are closely tied to reciprocal adjustments in a repeated Cournot duopoly game. When a player observes that the other did not cooperate (i.e., the other increased production), the player is more likely to make an upward adjustment (interpreted as punishment), and the size of this adjustment is positively correlated with emotional arousal. Disgust is associated with advantageous inequity (own one-sided gain), and positive affect is associated with mutual gains. The study provides evidence that emotions play a role in motivating reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not directly measure or report the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The Cournot duopoly is adjacent to public goods games, and punishment is implemented as endogenous behavioral adjustment rather than as a discrete, explicit punishment option.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the emotional and motivational underpinnings of reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas with continuous strategy spaces. It suggests that endogenous punishment-like responses (upward adjustments after defection) are emotionally motivated and can be costly for both players, potentially reducing efficiency. However, because the study does not manipulate explicit punishment mechanisms or report efficiency outcomes as a function of punishment, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the psychological processes underlying endogenous punishment in adjacent game structures, but should not be used as direct quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2009). Avoiding the sharp tongue: Anticipated written messages promote fair economic exchange. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The opportunity for receivers to send ex post written messages (expressing disapproval or emotion) in a one-shot dictator game leads to a statistically significant reduction in the frequency of maximally selfish (10%) offers by dictators, compared to the standard dictator game. However, the frequency of fair (50/50) offers is not significantly increased. The effect of non-monetary sanctions (messages) is smaller than that of monetary punishment (rejection) in ultimatum games, which more strongly reduce selfish offers but at the cost of efficiency due to destroyed surplus. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, as the dictator game does not involve group production or a public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal, non-monetary sanctions (written messages) on selfishness in a simple allocation game, but not in a public goods or group production context. It shows that non-monetary sanctions can reduce selfish behavior, but less so than monetary punishment. However, because the game is a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated or multi-player public goods game), and efficiency is not measured, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that in environments where only non-monetary sanctions are possible, some reduction in selfishness may occur, but the effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be directly inferred from this study.

- source: Mill, W., & Morgan, J. (2022). The cost of a divided America: an experimental study into destructive behavior. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both Clinton and Trump voters are willing to destroy the payoff of another participant, but the probability of doing so is significantly higher when the other is an outgroup member (opposing voter). This effect is mainly driven by Clinton voters, who increase their probability of spiteful behavior by 34% when facing an outgroup member. Trump voters are generally more spiteful overall, but do not differentiate between ingroup and outgroup. Attitudinal measures (social distance, feeling thermometer) are strongly correlated with spiteful behavior. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on direct, antisocial destruction of another’s payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot, two-player allocation task with the option to destroy the other’s payoff at no benefit to self (pure spite), not a repeated public goods game with punishment. There is no group production, no opportunity for cooperation, and no efficiency or welfare outcome reported. The findings are relevant for understanding the prevalence and moderators of destructive behavior (spite) in polarized contexts, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The closest relevance is to the literature on antisocial punishment or joy-of-destruction, but the mapping to PGG-like efficiency outcomes is weak.

- source: Batsaikhan, M., & Putterman, L. (2019). An Honest Day's Pay: Cooperation among Entrepreneurs vs. Students, and Linkages to Real-World Business Success. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Entrepreneurs in Mongolia display higher and increasing cooperation in a repeated property rights dilemma game compared to students, as measured by more tokens allocated to production and collective protection and fewer to theft. The opportunity to vote for mandatory collective protection (akin to a tax) is more frequently chosen by entrepreneurs, but this does not substantially change their already high cooperation levels. The chat treatment (pre-scripted messages) is associated with higher collective protection among entrepreneurs. There is no formal punishment mechanism (costly sanctions) as in standard PGGs; theft can be used as retaliation, but this is not a direct analog. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but discusses how behavioral allocations relate to social optimum. More successful entrepreneurs in real life are more cooperative in the lab. The main outcomes are behavioral, not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. The experimental design lacks a formal costly punishment mechanism; instead, it features theft (which is a form of negative externality, not a sanction) and collective protection (akin to public good provision). While the paper discusses how cooperation (less theft, more production, more collective protection) would increase efficiency, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, nor does it manipulate punishment in the relevant sense. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, though it provides contextual evidence about cooperation, institutional choice, and behavioral trends in repeated social dilemmas.

- source: Andersson, O., & Wengström, E. (2012). Credible communication and cooperation: Experimental evidence from multi-stage Games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing only pre-play communication leads to higher cooperation rates in the first stage (prisoner's dilemma) than allowing both pre-play and intra-play communication. Sending or receiving messages that signal cooperative intentions has a strong positive effect on cooperation when only pre-play communication is possible, but this effect is significantly weakened when intra-play communication is allowed.
