The results support the theoretical prediction that renegotiation opportunities (via intra-play communication) undermine the credibility of punishment threats and thus reduce the effectiveness of communication in sustaining cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral (not payoff) effects of communication structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of communication structure (timing and frequency) on cooperation in multi-stage games with punishment/reward strategies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that communication structure can moderate the effectiveness of punishment/reward threats in sustaining cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency in similar environments. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma plus coordination game, and punishment is implemented via equilibrium threats rather than explicit costly punishment. The main actionable insight is that more communication opportunities (especially intra-play) can reduce the effectiveness of pre-play communication in sustaining cooperation, likely due to renegotiation.

- source: Hoffmann, L. (2022). Cooperation in the name of God? Experimental evidence from Ghana and Tanzania. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that religious reminders of punishment or reward decrease cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma among Christian and Muslim participants in Ghana and Tanzania. The punishment prime reduces cooperation by about 8 percentage points, and the reward prime by about 6 percentage points, relative to the control. There is no evidence that religious primes increase cooperation. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and beliefs/expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma (not a repeated PGG), and the 'punishment' and 'reward' treatments are religious primes, not institutionalized peer punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. Thus, the paper is only weakly informative for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that not all forms of punishment/reward framing increase cooperation, and that context and mechanism matter.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., Rincke, J., & Tuset-Cueva, A. (2019). Rent extraction and prosocial behavior. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when an administrator actively chooses to extract a 10% rent from contributions, average contributions to non-profit associations drop significantly (by 41%) compared to a control where the same share is assigned passively. This effect is robust to framing and is interpreted as a motivational effect: rent extraction undermines contributors' general willingness to give, rather than triggering targeted negative reciprocity. The effect persists even when contributors interact with a new, uninvolved administrator in a subsequent round, indicating a spillover on generosity. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the design is a donation game with some similarities to public goods games but is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that visible anti-social actions by an authority figure (rent extraction) can sharply reduce prosocial contributions in a donation context, even when the price of giving is held constant. However, because the outcome is contribution rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a donation game rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The result suggests that introducing a salient, self-serving authority or punishment-like mechanism that is perceived as unfair or anti-social may reduce cooperation and thus efficiency, but the direct quantitative effect on efficiency in a PGG is not measured here.

- source: Jones, M. T. (2014). Strategic complexity and cooperation: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Increasing the complexity of strategies required to sustain cooperation in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma reduces the frequency of cooperation and increases the prevalence of simple selfish strategies. This effect is observed both when the cooperate action and when the defect action are made state-dependent, increasing the information processing required for cooperation or punishment, respectively. The study also finds that higher cognitive ability is associated with greater cooperation in the baseline, but this advantage is reduced when complexity is increased. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures such as cooperation rates and strategy inference.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cognitive or strategic complexity of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas reduces cooperation rates, and by implication, would likely reduce efficiency or group payoff if measured. However, the study is not a public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on endogenous punishment via repeated game strategies (e.g., Grim Trigger). The findings are most relevant for predicting how complexity or cognitive burden might moderate the effect of punishment or cooperation-supporting mechanisms in repeated games, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games. Use this paper as informative indirect evidence about the role of complexity and cognitive ability in sustaining cooperation, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Kusakawa, T., Ogawa, K., & Shichijo, T. (2012). An experimental investigation of a third-person enforcement in a prisoner's dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that when a third party (M) can observe the actions of two players in a one-shot PD, the cooperation rate between those two players is significantly higher than when M cannot observe. This is attributed to the threat of future punishment by M in subsequent repeated PD games. The effect is measured in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or total payoff. The study provides evidence that third-party observation and the possibility of future sanctioning can facilitate cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a third-party observer with future sanctioning power can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or directly measure efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that third-party monitoring and the threat of future punishment can be a strong facilitator of cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly tested. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate standard punishment mechanisms (e.g., costly punishment within the group), so caution is warranted in transferring the findings.

- source: Wittman, D. (2005). The internal organization of the family: Economic analysis and psychological advice. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that advice from clinical psychology on setting boundaries and using natural or logical consequences (rather than punishment) in families is consistent with economic reasoning about property rights and efficiency. It argues that punishment systems in families create externalities and deadweight losses, and that structuring choices so that parents are indifferent to children's decisions (the 'indifference principle') minimizes manipulation and strategic behavior. The paper suggests that minimizing emotional involvement and not using punishment leads to more functional, efficient family relationships, but these claims are theoretical and not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its arguments are theoretical and analogical, suggesting that punishment may introduce inefficiencies (externalities, deadweight loss) in small-group settings, and that structuring choices to avoid punishment may be more efficient. However, these claims are not tested in controlled experiments or public goods game variants, so the paper offers only indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment can reduce efficiency by increasing transaction costs and strategic manipulation.

- source: Eisenbarth, S., Graham, L., & Rigterink, A. S. (2021). Can Reminders of Rules Induce Compliance? Experimental Evidence from a Common Pool Resource Setting. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The SMS reminder intervention increased self-reported knowledge of forest use rules and the perceived probability of sanctions for rule-breaking, but did not increase actual scrutiny or sanctioning of others. There was no significant effect on compliance with forest use rules or on forest use (measured both by self-report and satellite data). The intervention did not systematically reduce resource extraction or improve compliance, and thus did not provide evidence that reminders of rules (and increased perceived sanctions) improve efficiency or group welfare in this common pool resource context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common pool resource games, as it does not manipulate or directly measure actual punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The intervention only increased perceived probability of sanctions, not actual sanctioning or compliance, and did not reduce resource use. Thus, the findings suggest that increasing perceived sanctions via reminders alone may not be sufficient to improve efficiency or reduce overuse in common pool resource settings. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that behavioral nudges increasing perceived sanctions (without changing actual punishment mechanisms or costs) are unlikely to have strong effects on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Isoni, A., & Sugden, R. (2019). Reciprocity and the Paradox of Trust in psychological game theory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that models of reciprocal kindness, as formalized in psychological game theory (e.g., Rabin 1993, Charness & Rabin 2002, Falk & Fischbacher 2006), cannot explain mutually beneficial trust and trustworthiness in Trust Games—this is termed the 'Paradox of Trust.' The paper argues that trust and trustworthiness are better explained by a different form of reciprocity, namely reciprocal cooperation, where individuals are motivated to play their part in mutually beneficial practices conditional on others doing the same. The paper sketches how this approach can be applied to public goods games, but does not provide empirical results or efficiency data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative results that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is conceptually relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of cooperation and the limitations of certain reciprocity models. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that models focusing solely on rewarding/punishing intentions (reciprocal kindness) may not capture the mechanisms that lead to efficient cooperation in trust or public goods games. Instead, models of reciprocal cooperation may be more appropriate for predicting when mutually beneficial outcomes (i.e., high efficiency) can arise. The paper does not address specific game design dimensions or provide data on how punishment affects efficiency.

However, it does not provide new empirical or simulation data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions quantitatively affect efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that behavioral economics provides important insights for climate change policy, especially regarding the need for lower social discount rates and the role of social preferences in cooperation. It reviews evidence that cooperation in public goods games and real-world analogues is conditional and often requires the possibility of punishment or sanctions to be sustained over time. The authors argue that, in the context of climate negotiations, sanctioning mechanisms are likely essential for long-term effectiveness, as cooperation otherwise tends to unravel. However, the paper does not provide new empirical estimates of efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions.

However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency changes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The discussion is at the level of mechanisms and qualitative expectations, not empirical effect sizes. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the general expectation that punishment mechanisms are likely to increase cooperation and possibly efficiency, but does not allow for fine-grained, dimension-specific predictions.

  findings: The paper argues that evolutionary psychology provides a scientific basis for understanding preferences that include reciprocity and punishment, not just self-interest. It suggests that the human tendency to punish noncooperators, even at a cost, is an evolved trait that helps sustain cooperation in social dilemmas. The authors cite experimental evidence (not their own) that introducing punishment options in public goods games leads to higher and sustained cooperation, but the paper itself is conceptual and does not present new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.

For prediction tasks, this paper provides conceptual support for including punishment as a positive moderator of cooperation, but does not offer quantitative or design-specific guidance. - source: Johnson, D. D. P., Price, M. E., & Van Vugt, M. (2013). Darwin's invisible hand: Market competition, evolution and the firm. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper proposes that the standard economic view of market competition as a Darwinian process is incomplete, because it overlooks the group-level selection that occurs among firms. Using multi-level selection theory, the authors argue that both individual and group selection pressures shape behavior in firms, leading to a mix of self-interested and group-beneficial behaviors. The paper draws on existing empirical literature (including public goods games and studies of cooperation and punishment) to illustrate how evolved human tendencies for cooperation, fairness, and punishment can be harnessed or undermined in organizational contexts. However, it does not provide new empirical findings or direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or cooperation in public goods games or firm settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how evolutionary mechanisms (including punishment and cooperation) might affect efficiency and cooperation in group settings such as firms, referencing public goods game literature. However, it does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its value for prediction is mainly in highlighting the importance of both individual and group-level incentives, and the potential for punishment and reputational mechanisms to support cooperation, but it does not specify how game design dimensions quantitatively moderate these effects.

- source: Stoelhorst, J. W., & Richerson, P. J. (2013). A naturalistic theory of economic organization. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation on a large scale is an evolutionary anomaly, explained by a combination of multi-level selection and gene-culture co-evolution. It posits that mechanisms such as punishment, reward, and norm enforcement are universal features that help sustain cooperation, but the specific forms these take are historically and culturally contingent. The paper references empirical findings from public goods experiments and cross-cultural studies, noting that punishment (both altruistic and antisocial) is universal but varies in its effects on cooperation and group welfare. It also highlights that antisocial punishment can undermine group efficiency. However, the paper itself does not report new experimental results or quantify efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and norm enforcement can sustain or undermine cooperation in public-goods-like environments, and how these mechanisms are shaped by evolutionary and cultural factors. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universal, but depends on the balance between altruistic and antisocial punishment, which is in turn influenced by local norms and institutional strength. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that game design dimensions (such as the presence and type of punishment, group size, and information structure) interact with cultural context to determine efficiency outcomes, but does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Wilson, D. S., Ostrom, E., & Cox, M. E. (2013). Generalizing the core design principles for the efficacy of groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that Ostrom's core design principles, including monitoring and graduated sanctions (punishment), are critical for the efficacy of groups managing common-pool resources and can be generalized to other group contexts such as education and neighborhoods. It provides theoretical justification for why these principles suppress within-group conflict and promote cooperation, referencing evolutionary and game theory. The paper discusses case studies (drawn from other literature) where the implementation of these principles led to improved group functioning, but does not report new experimental or quantitative results on efficiency or payoffs. The main finding is that the presence of monitoring and sanctioning mechanisms is associated with more effective group outcomes, but this is discussed in terms of group efficacy and cooperation rather than explicit efficiency or payoff metrics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment (as part of graduated sanctions) and monitoring are important for group efficacy in social dilemma settings, including but not limited to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or parameterized results that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games as a function of game design dimensions. Its value for prediction is mainly in reinforcing the importance of including monitoring and sanctioning mechanisms in models of group cooperation, and in highlighting that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on their implementation and the broader institutional context. For direct prediction of efficiency outcomes, empirical studies with quantitative results are needed.

- source: Stringham, E. P. (2011). Embracing morals in economics: The role of internal moral constraints in a market economy. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.

  findings: The paper finds that internal moral constraints are a powerful and often underappreciated source of cooperation in economic and social life. It argues that external constraints (such as punishment and legal sanctions) are limited in their effectiveness and practicality, especially in complex or informal environments. The author reviews evidence from experimental economics, behavioral economics, and case studies to suggest that people frequently cooperate due to internalized norms, moral rules, or self-command, even when external punishment is absent or weak. The paper suggests that efforts to strengthen internal moral constraints (through education, socialization, or community formation) may be more effective in promoting cooperation than increasing external sanctions. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it analyze public goods games or punishment treatments directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is primarily a theoretical and conceptual argument about the relative importance of internal versus external constraints for cooperation. While it references experimental literature showing that cooperation can occur without punishment, it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment treatments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may provide indirect context for why punishment may not always be necessary for cooperation, but it does not offer quantitative or design-specific guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment.

- source: Kamei, K., & Putterman, L. (2018). REPUTATION TRANSMISSION WITHOUT BENEFIT TO THE REPORTER: A BEHAVIORAL UNDERPINNING OF MARKETS IN EXPERIMENTAL FOCUS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of cooperators are willing to incur a cost to report defectors, even when there is no direct material benefit. Reporting is much more common when it is free, but remains significant at positive costs. Reporting is overwhelmingly targeted at defectors by cooperators, with little reporting in other pairings. The frequency of cooperation is relatively stable across treatments (about 50-60%), and beliefs about others' cooperation and reporting are correlated with own behavior. The results are consistent with models of social preferences (inequity aversion) and with emotional motivations (anger, obligation). However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the motivational underpinnings of costly punishment-like behaviors (here, reporting) in social dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment, as it studies a two-player prisoners' dilemma with a costly reporting option that can affect a partner's reputation and future interactions. The findings suggest that costly punishment/reporting is more likely when costs are low and when social/emotional motivations are present, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from enabling punishment in a PGG. The results may inform priors about the likelihood of costly punishment being used, but not about its effect on group efficiency.

- source: Le Maux, B., & Necker, S. (2023). Honesty nudges: Effect varies with content but not with timing. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Honesty nudges, especially those that mention the possibility of monitoring (deterrence), significantly reduce cheating in a repeated individual reporting task. The effect of nudges is similar whether implemented before or after some decisions have been made. Adding individualized feedback does not further reduce cheating beyond the deterrence message. The reduction in dishonesty is larger among those who were previously dishonest. However, the study does not analyze group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the game is not interactive or public-goods-like.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: deterrence messages (even without real sanctions) can reduce dishonest behavior in individual settings, and the effect is robust to timing. However, because the design is not a public goods game, there is no group interaction, and no efficiency or group payoff is measured, the results should be used with caution for downstream prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

  findings: The study finds that influence-based preferential selection and the coevolution of game type and strategy can significantly increase cooperation rates and the average payoff of cooperators, especially in the Snowdrift game regime. Higher influence growth factor and higher 'sucker's payoff' (sigma) both lead to higher average payoffs for cooperators and greater resource accumulation. The introduction of a resource protection mechanism further increases cooperation and makes resource distribution more equitable. While explicit punishment is not modeled, the results suggest that mechanisms which promote cooperation (such as influence-based selection) also improve group-level payoffs and resource efficiency.

The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness of mechanisms that promote cooperation (e.g., higher influence growth, more favorable game parameters) will increase both cooperation rates and average payoffs, analogous to increased efficiency. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the direct transferability to punishment-enabled PGGs is limited. The results are most informative for adjacent mechanisms (e.g., reputation, influence, resource redistribution) rather than direct punishment.

- source: Kagel, J. H. (2018). Cooperation through communication: Teams and individuals in finitely repeated Prisoners' dilemma games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Allowing unrestricted communication between opponents in finitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games leads to a large increase in early-stage cooperation rates for both teams and individuals. With communication, stage-one cooperation rates rise from 62.2% to 92.9% for teams and from 57.4% to 97.9% for individuals. Communication content focuses on mutual benefit and fairness, with little explicit discussion of punishment. Teams are more likely than individuals to plan and execute defections near the end of the game, consistent with greater strategic reasoning. Retaliation for defection is observed but is not a formal punishment mechanism. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication (chat) between players in finitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games greatly increases cooperation rates, especially in early rounds, for both teams and individuals. However, it does not directly report or analyze efficiency or group payoff, and does not implement or manipulate punishment mechanisms. Thus, while the findings are highly informative about the effect of chat/communication on cooperation, they are not directly transferable to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The evidence is most useful for understanding the impact of chat and the behavioral context in which punishment might operate, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes with or without punishment.

- source: Borzino, N., Fatas, E., & Peterle, E. (2023). In transparency we trust an experimental study of reputation, transparency, and signaling. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that providing ex-post transparency about receiver actions (Transparency treatment) significantly increases trust (the amount sent by senders) compared to both Baseline and Reputation (ex-ante information) treatments. However, neither information treatment increases trustworthiness (the amount returned by receivers) on average. The effect of the non-binding recommendation is positive but declines with higher suggested values and over time. The study does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include a punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. Its findings are only weakly relevant for the prediction task, as it focuses on how information and recommendations affect trust and trustworthiness in a networked trust game. The design and outcomes are adjacent to public goods games, but not directly transferable for the purpose of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Sääksvuori, L., & Ramalingam, A. (2015). Bargaining under surveillance: Evidence from a three-person ultimatum game. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that third-party surveillance (the ability of an inactive third-party to observe pre-play chat between proposer and responder) does not affect the allocation of resources in a three-person ultimatum game. Whether the right to observe is exogenously granted or endogenously purchased, and whether the third-party can signal their presence, has no significant effect on the amount allocated to the third-party. Third-parties are willing to pay for the right to observe, and are over-optimistic about the effect of their observation, but their presence does not change outcomes. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The design is a one-shot three-person ultimatum game with pre-play chat and optional third-party monitoring. The main finding is that third-party surveillance alone (without punishment or sanctioning power) does not affect allocations or outcomes. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that mere monitoring or transparency, in the absence of sanctions, may not improve efficiency or resource allocation.

- source: Serdarevic, N., Stromland, E., & Tjotta, S. (2021). It pays to be nice: The benefits of cooperating in markets. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that enabling mutual partner choice in repeated prisoner's dilemma games leads to higher overall earnings (efficiency) compared to random matching. This effect is robust across three lab experiments with different group sizes (5 or 9) and information conditions (reminder of full vs. current history). Cooperators benefit most from partner choice, earning significantly more than free riders, who actually earn less under partner choice than under random matching. The effect of partner choice on efficiency is larger in groups of 9 than in groups of 5, but the difference is not statistically significant. Reminding subjects only of their current period's information (rather than full history) increases efficiency more. There is no punishment or explicit sanctioning; exclusion from play is endogenous to the partner choice process.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous partner choice (mutual consent matching) in repeated social dilemma games increases efficiency relative to random matching, especially for cooperative types. While not a public goods game per se, the repeated prisoner's dilemma with continuous contributions and endogenous exclusion is closely related. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment, mechanisms that allow for partner selection and exclusion can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The results also indicate that group size and information about partner history can moderate the efficiency gains from partner choice, though the group size effect is not statistically significant. For prediction tasks, the presence of partner choice (vs. random matching) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly for cooperators, even without punishment.

- source: DeAngelo, G., Ferrell, P., & McCannon, B. (2017). Sources of deviant behavior: Contrasting alternative explanations in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who experience deteriorating social interactions (declining trust) in a repeated Trust Game are more likely to extract more from a common resource in a subsequent game, reducing group efficiency. Similarly, individuals' expectations about the typical behavior of others (empirical social norms) strongly predict their own extraction behavior. The study does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes. Instead, it suggests that interventions targeting social history or social norms may be more effective than sanctions in reducing deviant behavior and improving group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the experimental design. However, it provides strong evidence that social history and social norms are important determinants of efficiency in common resource dilemmas. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering alternative interventions.

- source: Huber, J., Shubik, M., & Sunder, S. (2018). Financing of public goods through taxation in a general equilibrium economy: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enforceable taxation—especially when set by democratic vote—sustains high efficiency and public good provision, while voluntary anonymous contributions (VACs) lead to rapid decline in both. Endogenous (voted) tax rates result in higher efficiency and private good production than fixed exogenous tax rates. When subjects can choose, they almost always vote for taxation over VACs. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. The results suggest that, in this dynamic general equilibrium setting, enforceable taxes are a robust and efficient solution to the public goods provision problem, while VACs are unreliable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in dynamic public good economies with and without enforceable taxation, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. The findings show that, in the absence of punishment, enforceable taxes (especially when set endogenously) yield much higher efficiency than voluntary contributions. The design is adjacent to standard PGGs: it is a dynamic, general equilibrium model with investment, depreciation, and a government actor, rather than a simple linear PGG. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing enforceable taxation (not punishment) on efficiency, but do not inform about punishment effects. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper provides a strong control (no-punishment) baseline, but no evidence on the effect of adding punishment.

- source: Schories, F. E. (2022). The Influence of Indirect Democracy and Leadership Choice on Cooperation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the way an institution is implemented (democratically by an elected leader vs. exogenously or by a random dictator) has a substantial effect on cooperation and group efficiency. When the group leader is elected and chooses to implement a more cooperative payoff structure, both cooperation rates and average earnings are significantly higher than in otherwise identical conditions where the change is imposed exogenously or by a randomly assigned leader. The democracy premium is large and statistically significant in the representative democracy (elected leader) treatment, but not in the random dictator treatment. The effect is especially pronounced among subjects who initially did not prefer the institutional change. The paper provides direct evidence that procedural legitimacy (democratic election) increases efficiency in social dilemma settings, even when controlling for selection and information effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in social dilemma environments structurally similar to the prisoners' dilemma (and by extension, public goods games), the introduction of a more cooperative institution (e.g., a coordination game) by a democratically elected leader leads to higher efficiency (group payoff) than if the same institution is imposed exogenously or by a randomly assigned leader. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of institutional changes on efficiency, the method of implementation (democratic legitimacy) is a critical moderator. However, the results do not inform about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, as these are not present in the design. The evidence is most relevant for settings where institutional change is possible and the legitimacy of the process is a variable of interest.

- source: Fornwagner, H., & Hauser, O. P. (2022). Climate Action for (My) Children. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that parents invest, on average, over 80% of their endowment in VCA (planting trees), with the highest investment when observed by their own child. The effect of being observed by one's own child is significant among parents with a high school diploma and those with strong climate change beliefs. No significant effect is found for being observed by a stranger adult or child. The study does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanism. The outcome is a direct measure of efficiency/welfare (how much of the endowment is invested in the public good). The design is adjacent to a public goods game but lacks group interaction, punishment, or reward features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how observability and social context (especially intergenerational observability) affect voluntary contributions to a public good, but it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to a PGG (single-player, no group interaction, no punishment/reward). Thus, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Vorlaufer, T. (2019). Effects of double-anonymity on pro- and anti-social behavior: Experimental evidence from a lab in the field. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that generalized reciprocity (helping if helped) can evolve and persist in structured populations, even without active assortment or reputation mechanisms, provided that social interactions are limited to local neighborhoods. The expected payoff for reciprocators can exceed that of non-reciprocators when reciprocators are sufficiently clustered, leading to the dominance of cooperative strategies in the stationary distribution. The model is adjacent to public goods games but does not include group-level public goods, punishment, or sanctioning. Efficiency is analyzed in terms of expected payoffs for different strategies, and the results show that network structure and the independence of spontaneous helping and reciprocation are key moderators of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or group-level public goods. However, it provides theoretical evidence that local interaction structure and the independence of helping and reciprocation can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation and the resulting payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that network structure and the possibility of reciprocation (even without punishment) can be important moderators of efficiency, but the absence of punishment in the model limits its direct applicability to the target prediction task.

- source: Sonntag, A., & Zizzo, D. J. (2019). Personal accountability and cooperation in teams. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing group members the option to acquire information about the true source of others' output (endogenous accountability) leads to the highest average effort and group output, even though almost no one actually chooses to acquire the information. High accountability (where the source is always revealed) leads to the lowest effort and the fastest decline in cooperation over time. Low accountability (no source information) results in intermediate effort. The results suggest that the mere possibility of being observed (the 'Damocles effect') can sustain higher effort, even if observation is rare. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the study does not report efficiency as a ratio, but group output is a direct function of effort.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or team production. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of information/accountability structures on group output in a repeated team production context. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism, the findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with punishment enabled. However, the results suggest that making accountability optional (even if rarely used) can sustain higher group output than either always-on or always-off accountability. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant as a control or comparison for environments without punishment, but with varying information about individual contributions.

- source: Salazar, M., Shaw, D. J., Czekóová, K., Stanek, R., & Brázdil, M. (2022). The role of generalised reciprocity and reciprocal tendencies in the emergence of cooperative group norms*. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that, in a repeated anonymous bargaining game with no punishment or reputation, cooperative (equal) demands become more common over time in many groups, indicating the emergence of cooperative-like group norms via generalised reciprocity. However, there is substantial heterogeneity between groups, driven by the mix of individual reciprocal tendencies. Some groups converge to egalitarian outcomes, while others remain uncooperative. The presence of more 'Constant' or 'Conditional' cooperators in a group leads to more cooperative outcomes. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not directly analyze group efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible payoff, though it does report mean payoffs for different demand types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. However, it provides informative context about how group composition and reciprocal tendencies affect the emergence of cooperation in repeated anonymous interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that, even without punishment, some groups can achieve high cooperation depending on their composition. The findings may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Bohnet, I., & Frey, B. S. (1999). The sound of silence in prisoner's dilemma and dictator games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that reducing anonymity by allowing mutual identification (without communication) increases cooperation in the PD and generosity in the DG compared to fully anonymous conditions. Communication further increases cooperation in the PD but not in the DG. One-way identification (dictator knows recipient) also increases giving, especially when more information about the recipient is provided. The main mechanism is interpreted as reduced social distance and increased empathy, not reciprocity or fear of sanctions. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency directly, and all outcome measures are behavioral (choices), not payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that social context (identification, communication) can increase cooperation or generosity, but it does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency under different game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only very weak, indirect context about the role of social distance and communication, not about punishment or efficiency.

- source: Schitter, C., Fleiss, J., & Palan, S. (2019). To claim or not to claim: Anonymity, symmetric externalities and honesty. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that removing anonymity (making claims public to other group members) significantly reduces dishonest overclaiming, while adding symmetric externalities (where claims reduce the pool for others) does not significantly affect honesty. There is no evidence that the combination of both mechanisms has a stronger effect than removing anonymity alone. The presence of symmetric externalities does not increase honesty, possibly because fear of being exploited by others crowds out pro-social motives. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are robust to controls and alternative model specifications.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence that increasing transparency (removing anonymity) can reduce dishonest behavior in environments where individuals can make unverifiable claims that affect others. The presence of symmetric externalities alone does not increase honesty. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only weakly relevant, as it does not manipulate or measure those variables.

- source: Xiao, E., & Bicchieri, C. (2010). When equality trumps reciprocity. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when reciprocity and equality motives are in conflict (i.e., reciprocating increases inequality), most trustees choose not to reciprocate, whereas in the standard trust game (where reciprocating reduces inequality), most trustees do reciprocate. Investors expect this difference. The study provides evidence that equality concerns can override reciprocity motives in one-shot trust games. However, the paper does not involve punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that social motives (like equality aversion) can override other pro-social motives (like reciprocity) in some contexts, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Mitani, Y. (2022). Is a PD game still a dilemma for Japanese rural villagers? A field and laboratory comparison of the impact of social group membership on cooperation. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social group membership (in-community vs. out-community) has a strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma game, both in the field (with rural villagers) and in the lab (with students). Villagers achieve near-universal cooperation with in-community matching, and still high cooperation with out-community matching. Students cooperate much less, but the group identity effect is still present. The paper does not include any punishment or reward treatments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding the effect of social context and group identity on cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that social group identity can strongly increase cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, especially among rural villagers. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but not for predicting the effect of adding punishment.

- source: Song, F., & Zhong, C. B. (2015). You scratch his back, he scratches mine and I'll scratch yours: Deception in simultaneous cyclic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in simultaneous cyclic networks, participants are more likely to engage in deception (lying about the endowment size) on behalf of others than when acting for themselves. This effect is present both in small (dyadic) and larger (20-30 person) cyclic networks, and is driven by the anticipation of indirect reciprocity. Breaking the reciprocity loop reduces deception. The study uses a modified ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and does not involve punishment or sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Alberti, F., & Güth, W. (2013). Studying deception without deceiving participants: An experiment of deception experiments. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that strong incentives lead participants in the experimenter role to deceive others, but that this deception does not significantly affect the behavior of allocators or recipients in subsequent rounds, even after debriefing. There is no evidence that deception changes generosity or acceptance thresholds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game is a dictator game variant rather than a public goods game. The findings may be marginally informative for understanding the robustness of participant behavior to negative experiences (such as deception), but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions in PGGs.

- source: Gurven, M., Zanolini, A., & Schniter, E. (2008). Culture sometimes matters: Intra-cultural variation in pro-social behavior among Tsimane Amerindians. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds substantial and significant variation in dictator game offers across Tsimane villages, even after controlling for demographic, acculturation, and demand variables. Villagers can predict their own village's offer patterns, and these are partly aligned with local moral judgments about fairness. Publicly revealing offers (public DG) increases average offers by about 4% but does not reduce variation or eliminate village differences. The presence of loggers in a village is associated with lower offers. The study does not include any punishment, sanctioning, or reward mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about the baseline variation in pro-social behavior (as measured by DG offers) in the absence of punishment or strong social norms, and discusses the potential role of punishment in stabilizing behavior and reducing variation, referencing other literature. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is mainly useful as a negative control: it shows that in the absence of punishment, pro-social behavior can be highly variable and locally contingent, and that public exposure alone (without punishment) has only a modest effect on increasing offers.

- source: Cassar, A., Grosjean, P., Khan, F. J., & Lambert, M. (2023). Mothers, fathers, and others: Competition and cooperation in the aftermath of conflict. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that exposure to conflict increases egalitarianism (interpreted as a proxy for cooperation) among non-parents, especially non-parent males, but not among parents. Conflict exposure reduces competitiveness for most groups, except for mothers, who become more competitive after victimization, particularly when they are single, have many children, or have young children. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/reward mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on how conflict exposure affects behavioral proxies for cooperation and competition may be of limited indirect interest for understanding how adversity or group context shapes prosociality, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Castagnetti, A., Proto, E., & Sofianos, A. (2023). Anger impairs strategic behavior: A Beauty-Contest based analysis. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Inducing anger in participants leads to significantly lower payoffs and worse strategic performance in a 10-round, 3-player beauty contest game. Angry participants deviate further from the best response and are more likely to use non-strategic (level-0) reasoning. The effect is robust across group compositions and persists after controlling for prior play. Inducing sadness does not have a significant effect on payoffs or strategic reasoning. The results suggest that anger specifically, rather than negative emotions in general, impairs strategic thinking and reduces efficiency in strategic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is informative for the prediction task in that it demonstrates how incidental emotions (specifically anger) can reduce efficiency in strategic games by impairing cognitive reasoning. The findings suggest that if punishment mechanisms in public goods games induce anger, this could potentially reduce efficiency, not by changing social preferences, but by impairing strategic reasoning. However, since no punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game is studied directly, the transfer value is limited and indirect.

- source: Almeida, S. (2023). Punishment credibility and cooperation in public good games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Children gave significantly more candies to the other child in the Ultimatum Game (where unfair offers can be punished by rejection) than in the Dictator Game (where the recipient has no power), indicating that the threat of punishment increases prosocial allocations. Higher Honesty-Humility (a personality trait) was associated with more generous offers in both games. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a public goods game or a close variant. The findings are relevant for understanding how the possibility of punishment (retaliation) affects fairness-related behavior in children, but not for predicting efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that the threat of punishment (in the form of possible rejection in the Ultimatum Game) increases prosocial allocations compared to a no-punishment baseline (Dictator Game) in children. However, the design is not a public goods game, does not involve group payoff or efficiency, and does not report payoff-based outcomes. The evidence is relevant for understanding how the possibility of punishment can shift individual allocation behavior, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments.

- source: Bügelmayer, E., & Spiess, C. K. (2014). Spite and cognitive skills in preschoolers. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that preschool children with higher cognitive skills are more likely to display spiteful behavior in allocation tasks, especially boys. Spiteful behavior is operationalized as choosing to reduce another child's payoff, sometimes at a cost to oneself. The relationship is robust to controls for personality, empathy, and household characteristics. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any aggregate welfare outcome. The experimental design is not a public goods game or a repeated group interaction, but rather a set of one-shot allocation decisions between the subject and an anonymous other.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses spite as a motive related to punishment in the literature, the experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or group cooperation. The findings may be of indirect interest for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punitive or spiteful motives, but they do not inform the quantitative relationship between punishment and efficiency in PGG-like games. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Zultan, R. (2012). Strategic and social pre-play communication in the ultimatum game. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that pre-play communication (both unrestricted and restricted) increases proposer offers in the ultimatum game compared to no communication. However, only unrestricted (strategic) communication leads responders to demand higher minimum acceptable offers. The mechanism underlying communication effects differs by communication protocol: strategic communication enables coordination on social norms (e.g., equal split), while social (non-strategic) communication increases generosity via social preferences. The study does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve public goods, punishment, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are specific to the ultimatum game and the effects of communication protocols on offers and acceptance thresholds. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Durrett, R., & Levin, S. A. (2005). Can stable social groups be maintained by homophilous imitation alone?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper demonstrates that stable social groups and polarization can emerge in agent-based models where individuals imitate others who are similar to themselves (homophily), even in the absence of any explicit payoffs, rewards, or punishments. The stability of group structures depends on the rate of introspective (idiosyncratic) changes and the strength of homophilous imitation. The models are not payoff-based and do not include any public goods, cooperation, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not model or analyze any payoff, efficiency, or sanctioning mechanism, and thus provides no direct or indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes. Its findings are not transferable to the prediction task as defined.
